## F: finickity–flying survey

### finickity
 
 <i>adj.</i> fussy

>  <b>McGILL</b> finickity fussy; combining finicky and pernickety; eg `That kid's so finickity about her food, I'm surprised she doesn't look half starved.' ANZ. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,45</i>)

### finish
 
 <i>n.</i> used of animals considered to have an adequate fat cover for a particular market or carcass grade. [(freezing works)]

>  Finish. A term usually applied to animals considered to have an adequate fat cover for a particular market or carcass grade. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,34</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> ?The double indent around the outer edge of a ploughed field; ?the higher mounded and furrowed part of a paddock where the ploughing has `finished'.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Even when running out in a grass paddock she [the heading dog] would take advantage of the depression of an old `finish', running with her belly almost touching the ground. In a freshly ploughed paddock the finish was deep enough for her to keep almost completely out of sight. <i>(1971,Ten Thousand Dogs,18</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> the polish or extra class in a good dog's work.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Finish: the polish or extra class in a good dog's work. <i>(1971,Ten Thousand Dogs,168</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a quality that distinguishes an excellent working dog from others.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Glossary Finish; the polish or extra class in a good dog's work. <i>(1971,Ten Thousand Dogs,168</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) [stock] to present in first-class condition for sale.

>  <b>GASCOIGNE</b> Just to allow all the wethers to run on, so many to a paddock or with the gates opened, while it will allow a percentage to finish, certainly will not permit of the whole flock being disposed of satisfactorily. <i>(1965,From the Top of a Hill,122</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> The Kapuka place, managed by Russell Matthews is just as important for finishing the station's cattle. <i>(1987,People, Places & Paddocks,117</i>)

>  It's now taking farmers 18 months to finish cattle instead of 30 months. <i>(Aug 8, 1996,NZ Farmer,13</i>)

>  Because of the balance of hills and flats we can add value to our sheep by finishing them on the flat country ... <i>(Aug 5, 1999,Taranaki Daily News,13</i>)

>  We have a number of properties throughout the province from store hill to easy downs for finishing stock. <i>(Dec 2, 2000,Press, The,59</i>)

>  All lambs finished on station. <i>(May 19, 2001,Dominion,33</i>)

>  All surplus lambs and cattle are finished on farm. <i>(Nov 30, 2002,Dominion,C21</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a presentation quality that distinguishes farm animals (formerly often broadly described as ''fat'' and more recently as ''prime'').

>  There was an evident 'bloom' or finish on rape-fed lambs which was lacking among those on lucerne. <i>(Dec 20, 1928,NZ Journal of Agriculture,387</i>)

>  <b>GORDON72</b> The beef herd of 1300 cows has been loosely fixed at two-thirds Hereford and one-third Shorthorn, the latter for their 'extra finish and milking ability.' <i>(1987,People, Places & Paddocks</i>)

### finish off
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to fatten or complete the condition of an animal [for drafting, slaughter or sale.]

>  <b>MILLS ET AL</b> Run cattle, which apart from providing additional income, play an important role by regulating and controlling pastures in the best possible condition for sheep; and fattening cattle, usually grazed and finished off on heavier country. <i>(1964,Practical Guide to Handling Dogs and Stock,103</i>)

>  .. if the lambs are to be got away fat 'off the mother' they should have abundant milk during the first three months and first class young growth or supplentary food to finish them off. <i>(Sep 1, 1937,Weekly News,81</i>)

>  Stores are brought down off the hill runs to be finished off on the luscious paspalum-white clover pastures on the flats. <i>(Jan 15, 1945,NZ Journal of Agriculture,15</i>)

### finished
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of sale stock] prime or well-conditioned.

>  <b>GASCOIGNE</b> Up above on the rails an auctioneer's voice rattled machine-like at the crowd. They were mostly good, well-finished cattle. <i>(1965,From the Top of a Hill,78</i>)

>  <b>GASCOIGNE</b> Once a fortnight, right through good growing weather, it was found practicable to bring the fattening mobs through the yards and draft off the finished wethers for the next day's sale. <i>(1965,From the Top of a Hill,122</i>)

>  It's pretty hard to get cattle finished through winter. The feed just doesn't have the guts to it somehow. <i>(Apr 2001,Growing Today,62</i>)

### finisher
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a farmer who deals with fat stock or farms prime stock for sale.

>  He says he would want $225 for a 100kg calf from finishers, but knows he will not get it. The finger is being pointed at the calf finisher ... <i>(Jul 6, 1995,NZ Farmer,11</i>)

>  Competition was good from a relatively small bench which included some of the North Island's biggest finishers of cattle. <i>(Sep 18, 1997,NZ Farmer,46</i>)

>  Opportunity for fat-lamb finisher to share-farm up to 1200 ewe hoggets for 12 mths. <i>(Dec 2, 2000,Press, The,37</i>)

>  As finishers, we have to supply what we are committed to supply. <i>(Apr 12, 2001,NZ Farmer,30</i>)

>  Like many bull finishers, Waldin is still unsure of the price he will have to pay for 100g weaner bulls this spring. <i>(Jul 2001,Country-Wide,01</i>)

>  Lamb finishers wanted. <i>(Apr 20, 2002,Hawke's Bay Today,27</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a farmer who takes stock and "finishes" their preparation

>  The finishers do very well out of it - they are paid to graze the lambs, they can breed from them and they can keep their wool... SheepLink has been embraced by about 20 finishers, evenly divided between the North and South Islands, and will have 30,000 two-tooths for sale in January. <i>(Jun 10, 2004,Dominion Post,C7</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a crop or stock-feed used to fatten stock for sale or slaughter.

>  Indications, however, are that as a finisher for forward stores, lucerne is slower than good rape. <i>(Dec 20, 1928,NZ Journal of Agriculture,387</i>)

### finishing
 
 <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of stock] being fattened for slaughter.

>  As well as being a finishing farm for 40,000 lambs, it has room for 900 finishing deer, a pine plantation and a 720-cow milking platform. <i>(Aug 30, 2002,Dominion,C5</i>)

>  Central Hawke's Bay farmers fearing a drought similar to the 1982-83 dry spell are selling finishing stock early. But it's not just finishing cattle farmers are ditching early. <i>(Sep 18, 1997,NZ Farmer,5</i>)

>  The results can be seen in his herd of 1200 finishing bulls divided into 50 mobs rotated on two-hectare cell blocks. <i>(Oct 26, 2001,Dominion,17</i>)

>  The property winters approximately 13,500 sheep and 1,700 cattle, which includes breeding ewes and cows, finishing lambs and bulls, totalling approx 21,000 su. <i>(Aug 10, 2002,Dominion,B10</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of property] capable of fattening or priming stock.

>  .. the cattle are weighed regularly and put onto the finishing flats. They go to the forest in the spring and the big ones can be held there through the winter because the farm's finishing country is limited. <i>(May 19, 1993,NZ Farmer,21</i>)

>  This is an outstanding sheep and cattle breeding, and finishing station ... <i>(Oct 19, 2002,Dominion,C19</i>)

>  542 acres approx. predominantly strong cropping and finishing flats. <i>(May 12, 1993,NZ Farmer,33</i>)

>  For sale by public tender, finishing land - Motukarara. Strong summer finishing country for lambs and steers/replacements .. A beautifully presented breeding and finishing property. <i>(Dec 2, 2000,Press, The,59</i>)

>  With the dairy farm on the best land, we don't have enough finishing country to carry them through. <i>(Apr 26, 2001,Taranaki Daily News,15</i>)

>  Fabulous warm finishing country with many ideal building sites. <i>(May 25, 2001,Hawke's Bay Today,21</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the priming or fattening of stock for sale or slaughter.

>  Applicants should have experience in lamb finishing and be capable of achieving high performance from quality stock. <i>(Dec 8, 2001,Hawke's Bay Today,29</i>)

>  Fenced into approx. 40 paddocks, with new 10 cell paddocks for intensive cattle finishing. <i>(2001,Manawatu Property Press,25</i>)

>  The successful applicant will be involved in the running of a 15,000 stock unit complex within the property focusing on lamb and cattle finishing. <i>(Nov 30, 2002,Dominion,C21</i>)

### finishing block
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a land area where stock are primed or fattened for sale or slaughter.

>  The property is ideally suited as a finishing block or dairy run off. <i>(Apr 6, 2002,Hawke's Bay Today,26</i>)

>  .. the farm was ideal for conversion to dairy .. but would just as easily suit hill country farmers needing a finishing block for weaner cattle. <i>(Sep 12, 2002,Wanganui Chronicle,21</i>)

>  The 40 ha specialist lamb finishing block will this season put through some 9600 home-bred lambs .. On the specialist lamb finishing block, this should nudge 16,000 kg DM/ha, with rotational grazing ensuring high utilisation of feed grown. <i>(2002,Wool Grower,Spring 19</i>)

### finishing farm
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a farm where stock are fattened or primed for slaughter.

>  Ideal entry level finishing farm suited to calf rearing and trading. <i>(May 31, 2002,Dominion,17</i>)

>  As well as being a finishing farm for 40,000 lambs, it has room for 900 finishing deer ... <i>(Aug 30, 2002,Dominion,C5</i>)

>  Stock manager for 505ha breeding and finishing farm, close to Rotorua. <i>(Nov 30, 2002,Dominion,C21</i>)

### finishing farmer
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a farmer who buys in or breeds animals to fatten for slaughter or export.

>  Mr Polson, a sheep and beef finishing farmer from hill country near Wanganui, is in his second year of presidency of Federated Farmers. <i>(Aug 24, 2000,Taranaki Daily News,15</i>)

### finishing paddock
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a fenced area of lush growth where stock for sale are placed for a short time to fatten or prime.

>  <b>GASCOIGNE</b> Run in this manner the sheep fatten quickly and it is surprising how fast the whole mob can be put through, provided a 'second cut' is given as good treatment as possible before it reaches the finishing paddocks. <i>(1965,From the Top of a Hill,122</i>)

### finishing unit
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a farm property that fattens prime stock for sale or slaughter.

>  The property is run in conjunction with a 3500 acre breeding unit at Rangiwahia and serves as the finishing unit for the store stock produced on the Rangiwahia farm. <i>(Aug 4, 2001,Dominion,33</i>)

>  This unit has received an intense annual top dressing programme since 1991 and is currently farmed as a finishing unit in conjunction with another property. <i>(Mar 22, 2002,Hawke's Bay Today,3</i>)

>  *A farmable lifestyle block, or a fertile extensive finishing unit. <i>(Jun 27, 2005,NZ Farmers' Weekly,29</i>)

### finishing-off property
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a farm where stock is taken from the high country and conditioned or fattened for sale.

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> Four of the workforce were farm managers: they each looked after separate finishing-off properties (none larger than 517 hectares) quite apart from the main station. <i>(1997,Station Country III,13</i>)

### finless flounder
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>PAUL</b> There are two deepwater flatfishes in our waters. The spotted flounder, <i>Azygopus pinnifasciatus</i> is widespread in 300-800 m. ... The finless flounder, <i>Mancopsetta milfordi</i>, is a left-eyed flounder of the Campbell Plateau ... <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,145</i>)

>  <b>AYLING</b> FINLESS FLOUNDER <i>Mancopsetta milfordi</i> ... [307] Positive identification of this species is easy as there are no pectoral fins and the pale white-brown body is scaleless. <i>(1982,Collins Guide,306,1984 ed.</i>)

### finner
 
 <i>n.</i> [see fin whale] (See [fin whale](../F#fin-whale))

>  From them the chief peculiarities of the Finner, or Finback, of Razor-back, or Pike-whale, or Rorqual, as this species is variously termed in different parts of the world, can be seen. <i>(Aug 23, 1900,Otago Witness,54</i>)

### finning
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>GRADY</b> Finning - Action of a whale when lying partly on its side, raising one fin out of the water and striking it upon the surface, causing a splash. <i>(1982,Perano Whalers,227</i>)

### fiord
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>POTTS</b> ... or amongst the fiords and inlets or sounds of the south-west coast ... <i>(1882,Out in the Open,61</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The black weka is undoubtedly becoming more plentiful in the Fiord County Forest. <i>(1919,TrNZI,51/222</i>)

### Fiord country
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  PROSPECTING THE FIORD COUNTRY. By Wm. Wyllie ...They then had to boat the provisions to the head of Edwardson Sound, and then swag it up the creek. <i>(Apr 4, 1903,Otago Daily Times</i>) [NOTES: (VUW Fildes Clippings 621/46)]

### Fiord County
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  BLACK WEKA (<i>Gallirallus brachypterus</i> Lafresnaye). It is pleasant to be able to record that the black weka is undoubtedly becoming more plentiful in the Fiord County forest. <i>(1919,TrNZI,51/222</i>)

### Fiordland
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The less important [is] ... near the celebrated Milford Sound, in Fiordland. This locality is remarkable owing to the presence with the platinum of that peculiar nickel-iron mineral awaruite. In appearance it is not unlike platinum, and was mistaken by the miners for that mineral. ... Both the metal and the awaruite occur in a district composed ... of serpentine and dunite. <i>(1911,TrNZI,43/451</i>)

### Fiordland crested penguin
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment)

>  His "boring" job in Southland sees him in charge of around 20 island sanctuaries, containing some of the country's unique birds, including mohua, stitchbirds, saddlebacks and the Fiordland crested penguin. <i>(Mar 2002,Thin Green Line, The,9</i>)

>  <i>Fiordland Crested Penguin Eudyptes pachyrhynchus</i> GRAY, 1845 ... OTHER NAMES:<i>Tawaki, thick-billed penguin, crested penguin, Fiordland penguin. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,50</i>)

>  As we left the ship in boats the braying noise of the crested penguins became incessant. <i>(Jan 30, 1890,Otago Witness,15</i>)

>  The Snares, two small islands and a scatter of rocks 150km south of Stewart island, were spared the usual rats and cats, their tussock meadows and forested gullies, burrowing muttonbirds and indigenous crested penguin remain pristine. <i>(Mar 18, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),27</i>)

>  Two other species of penguin - Fiordland crested and little blue - also nest on Whenua Hou. <i>(Sep 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 59/60</i>)

>  On our port bow, a Fiordland crested penguin, the rarest in the world, has squirmed his way through the water <i>(Jun 2003,AA Directions,No. 48/52</i>)

>  As we pass the Shelter Islands we catch a glimpse of a bottlenose dolphin and a Fiordland crested penguin with a distinctive yellow stripe over each eye. <i>(Dec 17, 2010,Waikato Times,14</i>)

### Fiordland moose
 
 <i>n.</i> one of a population of Canadian moose introduced into Fiordland for hunting

>  A group of West Coast businessmen have offered a prize to anyone who can prove the South Island moa and Fiordland moose still exist. <i>(Jan 2000,North & South,104</i>)

### Fiordland skink
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) a skink remarkable for its habitat on wave-washed rocks in the south-western coast of Fiordland; a sun basker but capable of surviving in turbulent water for some time. [<i>Oligosoma acrinasum</i>]

>  We learn .. that Fiordland has a type of skink which jumps into rock pools when disturbed... <i>(2004,NZ Geographic,No. 69/17</i>)

>  Unlike most skinks, Fiordland skinks bask communally - 30 or more animals may pile on top of one another in a slinky pyramid. <i>(Jul 2000,NZ Geographic,No. 47/64</i>)

### Fiordland wapiti
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  It was gratifying to note that the writer recognised the recreational value of the Fiordland wapiti... The legislation is largely responsible for the fact that the wapiti herd is only a shadow of its former attraction, a Source of bitterness and anger with hunters. <i>(Feb 28, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),6</i>)

### Fiordlander
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  I find that the drowned trees had probably come from what the Fiordlanders call a tree avalanche. Because Fiordland was so thoroughly gouged by glaciers, there is virtually no topsoil on the steeper valley sides. Even the fully-grown forest has only the precarious hold of a woven skin of roots that stretch over rock. This net has a weak grip and if one section tears free it can peel the forest from the cliff-faces in great strips. The fall of just one tree can trigger a tree avalanche. <i>(Jan 1992,NZ Geographic,92</i>)

### fir
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BARKER</b> [We] ascend ... to a large terrace-like meadow, sheltered from the north-west winds by a thick belt of firs, blue gums, and poplars ... <i>(1866,Station Life in NZ,52,1870 ed.</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SAVAGE</b> The timber of which we have the most knowledge at the present is the fir, which grows here to an amazing height, and of such dimensions, as to admit being formed into a canoe capable of containing thirty persons, or in other words, five and six feet diameter. [J.C. Andersen's note to Savage above] Danthonia pauciflora in CIG, whence the name. The timber of which we have the most knowledge at present is the fir, which grows here to an amazing height. and of such dimen- sions as to admit of being formed into a canoe capable of con- taining thirty persons, or in other words five and six feet diameter.' SAN, p. 8. Savage was only at Bay of Islands, and saw no forest. <i>(1807,New Zealand (various authors/titles),8</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> Once used in many places in NZ, popularly and indiscriminately, for <i>Pinus</i> sp, esp. <i>P. radiata</i> (formerly <i>insignis</i>; by the 1960s an old-fashioned solecism, since pine trees and timber have become common, and commonly known. [[Savage NZ 107 p62] See also Kahikatea, for `white fir'. FIR(-TREE)]

>  [Otago-Canterbury East coast] ... treeless, except where the bush remains in kloofs or gullies, or where a patch of fir and gum marks the homestead. <i>(1895,NZ Reader,228</i>)

>  <b>CRESSWELL</b> ... the white Alps in a great semi-circle around, the clump of fir-trees at the corner ... cf 1933 May <i>op cit </i>80 On one side [the drive] is bordered by a thin belt of tall pine-trees.] <i>(Jun 3, 1932,Letters,66</i>)

>  <b>CRESSWELL</b> There was a large fir-tree near the path to the cricket-field, whose lowest boughs came so near to the ground they hid all beneath them; ... <i>(1939,Present Without Leave,20</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> [he uses pine-tree later] ... and round the next bend/ The fir trees with their pollen-bearing candles,/ .../ Wooden motels and pine tree islands .../ [at Queenstown]. <i>(1979,Collected Poems,366,(1966) ed.</i>)

>  <b>BENNETT</b> We had our games [c1907]. A favourite was `Monkey', where the contestant climbed one of the fir trees along one side of the ground (still there at the present day) and did a grand traverse of as many trees as possible. It was characterized by noise, boasting, cheating and an occasional sprained wrist. There was cockfighting in which each of two players, mounted pick-a-back, tried to resist dislodgement. <i>(1980,Canterbury Tale, A,34</i>)



 
<b>4</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Agatha australis. </i>

>  (And) <i>(1846,NZ Journal</i>)



 
<b>5</b> <i>n.</i> red pine early reciprocation Cf red pine, red fir.

>  <b>HEAPHY</b> The red pines are the <i>Totara, Towa, Mahi</i>, and <i>Rata</i> ... All these red firs make good spars for ships, and are frequently used by vessels visiting the settlements. [cf also p.6 Kauri fir q.v.] <i>Ibid.</i> 43 The woods most used in ship-building are the red firs, and especially the Totara and Rata. <i>(1842,New Zealand (Heaphy),42</i>)



 
<b>6</b> <i>n.</i> white pine cf fir

>  <b>YATE</b> The cocoa-nut tree, which abounds in the Friendly Islands, appears, in the distance, much like the mangroves, and the kahikatea, or white fir, of New Zealand; ... <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),9</i>)



 
<b>7</b> <i>n.</i> popular name for an exotic pine species, usu. <i>radiata</i>. See fir(-tree).

>  <b>SCANLAN</b> At the clump of firs, the car swung right down a side road. <i>(1944,March Moon,5</i>)

>  <b>FRAME</b> The Beach. Why wasn't everyone going to the Beach? ... they set off down the fir-bordered road that led to the sound of the sea ... <i>(1984,Some Other Country,102</i>) [NOTES: 1951 Frame `Swans' in 1984 Some Other Country 102]

>  The day had been very warm, with a nor-wester threshing at the tops of the firs and bluegums behind the Blakiston homestead ... <i>(1962,Landfall Country,162</i>) [NOTES: (Courage `An Evening for a Fish')]

### fire
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> Many men were lost [in the mine] ...some by striking gelly with their picks after the explosive had malfunctioned in the main `fire'. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,5</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> get going

>  They are frustratingly inconsistent yet can beat any side on their day, especially if [opening] batsmen.. can fire, freeing up the talented middle order. <i>(Feb 2, 2003,Sunday Star Times,B14</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [car salesmen's]

>  Car dealers have a collection of jargon they use on the yard, so if you are thinking of taking up selling cars as a career, there are a few terms you should know. ... Nowadays most cars have `fires,' or heaters, but not all of them have `public trust,' or rust. <i>(Jan 22, 1986,NZ Herald,Sect. 2/1</i>)

### fire and flute
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <b>McGILL</b> fire and flute car heater and radio, from the days when they were not standard in our cars. <i>(1989,Dinkum Kiwi Dictionary, The,31</i>)

### Fire Annihilator
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  It was intended to supply future vessels sailing for this colony with Phillips's Fire Annihilator, which, by evolving a certain gas, extinguishes fire almost instantaneously. <i>(Jun 14, 1851,Lyttelton Times,5</i>)

### fire box
 
 <i>n.</i> a woodbox

>  <b>KNOX</b> I took the last piece of wood from the fire box and put it into the flames ... <i>(1987,After Z-Hour,151</i>)

### fire brick
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>POWELL</b> FIRE BRICK (<i>Asterodiscus truncatus</i>). A large and brilliant coloured species taken occasionally by trawlers, operating in the vicinity of the Hen and Chicken Islands and in the Bay of Plenty. It is more plentiful, however, in deep water off the coast of New South Wales, where the local fishermen call it the fire-brick on account of its flaming colours. <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,10</i>)

### fire in the fern
 
 <i>phr.</i> (historical) used esp. of the Hauhau campaigns

>  <b>REEVES</b> Spreading from point to point, dying down and then starting up, it was as hard to put out as a fire abroad in the fern. <i>(1898,Long White Cloud, The,296,(1950) 218 ed.</i>)

>  That plume of smoke you see rising above Te Tai Hauauru speaks of flames among the fern roots. <i>(Jul 16, 2004,Dominion Post,B4</i>)

>  <b>DOMETT</b> Far as the Reinga's self erelong - down to those very dead,/ Like flames in fern when winds are strong, his widening glory spread;/ [fn] Reinga - place of departed Spirits. <i>(1883,Ranolf and Amohia,1/250</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> [check] But when the white fire of a fanatic religion [Pai Marire] fused the people in a federation of hate against the Pakeha, all problems merged into one - that of race mastery ... it spread like a fire in dry fern; ... <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,2/3</i>)

>  <b>BEAGLEHOLE</b> ... a `fire in the fern', to use a vivid Maori metaphor. <i>(1936,New Zealand: A Short History,37</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> Fire in the fern for trouble or smouldering discontent. The expression to spread like fire in the fern, used of a rumour and even of an epidemic, is useful as well as alliterative. Dealing with the Maori Wars in the Sydney Telegraph as recently as February, 1940, a writer says: `The fire in the fern has now gone out. Justice is being done to the Maori', which reveals that in Australia the phrase is linked with New Zealand. <i>(1941,NZ Slang,44</i>)

>  <b>SOLJAK</b> New Zealanders have coined or adapted many expressions to meet local requirements, as illustrated by the following: ... fire in the fern: smouldering resentment; generally applied to the Maori wars and their aftermath <i>(1946,New Zealand (various authors/titles),117</i>)

>  [A radio talk on Egypt] Like `the fire in the fern' of the old Maoris, the trouble seems to smoulder and flare up from time to time. <i>(1950,Radio NZ / National Radio</i>) [NOTES: 1950s]

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> As the Maori wars, `the fire in the fern', died down, life in the colony was at a very low ebb. <i>(1959,History of New Zealand,150</i>)

>  fire-in-the-fern n. Maori wars, put down in one place, only to break out in another; any smouldering discontent, particularly industrial. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),8</i>)

>  The Maori had a phrase to describe the kind of guerilla war they practised so successfully against the British and colonist armies here last century, talking of "a fire in the fern". <i>(Jun 24, 1992,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  When the "fire in the fern" died in the King Country, the Armed Constabulary was quartered in Hamilton. <i>(Feb 11, 1937,King Country Chronicle,2</i>)

### fire lighter
 
 <i>n.</i> a figurative spark

>  Bastion Point became the fire lighter for the first substantial Treaty examination, first by Aucklanders and then by the rest of the nation. <i>(Apr 2004,Wel-com (Wellington),5</i>)

### fire lookout
 
 <i>n.</i> a watchtower, usually in a forested area, in which a person is posted to watch for and report fires.

### fire out
 
 <i>v.</i> get rid of, throw out [Jim Cameron writes : Known to me at least since 1960s eg. "do you want those old magazines? No, fire them out". (Me also - DEH). NSOD gives <i>fire out</i> as a US variant of fire=discharge, dismiss]

### fire track
 
 <i>n.</i> a track through bush or scrub to provide access for firefighting. [Jim Cameron writes : Collins gives <i>fire trail (Austral).]

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> The men would chase Gormie and they would all have to run for their lives down the fire-tracks. <i>(1950,Our Street,54</i>) [NOTES: check cite]

### fire, fire
 
 <i>exclam.</i>

>  <b>SEDDON</b> The cry of warning `Fire, fire' before a heap of stone and tree trunk was blasted by dynamite even droned its way to the classrooms. <i>(1968,Seddons, The,24</i>)

### fire-escape
 
 <i>n.</i> a parson, minister.

>  An absent-minded old boy blew into a gospel foundry the other day and when the `fire-escape' in charge said, `Let us prey,'[sic] he gave as his response, `On the people.' ... A Porridgebyterian fire-escape called Millar has protested against Sunday concerts at Greymouth. [also 2nd quote for Porridgebyterian - see nonce-forms collection] <i>(Sep 4, 1909,NZ Truth,219/1/4</i>)

>  ... a wowser-bird of the harp-and-halo variety (i.e., fire-escape, devil-dodger, hell-buster, - really a Bookie disguised as a Curate) happened along and did a buttinski by introducing the bunch of cuddle in the Allotment Coat to a not overcrowded straight and narrow path. <i>(Sep 2, 1918,Quick March,1</i>)

### fire-fly
 
 <i>n.</i> A tent-fly used to keep the campfire and its environs dry in wet weather. A special tent-fly used esp. to keep the campfire, and its surrounds, dry in wet weather. Cf fly.

>  <b>PRINCE</b> ... I was suddenly startled by the fire-fly which I had hung over the door space, being suddenly pulled to one side ... <i>(1889,Diary of a Trip,17</i>)

>  <b>BROOKES</b> [I, a pioneer surveyor in Taranaki, pitch] a large tent opposite my own, then throw over a large-sized sheet, called a fire-fly, so that the space between the two tents was by this contrivance kept dry in wet weather. <i>(1891,Frontier Life,111</i>)

>  <b>WILMOT</b> We put up one of the tent flies for a firefly, the proper firefly being left at main camp for cook's use when baking tomorrow. <i>(1897,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1897 Wilmot's Journal in 1952 Reed and Reed Farthest West 140]

>  Our fire-fly was made of oiled calico, and proved of great service ... as a nor'easter set in ... <i>(Dec 1904,NZ Illustrated Magazine,165</i>)

>  <b>DOBSON</b> When the tent was pitched and filled with brushwood, and a fire-fly at the end of the tent, and a flax or brushwood breakwind round the windy side, a snug camp was made in a very short time. <i>(1930,Reminiscences,91</i>)

>  <b>ORBELL</b> Fire Fly ... you will be wise, very wise, to take another fly or large piece of canvas to cover that special area of ground in front of your fire place ... <i>(1952,Comfort & Commonsense in the Bush,6</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> Under the lee of a rock outcrop, sheltered on two sides by clumps of mountain flax, we set up house; a ten by [200] ten feet tent under a twelve by twelve feet fly with a ten by ten feet fly extended from the tent as a firefly, to give some shelter to the chef at his cooking pots. <i>(1953,Golden Bush, The,199,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>Jennings</b> The baggage consisted of.. 6 tents, 6 flies, 2 Fireflies, 1 cwt. flour <i>(1880,Diary of an Expedition to the Table Land,1</i>)

### fire-number
 
 <i>n.</i> a number (attached to a farm gate etc.) serving as property identification in rural areas, primarily for fire protection purposes. [Jim Cameron writes : Occasionally used in advertisements for open homes etc.]

>  He said the problem arose because Mr Mckay's fire number was not on his map. <i>(Aug 15, 2000,Wairarapa Times-Age,00</i>)

>  Open Home Sunday, 23rd, 1.30-2.30pm, Fire No 9, Loopline. <i>(Jan 22, 2000,Wairarapa Times-Age,00</i>)

### fire-stick
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MARSDEN</b> Before we left this village for the next I took a fire-stick into a shed, where our boxes stood with our provisions, and laid a little brimstone upon it to see what effect the fire would have. <i>(1819,Letters & Journals,211,1932 ed.</i>)

>  <b>McLEAN</b> We made another search with lighted fire-sticks. <i>(Oct 27, 1845,Journal,1844 ed.</i>)

>  f. Halse [firestick here and previously =a torch (flaming)] OED f. C14 =burning brand He appeared with a fire-stick which he brandished about, apparently in search of something, and then lighted the remains of a candle <i>(1851,McLean Papers,IX/167</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> But now ... the Moa-hunters disappear from the scene; but not without affording an insight into their daily life, by leaving us ... fragments of canoes, whares, and of wooden spears, fire sticks, and other objects ... <i>(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,416</i>)

>  <b>KNIGHT</b> [Rangitikei 1850s] But like children we soon forgot and played as friendly as before, quite willingly went to beg a `fire stick' or do some little borrowing. <i>(1975,Poyntzfield,41</i>) [NOTES: c1920s Eliza McKenzie in Outlook in 1975 Knight Poyntzfield 41]

>  <b>ANTHONY</b> I logged up about 10 acres ..., and had three blistering, heart-rending days burning it all at once. I went around every heap with a fire-stick first, ... <i>(1924,Follow the Call,30,1975 ed.</i>)

>  ~ n.-fire'-stick n. a smouldering piece of wood carried by a aborigines, when travelling, to start a new fire; any burning piece of wood. fire'-weed n. several plants that spring up after forest has been burnt off. <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,4</i>)

>  With the coming of the European flora, and with the destructive agencies of man, with the firestick and the grazing of stock and rabbits, great modifications were made in the flora of the natural pastures. <i>(1936,Agricultural Organization in NZ,358</i>)

>  <b>LEVY</b> The slash-hook, the fire stick, and grazing animals, particularly store cattle, are all important in the maintenance of this essential balance. <i>Ibid..</i>) 273 It has taken only a few years of drastic treatment with the firestick, and with grazing animals, including rabbits, to turn this almost static tussock [274] cover back to bare earth, ... <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,74,1970 ed.</i>)

>  fire-stick n. lighted stick caried by abos. when moving from camp to camp. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),8</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) [sl.] a rifle or shot-gun.

>  a rifle or shot-gun - a firestick. [firestick] <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a burning torch used to light bush and scrub in land clearing.

>  <b>ANTHONY</b> I logged up about 10 acres .. I went around every heap with a fire-stick first. <i>(1924,Follow the Call,30</i>)

>  <b>MARSDEN</b> Before we left this village for the next I took a fire-stick into a shed ... <i>(Oct 27, 1819,Letters & Journals of Samuel Marsden,211</i>) [NOTES: (1932)]

>  <b>BELSHAW</b> With the coming of the European flora, and with the destructive agencies of man, with the firestick .. great modifications were made in the flora of the natural pastures. <i>(1936,Agricultural Organization in NZ,358</i>)

>  <b>FERRIS</b> The firestick became the real emblem of farming progress in New Zealand for many years, rather than the plough. <i>(1940,Cavalcade of Farming 1840-1940,34</i>)

>  <b>LEVY</b> It has taken only a few years of drastic treatment wih the firestick, and with grazing animals .. to turn this almost static tussock cover back to bare earth. <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,273</i>)

### fireman
 
 <i>n.</i> grey kiwi or roaroa [(still used in 20th century)]

>  The "roaroa', which is locally known as "the fireman" .. is not found on the eastern side of Preservation Inlet ... <i>(Mar 26, 1910,Evening Post,13</i>)

>  The [sports] bag on one occasion contained teal, whistling duck, kakapo, emu - fireman, or kiwi - and woodhens. <i>(Sep 21, 1872,Otago Witness,8</i>)

>  Mr F. Strange, in a letter to Mr Gould [Proceedings Zool. Soc. 1847 p15] says `I am told that a second (now a third) species of Apteryx is to be found in the Middle Island, and that it stands about three feet high; it is called by the sealers the `fireman.' <i>(1850,NZ Journal,X/119</i>)

>  <b>BORWICK</b> The Fireman, a sort of a kiwi, stands three feet in height. <i>(1855,Geography Australia and NZ,180</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> [South Island Kiwi] Like many of the New Zealand birds the apteryx is nocturnal in its habits, dwelling beneath extensive beds of fern, where it conceals itself during the day. The largest species, the `fireman' of the whalers, is from the Middle Island, and is considerably larger than the other two. <i>(1866,Polynesia,58</i>)

>  Most of the birds, including kiwis, are recognisable from the descriptions [of the Foveaux Strait sealers], except one - a large bird they called the `Fireman', because it made a noise like the wooden rattles which firemen of those days carried to give the alarm. <i>(1971,New Zealand's Heritage,1/11</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>WHITWORTH</b> .. firemen, [fish] somewhat resembling mullet. <i>(1870,Martin's Bay Settlement,59</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

### fires
 
 <i>n.</i> [(child marble players')]

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> Some of the typical terms used in marbles referred to the act of shooting and the movement of the marbles. Those related to ways of shooting marbles were cribbing, firsts, fudging, funks, funnicks, funnigans, flunks, forces, knee-high, knuckle-down-stiff, knuckles, and lasts. Terms connected with the movement of the marbles were: cannons, clears, dead-stick, double-hits, fires, funs, gees, haystacks, in smug, keeps, kills, kisses, lay-ups, manyes, ones, pink, ringer, rolls, scat, screws, skinned, skun, spiders, sported-off, stays, stops, tracks, throughs. <i>(1972,Folkgames of Children,175</i>)

### fireship
 
 <i>n.</i> Refers to an establish ed troop sport of floating lighted paper down the running stream of water which flushes through long, multi-seated lavatories, so disturbing an otherwise restful vacation. [(WW1)]

>  <b>MILLER</b> Crook with dysentery all day - and can't find anything cheerful to write about. To cap it all some misguided clown let a `fireship' go sailing down the port `wireless station.' This crude attempt at humour should be banned. <i>(1939,Camps, Tramps and Trenches,42</i>) [NOTES: 1917 Mar 20 in 1939 Miller Camps, Tracks and Trenches 42]

 <i>n.</i> pohutukawa. [(obs)]

### firetruck(ing)
 
 <i>n.</i> loss of bladder control when drunk [used at Massey University]

>  Alvin's letter to <i>Chaff</i> (Alvin being the Massey student who wrote "firetrucks are cool"), was not as harmless as it appeared. "Your 'happy' letter writer Alvin was not referring to the big red firetrucks one sees driving round town," she wrote. "Firetrucking", male student style, is the practice.. of getting so hideously mullocked that control of the bladder is lost. A wet patch on the front and legs of the trousers ensues. This is the signal that a firetruck has occurred." <i>(Sep 2000,Off Campus,5(3) : 5</i>)

### fireweed
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Aristotelia serrata (The Wineberry)</i> ... In bush clearings, it is one of the first plants to come up, and would, on this account, be termed by the Americans a `fireweed.' <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,246,[1940, p. 258] ed.</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> FIREWEED (<i>Erechtites</i> spp.), occurs commonly all over the country as a wayside weed, and usually comes in great abundance after bush burns [thinnings]. It is annual or rarely biennial, erect upright, 2 to 4 ft. high, ... <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,182,[1967] ed.</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Erechtites prenanthoides </i>(The <i>Prenanthes-like Erechtites</i>) ... A common `fire weed' appearing in the bush after burning. <i>(1940,Plants of NZ,470</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> FIREWEEDS (<i>Erechtites </i>spp. classified also as <i>Senecio</i> spp.) <i>(1981,Weeds of Roadsides,51</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Cerastium glomeratum.</i>

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> Another fire weed that thrived prodigiously on newly-burnt land was mouse-ear chickweed (<i>Cerastium glomeratum</i>). <i>(1926,Tutira,284</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> WILLOW WEED (<i>Epilobium</i> sp[p].). - This is also called herb willow, swamp willow, and by confusion redshank and fireweed. It is a native that occasionally grows as a weed in or near ditches. The name is sometimes given to redshank [59], and it is true that both polygonum and epilobium grow in damp places, have willow-like leaves and are about 2 feet high. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,126,[1967] ed.</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> FLEABANE (<i>Erigeron canadensis</i>), also called Canadian fleabane, woolly cobbler's peg and, mistakenly, fire weed, is abundant throughout the North Island, and is found in waste ground and dry pastures. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,171,[1967] ed.</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (?Canterbury)

>  Fireweed: <i>Celmisia spectabilis</i>. One of the few plants which shepherds can always identify. Sometimes called fire weed, as it flourishes after burns. <i>(Sep 30, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]



 
 <i>n.</i> A generic term for plants that spring up after burns, most commonly <i>Erechtites</i> spp. See also Australian, cotton, fireweed; hogweed.

>  <i>fire ... fire'-weed n</i>. several plants that spring up after forest has been burnt off. <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,4</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Erechtites arguta. </i>

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> (And) <i>(1909,AJHR</i>) [NOTES: Report on a Botanical Survey of Stewart Island]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Erechtites quadridentata. </i>

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> (And.) <i>(1909,AJHR</i>) [NOTES: Report on a Botanical Survey of Stewart Island]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Erechtites scaberula. </i>

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> (And.) <i>(1909,AJHR</i>) [NOTES: Report on a Botanical Survey of Stewart Island]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Erechtites diversifolia.</i>

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> (And.) <i>(1909,AJHR</i>) [NOTES: Report on a Botanical Survey of Stewart Island]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Erechtites atkinsoniae</i>; Cf fireweed.

>  <i>Australian fireweed [s] Erechtites atkinsoniae</i> F.v. Muell. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,3</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> AUSTRALIAN FIREWEED (<i>Erechtites atkinsoniae</i>) Perennial, introduced from Australia ... Australian fireweed is common in many areas north of the Manawatu, but does not occur in the South Island. <i>(1981,Weeds of Lawns,7</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Erechtites penanthoides. </i>

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> (And) - <i>(1907,AJHR,C-8</i>) [NOTES: Report on Botanical Survey of Kapiti Island]

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> (And). <i>(1909,AJHR,C-12</i>) [NOTES: Botanical Survey Stewart Island]

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> (And). <i>(1915,AJHR,C-6</i>) [NOTES: Plant Covering Kennedy's Bush (Canterbury)]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Erechtites glabrescens. </i>

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> (And.) <i>(1909,AJHR,C-12</i>) [NOTES: Botanical Survey Stewart Island]

### First Echelon/First Ech
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  [Caption] When I came over with the First Ech, young fella, this camp was just bare desert. <i>(Jul 26, 1943,NZEF Times,III/109 : 5</i>)

>  <b>BRUNO</b> Certain near-sober First Echelon wife-beaters and debt-dodgers. <i>(1944,Desert Daze,(foreword)</i>)

>  But the most common name for the first group of New Zealand soldiers to see active service in the Middle East during the Second World War is the First Echelon ... Soon after New Zealand declared war on Germany on September 3, 1939, a call went out for volunteers for a special force comprising a wide range of army units. The use of the military term `Echelon' in the sense of first, second or third echelon has puzzled many people. It refers to the position of an army in the form of steps or parallel lines, each line being a little to the left or right of the preceding one. However, the name was given because the main groups of New Zealanders sent overseas in previous wars had been called contingents. <i>(Sep 18, 1982,Evening Post,12</i>)

>  <b>Ihimaera</b> On 29 November 1940, we were ordered to the Middle East to join the First Echelon. <i>(2000,Uncle's Story, The,40</i>)

>  <b>SOLJAK</b> The first Kiwi echelon landed in Egypt in February, 1940, ... <i>(1946,New Zealand (various authors/titles),126</i>)

>  They'd both been in the first Echelon of the 2nd NZEF... <i>(1987,Metro Fiction,89</i>) [NOTES: (Brown Beachcombers)]

>  <b>SINCLAIR & HARREX</b> The troops left home in three 'Echelons'. The first went to Egypt. As eager volunteers, they were labelled 'the wife beaters and debt dodgers'. <i>(1978,Looking Back,215</i>)

>  <b>SANDYS</b> Everyone knew the First New Zealand Echelon was due to depart for its "unknown destination" sometime during the first week of January. <i>(1982,Love and War,98</i>) [NOTES: 1989 edition]

>  "First Ech." Jim already had a Vat 69 at his elbow. <i>(Dec 22, 1941,NZEF Times,I/26</i>)

>  <b>BARTLETT</b> Perhaps the same sand spit on which the Maori canoe Tainui had also grounded, bring[ing???] `First Fleet' immigrants centuries earlier. <i>(1974,Emigrants, The,35</i>)

### First Four Ships
 
 <i>n.</i> esp. of Canterbury settlement, the four earliest emigrant ships

>  <b>SEWELL</b> It had been told greatly to Deans' credit that when the first 4 Ships came out Deans supplied the settlers with meat at 6d a lb, ... <i>(1853,Journal,1/295,1980 ed.</i>)

>  [quoting Fox to Godley: Godley papers, Canterbury Museum] Canterbury which I had not visited since your four first ships were discharging their first batch of immigrants. <i>(1971,Speeches & Documents</i>) [NOTES: (1858 Dec 31) 1971 Speeches and Documents 33 [quoting Fox to Godley: Godley papers, Canterbury Museum]]

>  At one time, indeed, the Canterbury Pilgrims, as the early settlers were called, `ruled the roost' in social matters. Those who came out in the `first four ships' were looked up to by later arrivals. To have `come over with the Conqueror' bade fair to pale in face of having `come over in one of the first four ships' In the Southern States of America you claim blue blood by belonging to one of the `first families'. <i>(1876,Colonial Travel,203</i>)

>  <b>HART</b> In the month of September [1850] the band of Canterbury Pilgrims ... set sail for Canterbury from Gravesend in four vessels, now known as the historical four ships, viz., Sir George Seymour, Charlotte Jane, Randolph, and the Cressy. <i>Ibid.</i> 26 It is true that Sir George [Grey] was here just when the first four ships arrived ... <i>(1886,Stray Leaves,7</i>)

>  <b>HOCKEN</b> From the Argo to the Mayflower, from the Arawa to the Tory, a romantic story or a great repute has always attached to those `first ships' and their sailors which left the shores of home for some obscure and distant land. ... fifty years ago the John Wickcliffe and the Philip Laing of Otago, and the `first four ships' of Canterbury, were invested with a halo which will always surround them. <i>(1898,Contributions to the Early History of New Zealand,92</i>)

>  I have touched only upon those ... colonists arriving prior to the first four ships. <i>(1900,Canterbury Old & New,57</i>)

>  <b>GILLESPIE</b> ... communication with the homeland has been universal and continuous from the `first four ships' until wireless. <i>(1930,NZ Short Stories</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE</b> ... the general opinion among the first four ships in the Bay is that you should keep out of the public eye for a bit ... <i>(1935,Little Country, The,325,1937 ed.</i>)

>  <b>ACLAND</b> Caverhill was one of the most remarkable of the pre-Adamite settlers (those who arrived before the first four ships) in Canterbury. <i>(1975,Early Canterbury Runs,ix/270,(4th ed.) ed.</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> Canterbury had been chosen as the site, and the `First Four Ships', carrying the settlers or `Pilgrims', had arrived in Lyttelton at the close of the year 1850. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,21,1954 ed.</i>)

>  <b>CRESSWELL</b> Centennial History should go back no further than 100 years - in the case of Canterbury back to the arrival of the `First Four Ships' that brought settlers to the Province in December of 1850. <i>(1951,Canterbury Tales,7</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Their sheep station ... is one of those which has stayed in the family from the beginning. They were not First Four Ships but they were gentry and they became squatter aristocracy as of right. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,77</i>)

>  <b>WALLIS</b> `First four bloody ships,' said Charlie disgustedly. <i>(1963,Point of Origin,12</i>)

>  <b>McLAGLAN</b> Indeed, without the Deans' sheep the settlers from the `First Four Ships' would have been hard pressed for meat. <i>(1965,Stethoscope & Saddlebags,13</i>)

>  <b>HARPER</b> Brittain has also arrived in one of the First Four Ships, Sir George Seymour. <i>(1967,Kettle on the Fuchsia, The,57</i>)

>  <b>HASCOMBE</b> We learnt that those who can claim to belong to a family who came out to New Zealand in one of the first four ships to arrive in Lyttelton ... in 1850 belong to an elite society known as the Canterbury Pilgrims. <i>(1969,Down and Almost Under,56</i>)

>  <b>ACLAND</b> Caverhill was one of the most remarkable of the pre-Adamite settlers (those who arrived before the first four ships) in Canterbury. <i>(1975,Early Canterbury Runs,ix/270,(4th ed.) ed.</i>)

>  (I.A.Gordon) [in generalised sense] Fortunately what crossed the seas in the first four ships was the recipe for the real thing [sc. pikelet]. <i>(Jun 4, 1983,Listener (New Zealand Listener),55</i>)

>  <b>BROWN</b> ... he earned the valuable support of those Women's Guild ladies who could trace their lineage back through the first four colonising ships. <i>(1986,Weaver's Apprentice, The,146</i>)

>  <b>MACRAE</b> One of Atareta Poananga's original flotsam and jetsam - his people came out on the First Four Ships. <i>(1988,Awful Childhoods,27</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> They were not First Four Ships but they were gentry and they became squatter aristocracy [in Canterbury] as of right. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,77</i>)

>  The class of people [among the first Canterbury settlers] is fair enough: there were more ladies in the first 4 ships than Nelson can boast of now, and there is an advertisement for a piano-tuner. <i>(Feb 26, 1851,Torlesse Papers,200,1958 ed.</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE</b> ... there is a water-tight little community quietly proud of the fact that its people came out from the south of England in what are known as the First Four Ships. <i>(1935,Little Country, The,5,1937 ed.</i>)

### first go off
 
 <i>phr.</i> at once, immediately; at first go, first pop

>  <b>SMYTH</b> We didn't quite get the hang of things first go off but now I reckon it will be a `beaut' [record]. <i>(1929,Girl from Mason Creek, The,161</i>)

 <i>phr.</i> the early days of the West Coast gold rush. [[c1860-70]]

### first of the gold
 
 <i>phr.</i>

### first of the rush
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> In the early days of any gold-field, or at `the first of the rush' as it is called, all is bustle and excitement. <i>(1874,Colonial Experiences,86</i>)

>  <b>PFAFF</b> It was no uncommon thing at the first of the rush for bar tenders ... to land 500 or 600 passengers a day at a pound a head. <i>(1914,Diggers' Story, The,2</i>)

>  <b>MAY</b> For impatient young men, anxious to be `in the first of the rush', and for those weakened by privation, every West Coast river was a likely grave. <i>Ibid.</i> 528 `THE FIRST OF THE RUSH': the phrase is self-explanatory. Some deference was due to those `in the first of the rush'. <i>(1967,West Coast Gold Rushes,280,1962 ed.</i>)

 <i>n.</i> with ref. to goldrushes

### first settler
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The last remarks contained in the report with regard to the want of easy access to the back country, and the consequent unfair pressure of the difficulties upon the first settler who endeavours to open up the country, are perfectly just, and the remedy proposed is certainly a practicable one; the only difficulty is the providing of funds, but the work ought to be done. <i>(Jan 30, 1858,Otago Witness,4</i>)

>  Mr. Duke entered the Wararapa [sic] last week with about forty head of cattle. Mr. Duke is the first settler in that fine district. <i>(Apr 24, 1844,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,3</i>)

>  Wednesday last being the 22nd Anniversary of the arrival of the first settlers in Port Nicholson, was observed as a general holiday; and the Government Offices, Banks and other places of business were closed. <i>(Jan 24, 1862,Wellington Independent,2</i>)

>  The honourable member for Wairarapa (Mr Bunny) had stated that the reason Wellington had not advanced as rapidly as the other Provinces was because there were too many educated men among its early settlers, and that these men either could not or would not work. Now he (Mr Buckland) had resided in Wellington for some months immediately after the arrival of its first settlers, and his impression was that there was a willingness and desire to work among all classes . <i>(Sep 28, 1871,Otago Daily Times,5</i>)

>  LAKES WANAKA AND HAWEA .. The first settler who made his habited [sic] in this terra incognita was the late Mr Henry Norman, who came, here as the manager of a station for a Wellington firm of squatters in February, 1859. <i>(Apr 9, 1881,Otago Witness,9</i>)

>  Now that there is a town of 1000 or more inhabitants within a few miles of the Knapdale homestead, a railway within three or four miles, and good roads all round the estate, it is not easy to imagine the difficulties and dangers that its first settler had to undergo - no neighbour for miles; no practising doctor for 100 miles, no help or comfort in any way whatever. <i>(Aug 14, 1890,Otago Witness,19</i>)

>  Then came the first settlers, men of energy and strong of heart, who rushed their way through many difficulties. <i>(Jul 3, 1923,Otautau Standard & Wallace County Chronicle,4</i>)

 <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of sheep] shorn only once in a twelve-month period.

>  The best of the first-shear ewes sold at $24-$28. [first shear] <i>(Jul 20, 1995,NZ Farmer,39</i>)

### first-footing
 
 <i>n.</i> introduced Scottish custom of visiting friends at midnight on New Year's Eve in order to be the first to put a foot within their doorway. [two citations below are of verb to first-foot]

>  Under the new law Scots gathered together in a No-license district for the discussion of whusky[sic] and hogmanay are liable to prosecution, and `first-footing' is a crime [re Ashburton]. <i>(Jan 14, 1911,NZ Truth,290/4/4</i>)

>  <b>GASKELL</b> `We were first-footing,' said Jack. `In case the Japs come before New year.' 1978 editorial note 188 Introduced Scottish custom of visiting friends at midnight of the New Year's Eve in order to be the first to put a foot within their doorway. <i>(1978,All Part of the Game,46</i>) [NOTES: 1945 Gaskell `Tidings of Joy' in 1978 All Part of the Game 46]

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Once [1939-1942] when we were first footing the Cass residents on New Year's Eve [the Lizzie] crashed through three double gates one after the other. <i>(1947,Wayleggo,148</i>)

>  <b>MARSHALL</b> `Used to be real live-wires when we were first married,' said Mrs Foden philosophically. `Never missed a tin-kettling or first-footing; euchre evenings, sales, duck shoots, dances, send-offs, fires. We always went. ...' <i>(1979,Supper Waltz Wilson,47</i>)

### firstie
 
 <i>n.</i> [-->1951 14-16 M 59 Wellington H.S. 25 FIRSTIES]

### firsts
 
 <i>n.</i> [(child marble players')]

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> Some of the typical terms used in marbles referred to the act of shooting and the movement of the marbles. Those related to ways of shooting marbles were cribbing, firsts, fudging, funks, funnicks, funnigans, flunks, forces, knee-high, knuckle-down-stiff, knuckles, and lasts. Terms connected with the movement of the marbles were: cannons, clears, dead-stick, double-hits, fires, funs, gees, haystacks, in smug, keeps, kills, kisses, lay-ups, manyes, ones, pink, ringer, rolls, scat, screws, skinned, skun, spiders, sported-off, stays, stops, tracks, throughs. <i>(1972,Folkgames of Children,175</i>)

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> Here are some [marble] terms from Taranaki around 1880-1890: firsts, lay-up, stakes, tips, sticking-taw, nothings, anythings, fade-it, knuckledown stiff, dubs, finking [sic, ?funking], sips, spanning, placing; and from Dunedin School in 1949: knee high, manyies, tracks, screws, nuts, sleets, kills, forces, dig, dubs, cheats. <i>(1981,History of Children's Play,269</i>)

### firsts and lasts
 
 <i>phr.</i> [(marble term)]

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> Action terms which refer to the shooting of the marbles are: Knuckle down Stiff, Knee High, Funks [Flunks sic] or Funnicks, Slips, Firsts and Lasts and Forces. ... The term `Knuckle down Stiff' (Taranaki [18]80, Gisborne [18]85, Waitara [18]95) is mentioned by [Lady A.B. Gomme Vo. 1, p.113]. ... As Taranaki [18]80 says, `The term was called out in order to prevent too easy a success against a marble near the ring'. It was less advantageous to fire with the hand on the ground than with the hand elevated, perhaps at `Knee High'. <i>(1953,Unorganized Games,2/769</i>)

### fisc
 
 <i>n.</i> OED : Any royal or state treasury; an exchequer. Now rare (Hist. or with allusion to confiscation').

>  Like guarding the fisc, resource management is a proper activity for government. <i>(Apr 14, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),38</i>)

### fiscal bikie
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  "Fiscal bikies" was what former prime minister David Lange called them when the vice-chancellors cornered him in the 1980s complaining that they could not increase enrolments while also accepting reduced budgets. <i>(Oct 7, 2002,Dominion Post,B7</i>)

### fiscal envelope
 
 <i>n.</i> a Government limitation on the total amount of money payable to Maori claimants under the Treaty of Waitangi settlements [Surfacing again in 1996 as a specific term for Maori refunds]

>  Such is the opposition to the proposals, some believe the issue could be more heated than the "fiscal envelope" hui of the mid-1990s, the last time Maori and the Crown met head on... The fiscal envelope was the term given to the then-National Government's plan to limit Treaty settlements. It was eventually dumped. <i>(Aug 29, 2003,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

>  To Smith it was simply a classic piece of radical publicity seeking, designed to focus debate on the government's now-famous "fiscal envelope". which would settle all Treaty claims for $1 billion in 10 years. <i>(Jan 14, 1995,Listener (New Zealand Listener),33</i>)

>  The fiscal envelope is like Selwyn Toogood's old quiz show, except it's called 'Money <i>and</i> the Bag.' <i>(May 13, 1995,Listener (New Zealand Listener),26</i>)

>  Maori expectations of big financial settlements soon rose so rapidly that Jim Bolger's ministry introduced the "fiscal envelope", limiting them to $1 billion. <i>(May 1, 2002,Dominion,10</i>)

>  The Maori leaders at those huis in the early 1990s conveyed to Parliament such strong messages as their opposition to the billion-dollar fiscal envelope. <i>(Mar 12, 2001,Dominion,2</i>)

>  Cabinet papers obtained by The Evening Post confirm that settlement payments are going to blow out well beyond the $1 billion cap - the so-called fiscal envelope - that the former National Government imposed in 1994. <i>(Jul 20, 2000,Evening Post,1</i>)

>  Moutoa might still never have happened without the galvanising spark of the fiscal envelope. <i>(Apr 22, 1995,Listener (New Zealand Listener),20</i>)

>  The Fiscal Envelope, what a lot of hooey! <i>(Feb 26, 1995,Sunday Star Times,C4</i>)

>  Bluntly, the fiscal envelope is a power struggle over who will control New Zealkand's resources. <i>(May 6, 1995,Listener (New Zealand Listener),14</i>)

>  Officially, the fiscal envelope - that sought to place a billion-dollar cap on Treaty settlements - is dead.... Yet the rest of the agreement suggests that, although the dread words "fiscal envelope" may no longer be utttered in decent company, the underlying process is really still alive and kicking. <i>(Feb 8, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener),28</i>)

### fish
 
 <i>n.</i> a whale; whales collectively. [(whalers')]

>  <b>BELL</b> If the fishing is to be carried on by a shore party, the try pots and huts are erected on the beach ... The boats are sent out at daylight every morning, and when ... they kill a fish it is towed ashore and flinched and boiled upon the beach. <i>(Nov 1831,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1831 Nov Bell Letter in 1913 McNab Old Whaling Days 9]

>  <b>POLACK</b> Tales of which [the whale] is the hero are ... significantly termed `fish stories.' <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),2/242</i>)

>  <b>HEAPHY</b> While [the whalers'] boats would be off the heads of the harbour, occasionally capturing `fish' there. <i>(1842,Narrative of a Residence in  Various Parts of NZ,121</i>)

>  The tide was not so strong as to draw in the `fish' as they were termed. <i>(1879,TrNZI,33</i>) [NOTES: (Heaphy `Port Nicholson in 1839')]



 
 <i>v.</i> [(whalers')]

>  The example of ships fishing on this coast proves there is no material difference in time. <i>(May 21, 1802,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1802 May 21 Lieut.-Gov. King Queries to Whalers in 1908 McNab Hist Records 1/234]

>  <b>McNAB</b> Whalers had `fished' off the northern coast from about 1794. <i>(1913,Old Whaling Days,1</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [1984 16 F E31 Pakuranga Coll. 3 FISH [something disliked] -->1984 17 M E111Pakuranga Coll. 24E FISH [name for authorities] -->1984 17 M E113Pakuranga Coll. 29 FISH (HOMOSEXUALITY) -->1984 17 F E57 Pakuranga Coll. 29 FISH ! (HOMUSEXUALITY)]

### fish and chip brigade
 
 <i>n.</i> [Name given to group of young Labour MPs who ate together regularly from the fish and chip shop opposite Parliament and were responsible for the first abortive and late successful coups to replace Rowling and subsequently became Ministers in the Lange Rogernomics Cabinet.]

>  <b>MOORE</b> [Caption] The fish-and-chip brigade at the wake after David Lange's abortive leadership challenge. <i>(1987,Hard Labour,83</i>)

>  The final defeat of the "fish and chip brigade" has the potential to unlock a huge chunk of the electorate previously hostile to the Moore-led Labour Party. <i>(Dec 3, 1993,Independent (Auckland),13</i>)

>  The plotters, including Mr Lange, became known as the "fish and chip brigade" - a newspaper photo after the failed putsch having shown them eating a meal of fish and chips. <i>(Aug 15, 2005,Dominion Post,A2</i>)

>  I also believe that the fish-and-chip brigade were being groomed, flattered and cooerced by big business entities, from here and the US, who saw opportunities and took advantage. <i>(Sep 24, 2005,Listener (New Zealand Listener),7</i>)

>  Mr Prebble is the last parliamentary survivor of the so-called fish and chip brigade, named after a 1980 newspaper photo in which, oddly, Mr Prebble himself did not appear. It instad showed then Labour MPs David Lange, Mike Moore, Roger Douglas and Michael Bassett eating fish and chips at Parliament. <i>(Apr 28, 2004,Dominion Post,B4</i>)

>  Mr Prebble's name has for years been associated with the so-called fish and chip brigade - the group of young Labour idealists who plotted to overthrow party leader Bill Rowling and later formed the backbone of the Lange Cabinet. <i>(Apr 28, 2004,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

>  So the last of the fish and chip brigade is about to take his salt and vinegar and go. <i>(Apr 29, 2004,Dominion Post,C2</i>)

>  The shop achieved a name for being the haunt of the "fish and chip brigade", the Labour plotters of Roger Douglas, Mike Moore, Michael Bassett and David Lange. <i>(Apr 6, 2002,Evening Post,1</i>)

>  Ironically, those [he] saw as Mr Lange's "enemies" were not the foes on the Opposition benches, but erstwhile friends and colleagues: Roger Douglas and Richard Prebble; stalwarts of the fish and chip brigade that engineered Mr Lange's rise to the leadership of Labour inside five years of his arrival in Parliament. <i>(Feb 26, 1995,Sunday Star Times,C2</i>)

>  This is the place David Lange, Mike Moore, Richard Prebble and Roger Douglas made famous when the fish and chip brigade, as they were called, could still sit around a Cabinet or dining table without gagging. <i>(Aug 20, 2001,Evening Post,6</i>)

 <i>n.</i> [also fish and chip brigade]

 <i>n.</i> in the metaphorical and extended sense of an objection or difficulty in the way of a scheme, policy or proposed course of action which could "impale" it and cause it to come to grief. [Jim writes : Much used in the 6os by then Minister of Justice Ralph Hanan; it caught on among journalists and officials. International English? Not recorded in any dictionary I have seen and not noticed in any extra-NZ reading.]

>  [Hdg] First-home fishhooks seen. There are fishhooks in Government ideas about helping low income families into their first homes, say emergency accomodation workers. <i>(May 18, 2003,NZ Catholic,10</i>)

>  The answer seems obvious but there were fish-hooks. At the time, Japan had so many of these late-model used cars that they were an embarrassment. <i>(Oct 18, 2004,Time (NZ edition)</i>) [NOTES: NZ Auto Report, Advertising section: [iii]]

>  <b>McGILL</b> fish hooks difficulties; eg `Any fish hooks in the report?' <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,45</i>)

>  [Hdg] Fishhooks in radio waves. The government's decision to allocate a quarter of the hi-tech radio spectrum to a pan-Maori trust is full of fishhooks. <i>(May 24, 2000,Dominion,12</i>)

>  Similarly, if employers choose to regard every conceivable fish-hook in the new law with paranoid timidity, the chances of industrial harmony are very slim indeed. <i>(Jun 10, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),22</i>)

### fish-kicker
 
 <i>n.</i> [ref at www.jinxmagazine.com/fishkicker.html - a Canadian/Alaskan site. "The vast majority of species was removed via the foot, I would kick the things onto a belt all day long eighteen hours a day for forty some straight days deep, deep in the northern latitudes of the dismal Bering Sea. Basically I crawled into a box of living or half living things drug up from the deep ocean and kick them out. This for forty days in the year 1994 was my cog in life. I was a Fishkicker."]

>  And I'm not saying that because I have.. worked as a fish-kicker in white overalls unloading the fishing boats at the docks. <i>(May 21, 2003,Capital Times,13</i>)

### fish-oh
 
 <i>n.</i> fishmonger

>  Hunterville `fish-ohs' have been notified that owing to the oppressive labor[sic] laws the price of fish has been raised-round fish fish a farthing per pound, and flat fish a half-penny. <i>(Jul 2, 1910,NZ Truth,262/1/3</i>)

### fishdown
 
 <i>n.</i> an overfishing operation

>  This most recent experiment in overfishing, called a "fishdown" in the trade, removed 110 tonnes of probably ancient paua over two years along this formerly virgin coast. <i>(Jul 1998,NZ Geographic,No. 39/75</i>)

### fisher
 
 <i>n.</i> whaler (?ship or captain)

>  The following fishers are in Cloudy Bay:- The Dragon, full; Courier, 300 barrels William Stoveld, 300 barrels of black oil, and 400 of sperm; ... Mossman's shore whaling gangs have secured 170 barrels. <i>(Jul 27, 1831,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1831 July 27 Letter from Cloudy Bay in 1913 McNab Old Whaling Days 14]

### fisherfolk
 
 <i>n.</i> [OED: 1854 H. MILLER Sch. & Schm. xxii. (1857) 481 Some of our Cromarty *fisher-folk.]

>  North facing to capture the all day sun, this is an ideal location for the keen fisherfolk as the property has riparian rights, its own boatshed and ramp. <i>(Feb 22, 2006,NZ Herald,Summer Escape,  H12</i>)

### fisherman's flag
 
 <i>n.</i> [(? rare obs)]

>  The net ... was lying along the starboard rail, with the exception of the `cod', or purse, which was suspended from a gaff. This is facetiously termed the `fisherman's flag' or colours. <i>(1906,Red Funnel,414</i>)

### fishery
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>LIEUT.-GOV. KING</b> Do you think any advantage would attend the fishery by being allowed to go as far to the northward on this side of the Pacific as you are permitted to go on the east side? <i>(May 21, 1802,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1802 May 21 Lieut.-Gov. King Queries to Whalers in 1908 McNab Hist Records 1/234 ?Austr. ref.]

>  <b>BELL</b> The black whale fishery was tried in New Zealand some years ago, but it was abandoned until last year, when it was renewed by one vessel and two shore parties from Sydney, and one vessel from Hobart Town. <i>(Nov 1831,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1831 Nov Bell Letter in 1913 McNab Old Whaling Days 3]

>  [=whaling station] At 4 P.M. J.Robinson from Price's fishery and shipped two Hands. <i>(Apr 27, 1840,Piraki Log,107</i>)

>  The great mainspring of activity at Waikouate, and in these southern parts generally, is Mr. John Jones, of Sydney, who has, for many years, been engaged in the New Zealand oil trade. Most of the fisheries thereabouts are fitted out by him; and he is the principal purchaser and exporter of the oil caught along the coast. <i>(Jul 20, 1844,Nelson Examiner</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Monro Notes of a Journey (Nelson Examiner July 20 et seq.) in 1898 Hocken Contributions 241 [sc. whale]]

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> [=whaling station] There is a way over to the Kaikora fishery on the eastern coast. <i>(1959,Early Travellers,240</i>) [NOTES: 1846 Heaphy Expedition to Kawatiri in 1959 Taylor Early Travells 240]

>  <b>JAN WELD</b> [=shore whaling station] [We descended] upon the site of the old fishery of Amuri; there are no Europeans living there now, and the broken boats and deserted habitations told a melancholy tale of the decay of the whale fishery. <i>(Jan 1851,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1851 Jan Weld Letter in 1851 Feb 21 NZGG 31]

### fishie
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The fishies [officials] will be wanting the glory. <i>(Sep 17, 1999,Evening Post,22</i>)

### fishing
 
 <i>n.</i>

The boats are sent out at daylight every morning, and when ... they kill a fish it is towed ashore and flinched and boiled upon the beach. <i>(Nov 1831,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1831 Nov Bell Letter in 1913 McNab Old Whaling Days 9]

 <i>n.</i> whaling-station

### fisho
 
 <i>n.</i> fisherman or fisherwoman [Internet search Feb 2006: Austral (11 000 hits), NZ (1000); also used N Amer]

>  Fishos Fancy.Staying in The Mission House with a two hour boat charter trout fishing.. on Lake Tarawera. <i>(1999,It's On In Rotorua,6</i>)

>  Also novice fisho's can visit any of the sponsor outlets for advice and equipment so they can join in the fun. <i>(Jan 31, 2006,Wairarapa Midweek,20</i>)

>  A discussion with your local sports-goods shop assistant is critical to help determine the style of rod and reel that would suit your fisho's method of fishing. A surf-casting rod may not be appreciated by a boat fisho, nor will a game reel be appreciated by a rock fisho. <i>(Dec 15, 2005,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: http://www.northernadvocate.co.nz/storyprint.cfm?storyID=3664959 Accessed 27 Feb 2006.]

>  ...when you get a cracker day with a very light southerly the west coast off Wellington, from south of Makara up to Kapiti is a great playground for fishos and boaties. <i>(Apr 19, 2005,Independent (Auckland),17</i>)

 <i>v.</i> to use one's hands

### fit
 
 <i>n.</i> [(criminals')]

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> Fit. Sufficient evidence to convict (a wrongdoer): New Zealand c. (-1932). <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,279</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> an outfit for injecting or using a drug; a hypodermic; hypodermic syringe, contraction of `outfit'.

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> I bought five more cans of morphine, and he threw in a fit as well so (?) could hit myself. <i>Ibid.</i> 248 FIT (n) Hypodermic syringe. Contraction of `outfit'. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,13</i>)

>  [drugusers'] Some addicts continue to ... vomit at the thought of having a `fit' in their hands, he says. <i>(Apr 5, 1986,Evening Post,19</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a dose of (heroin) drug (or merely as fit = outfit?)

>  `I grabbed the fit and threw it out of the window. It wasn't actually mine but I could have used it.' <i>(Feb 17, 1983,Dominion,4</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  ...fit as a butcher's dog... <i>(Sep 23, 1999,Radio Windy</i>) [NOTES: HWO says used by a person with a an English dialectal accent; probably an English expression]



 
 <i>adj.</i> catchphrase: you've got to be fit @/y' gottabe fit/ you've got to be quick, up to the mark. [1937 (R. Mason) Tararua Tramping Club]

 <i>v.</i> to find or fabricate evidence to secure a conviction. [(criminals')]

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> see DUD UP. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,248</i>)

 <i>phr.</i>

### fit as a buck rat
 
 <i>phr.</i> fit as a fiddle; very (physically or mentally) fit. [ca1935 Havelock]

>  Pure raw shark liver containing all the god knows what to keep you fitter than a buck rat... <i>(Oct 20, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),43</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Tank Tarrant the head sherang. Tank Tarrant the greatest thing since sliced bread. Fit as a buck rat. <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,172</i>)

>  It used to be accepted that people who were `as fit as buck rats,' as many of the elderly I have known so unabashedly put it, were likely to be very eager for sex, ... <i>(Nov 21, 1983,National Business Review,57</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Grimy as a sweep, greasy as a butcher's pup manhandling ammunition, but oh I feel fine, fit as a buck rat. <i>(1945,Gunner Inglorious,19</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Roger Bloody Wilkinson too crook to go overseas with the boys ... Don't give me that. He's as fit as a buck rat. <i>(1949,Gun in My Hand, A,127</i>)

>  <b>CRUMP</b> He was about 45 and looked as fit as a buck rat. <i>(1960,Good Keen Man, A,13</i>)

>  <b>WALLIS</b> Go on, make yourself as fit as a buck rat and in exactly the right mood for it. <i>(1963,Point of Origin,15</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> fit as a buck rat strong, healthy and athletic. [Slatter] <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,45</i>)

 <i>n.</i> error for six o'clock swill

### five per cent
 
 <i>n.</i> [Jim writes: Local & Historical. Several Wairarapa purchases of land from Maori in the Grey period had clauses providing that five percent of the profits of the resale of the land was to be set aside for school, flour mills, etc. for the benefit of Maori. This promise was never full honoured and was a source of Maori grievance thereafter. The <i>(Wairapapa) five percents</i> are the subject of a current application to the Waitangi Tribunal (<i>Wairarapa Times-Age</i> 6/4/00).] (See [Wellington Tenths](../W#wellington-tenths))

>  <b>BAGNALL</b> The Tauwharenikau block purchase as well as Tuhitarata, was completed with the aid of a provision peculiar to the Wairarapa, the requirement that five per cent of the subsequent return from land sales should be paid back to the Maori vendors.<i>Ibid.</i> 204 More culpable was the with-holding of moneys from the five per cents... Some members of Government expected that the mill would be erected from the 'five per cents' the proceeds from sales in certain designated blocks, for which.. the construction of flour mills was an explicit objective. <i>(1976,Wairarapa : An Historical Excursion,102</i>)

### five per cent clause
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  References made in the claim still considered valid were guarantees and promises made to Ngati Kahungungu under what was known as the "Five Per Cent" clauses and mentioned in all crown purchase deeds relating to Wairarapa. <i>(Mar 4, 1999,Dominion,11</i>)

### five to six rush
 
 <i>n.</i> also five-to-six swill; describing the rush in the last hour in public bars during the time of six o'clock closing to buy and drink as much beer as possible. [(hist.) New Zealanders themselves talk about the `five to six scrum'. Magistrates have likened the performance [during the six o'clock swill] to `pigs at a trough'.]

>  <b>SLATTER</b> This land where Rugby is a religion and a race track a shrine and the five to six rush Communal Living. <i>Ibid.</i> 90 The bar is crowded for the five to six rush is in full swing. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,52</i>)

### five wounds
 
 <i>n.</i> catchfly

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> CATCHFLY (<i>silene [gallica]</i>). Varieties of this species have been named <i>S.anglica</i> and <i>S.quinquevulnera</i>. [1967 quinquevulnera] Commons names are spotted catchfly, flycatcher, sticky weed, and five wounds. It is common in cultivated land and waste places such as gravelly river-beds throughout both islands. ... The flowers are white or often dark red, or sometimes white with a blood red blotch on each petal so that this variety is called <i>quinque vulnera</i>, `five wounds'. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,74,1967 ed.</i>)

 <i>adj.</i>
