### five-eighth
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  He was five-eighth for Auckland against Taranaki. <i>(Oct 1, 1947,NZ Observer,27</i>)

>  New Zealand has.. introduced some distinctive features of its own - in a playing sense there was the unique five-eighth system ("if he's not a half and he's not a three-quarter, he must be a five-eighth"). <i>(Apr 13, 1992,Dominion Sunday Times,9</i>)

>  <b>ELLISON</b> The backs, as played in New Zealand, consist of a Full-back.. the Five-eighth, who stands about midway between the forwards and the three-quarters, and the half-back, who generally plays close up to the forwards. <i>(1902,Art of Rugby Football, The,12</i>)

>  It was true they played without Allen, one of the best five-eight [sic] players in the colony. <i>(Sep 15, 1899,Taranaki Herald,3</i>)

### five-eighths
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BASKERVILLE</b> An erroneous impression seems to prevail amongst the English players and critics that the five-eighths of the New Zealand game were merely extra centre three-quarters. <i>(1908,Modern Rugby Football,56</i>)

>  Bell.. may make a successful first 5/8 with a few more games there. <i>(Jun 28, 1944,Matamata County Mail,2</i>)

>  <b>QUINN</b> Five-eighths. A positional term used almost exclusively in New Zealand; other countries used 'stand-off', 'fly-half', or 'outside half'. It originated with the 1903 New Zealand captain, Jimmy Duncan. <i>(1991,Encyclopedia of World Rugby,95</i>)

>  Kiernan.. was weak at five-eighths. <i>(May 14, 1904,NZ Observer,10</i>)

>  There was nothing startling about the play of the five-eighths, but Church was on the slow side as half. <i>(May 17, 1913,NZ Observer,10</i>)

>  <b>ELLISON</b> A five-eighths can be a frightful nuisance to the opposite side by constantly changing his game. <i>(1902,Art of Rugby Football, The,35</i>)

>  <b>BARR</b> The TEAMS. Britain. - Full back ...; three-quarters - Gibbs, Ponty Jones, Chapman; half backs, Davey and W. Major; ... Otago. - Full back ...; three-quarters - Dwyer, Bennet, O'Sullivan; five-eighths, McLeod and Dansey; half back, Eckhold; <i>Ibid.</i> 165 The two half back system, which was afterwards to develop into the now well-known five-eighths, prevailed very largely about 1880 ... <i>(1908,British Rugby Team in Maoriland,36</i>)

>  [It is stated that in 1889 Alhambra came from Dunedin to play Merivale and played only one half-back instead of two. The man who would have been the second half-back `stood between [the half-back] and the three-quarters line.' The Club had no name for the new position, and some discussion took place about it. The argument was ended when a Merivale player said that if the new position was between half and three-quarters, he must be five-eighths. The name was adopted.] <i>(Jun 15, 1954,Press, The,12</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> see WIPER KICK <i>Ibid.</i> 172 Woodened out the five-eighths with a straight arm tackle. <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,44</i>)

>  five-eighth n. Austral. & N.Z. a rugby player positioned between the half-backs and three-quarters. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,420</i>) [NOTES: [cf. 1986 NZPOD 283]]

>  <b>STONE</b> James Duncan, of Otago,.. instituted the two five-eighths system in New Zealand. <i>(1938,Rugby Players Who Have Made NZ Famous,89</i>)

>  <b>PALENSKI</b> The pivot for most of Alhambra's attacks [sc. in a game in Dunedin between Kaikorai and Alhambra in 1890] was the extra player between the halfback and the three-quarter line. After the game players from both sides sat around discussing tactics and a Merivale back, one F. Childs, is credited with coming up with the new name, five-eighths. A blacksmith, Childs obviously thought logically. If the player was not a half and was not a threequarter, he said, he was between the two and therefore must be a five-eighth. <i>(1992,Our National Game,26</i>)

>  One of our well-known members Fred Childs gave the player between the halfback and three-quarter back the name of five-eighth which has been adopted throughout New Zealand ever since. <i>(1982,Blue and the White, The,12</i>) [NOTES: Fred Childs, blacksmith of Makikihi, b. about 1865. No other information]

>  <b>HENDRY</b> He wasn't a half and not a three-quarter so someone said he must have been a blooming five-eighth, and this is reported to be how this position first started. <i>(1984,Boots of the Bamas,37</i>)

>  The following Auckland team has been selected.. five-eighths Phelan, Riley; half-back Young. <i>(Aug 21, 1899,Taranaki Herald,3</i>)

>  <b>DAVIN</b> The two Star five-eighths would be too late to get back on side, however they ran. <i>(1949,Restless Earth,29</i>)

### five-finger
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  [Caption] Five finger, ngaio, cabbage trees, wineberry and pittosporums were among the species planted. <i>(Jun 10, 2004,Dominion Post,A3</i>)

>  And seeds from five finger and seven finger plants (plants with leaves that have fivce or seven lobes), have high levels of oleic acid which is also an important component of high temperature cooking oils such as sunflower oil. <i>(Nov 28, 2001,Capital Times,15</i>)

>  <b>MACLEAN</b> The deer began by colonising the forested ridges and spurs but when those were depleted of five finger and other palatable species, they moved up to the tussock tops. <i>(1994,Tararua : the Story of a Mountain Range,181</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>(1926,TrNZI,56/701</i>)

>  <b>LEVY</b> ... such species as lancewood, karamu, toro, ramarama, koromiko, kanono, kohuhu, kawakawa, hangehange, rangiora, five finger, patete, toru, tree daisies, broadleaf, kamahi, mahoe, tarata, karo, lacebark, wineberry, fuchsia, kowhai, with tree ferns, weki, and mamuku. Toetoe, phormium, cabbage tree, with swamp coprosma and hupiro may figure as a developmental phase on wet soils. <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,87,1970 ed.</i>)

>  five-finger n. any of various plants having five-petalled flowers or five lobed leaves, such as cinquefoil and Virginia creeper. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,420</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>Henderson</b> We are on safer ground when we come to the Asbestos Cottage speciality of five-finger juice. <i>(1981,Exiles of Asbestos Cottage, The,71</i>)

>  We noticed a weka had walked along the five-finger pole and was having a go at the turkey. <i>(Dec 19, 1992,Listener (New Zealand Listener),26</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Neopanax</i> (formerly<i> Nothopanax) arboreum</i>, five-fingered jack, New Zealand fig, snotty-gob, whauwhau, often changed to ohau.

 <i>n.</i> <i>Neopanax arboreum</i> [(obs.)] (See [five-finger](fin-fly.md#five-finger))

>  Broadleaf [fn <i>Griselinia littoralis</i>.], five-fingered Jack [fn <i>Panax arboreum</i>.], lacebark [fn <i>Hoheria angustifolia</i> and <i>Plagianthus betulinus</i>.], matipo [fn <i>Pittosporum</i>.], red matipo [fn <i>Myrsine urvillei</i>.], New Zealand coffee [fn Species of <i>Coprosma</i>.], titoki or native ash [fn <i>Alectryon excelsum</i>.], and the totara pine [fn <i>Podocarpus totara</i>.] jostle one another in the dense dry bush. ### five-o
 
 <i>n.</i> a police officer

>  Although better known terms such as <i>pig, filth</i> and <i>heat</i> are common, <i>five-o</i> is also used to describe a police officer. <i>(Oct 1999,NZWords,2(2) : 1</i>)

### five-o'clocker
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  There have been several "five o'clockers" recently. <i>(Sep 15, 1938,NZ Observer,17</i>)

>  Compulsory overtime had applied during the war; since then hundreds of men in Auckland, the so-called "five o'clockers", had declined to work after 5pm. <i>(1993,Wharfie,p122</i>)

>  [Hdg] Southland enjoys "Five O'Clockers" and Teas. <i>(Mar 23, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,30</i>)

>  [Hdg] Another Five O'Clocker. <i>(Oct 26, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,30</i>)

### fix bayonets
 
 <i>n.</i> methylated spirits (as a drink).

>  (Round the World for Ninepence, Jessies's [sic] Dream, Fix Bayonets, devotees called methylated spirits, recommending orange juice as a a dilutant. Diluted or neat, it can, and does, cause blindness.) <i>(Jun 10, 1984,NZ Times,10</i>) [NOTES: (J. Henderson)]

>  fix bayonets methylated spirits; variant of military term for Bermuda rum, with ref. its sting; WWII army. [Jim Henderson] <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,45</i>)

### fix up
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  Gunn never used the words `They were going to fix me up,' meaning that they intended preferring a charge against him. <i>(Oct 6, 1906,NZ Truth,68/6/2</i>)

### fixed bayonets
 
 <i>n.</i> (obsolete) a strong beer [cf P: brand of Bermuda rum late 19-early 20C.: 1914-18 Spirit made in POW camps; nf W3]

>  <b>ELKINGTON</b> We used to call the beer we bought in these [gum-] fields `fixed bayonets', `streaked lightning' and `fire water'; they were the three brands, and the names were strangely suitable. - <i>(1906,Adrift in NZ,241</i>)

### fixed foot
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [fencing] an anchor fixed to the post it supports.

>  <b>MAF</b> Fixed foot: where the foot itself is secured to the post before it is put in the hole. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,28</i>)

### fizgig
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> An informer to the police: Australian: since ca. 1910. In, e.g., Partridge 1937, and Sidney J. Baker, A Dictionary of Australian Slang, 1942 (and, a year earlier, in his new Zealand Slang). Often shortened to fiz(z): Baker, 1945. By contemptuous euphemism; not unrelated to thingamyjig. Baker (1945), however, points out that it may derive from fizgig, Australian for `fishing spear': cf. therefore, spear, v., 1. <i>(1949,Dictionary of the Underworld,247,1961 ed.</i>)

### fizz
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McGILL</b> Drinking fizz and eating sherberts. <i>(1989,Kiwi Baby Boomers,105</i>)

>  <b>BOOTH</b> The young swarmed into milk bars for ice cream sundaes and fizz and the jukebox. <i>(1997,Deadline : My Story,14</i>)

>  <b>COTTRELL</b> ... a bottle of `fizz' each. <i>(1942,Lost Cave of Pukerangi, The,81</i>)

>  <b>BEAGLEHOLE</b> [A special dinner would] include: ... meat ..., cabbage, puha, ... raspberry drink, `fizz', tea, and perhaps some beer. <i>(1946,Some Modern Maoris,96</i>)

>  <b>GASKELL</b> Micky grinned ... but his eye was on the fizz. <i>(1978,All Part of the Game,88</i>) [NOTES: [esp. children's] 1947 Gaskell `School Picnic' in 1978 All Part of the Game 88]

>  He swore he had only had raspberry fizz. <i>(Jan 1, 1945,NZ Dairy Exporter,77</i>)

>  <b>PARK</b> The fizz was made of cream of tartar and bicarbonate, and was a special treat. <i>(1957,One-a-pecker, Two-a-pecker,112</i>)

>  There were mostly Maoris in the shop getting fizz and pies. <i>(1950,Landfall,4(4) No. 16/310</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> an informer, a grass. [(police and criminals')slang]

>  Copper's NARK or police FIZZ - it's a dangerous game. In November ... a paid police informer .... died after being beaten ... and burned with cigarettes. ... A detective will tell `fizz', for example,: `This looks like good information, but I have to test it. <i>(Sep 25, 1988,Sunday Star (Auckland),A10</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  He is the proud owner of a 28 foot, twin V8 powerboat which he says is ideal for an "evening fizz" around Annapolis. <i>(Dec 12, 1999,Sunday Star Times,C1</i>)

### fizz boat
 
 <i>n.</i> a small motorboat usually driven by an outboard motor.

>  <b>McCARROLL</b> Yachties have a superior attitude to speedy power boats which they dismiss as fizz boats. <i>(1992,Life is so Complicated,159</i>)

>  [Hdg] 'Glorified fizz boat' tackles Cook Strait...<i> [Ibid.]</i> 58 On a clear crossing under the watchful eye of the skipper the occasional youngster has been known to steer the ship which.. is "really just like a big fizz boat." <i>(Mar 1996,AA Directions,57</i>)

>  Quietly paddling the Devonport-Takapuna coastline this summer I was nearly run over by an otherwise sane-looking Dad in a tinny pulling his child on an inflatable "biscuit". Only trouble was he was driving fast forward while facing backwards, waving at his child. <i>(Mar 2004,North & South,8</i>)

>  A couple fishing from a fizz boat express disbelief when we respond to their greeting, asking where we've come from. "You must be bloody mad!" <i>(Feb 14, 2004,Dominion Post,E18</i>)

>  Selling this boat isn't like selling a fizzboat. <i>(Jun 5, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),28</i>)

>  [Hdg] Fizz-boats, Frigates and Fast Ferries. <i>(Apr 2000,Elements,3: 1</i>)

>  Another thing to remember about charters compared with a couple of guys fishing off a fizz boat is that there are 20-odd baits in the water and that makes pretty impressive berley. <i>(2009,Mana,86/47</i>)

>  At the holiday resorts the hoi polloi are tolerated, if only just, despite their rude intrusions of fizz boats and beer cans. <i>(Jan 9, 1987,Dominion,6</i>)

>  <b>SCOTT</b> [caption to a picture of an old runabout] Edgar Bradley heralds the fizz boat era by fitting an Austin Seven motor to his homemade runabout. <i>(1987,Seven Lives on Salt River,50</i>)

>  <b>BURCHFIELD</b> fizz boat N.Z. colloq. Also fizzboat. [f. FIZZ sb. or v. + BOAT.] A motor-driven speed-boat. <i>(1988,Lexicographical & Linguistic Studies,194</i>)

>  The water was like glass as the Royal barge came in, chased by speedboats, `fizz boats' - the New Zealand name for outboards. <i>(Feb 24, 1977,Napier Daily Telegraph,19/7</i>)

>  here are everyman's little fizz-boat to the great petrol guzzling twin 200 horsepower outboard motor driven racing machines. <i>Ibid.</i> Jan. 90/1 It's within easy rowing distance of Bucklands Beach and is a popular fizz-boat haunt. <i>(Jan 1984,Metro,15/2</i>)

>  Unfortunately, our fizz boat was white with an orange trim and thus our childhood nautical exploits were confined to waterskiing down the Waikato River or fishing on the Firth of Thames. <i>(Jan 11, 2004,Sunday Star Times,D5</i>)

>  <i>Jewel by the Sea</i> tells of the period between 1917 and 1960, when the motels and fizz-boats started to take over. <i>(Nov 26, 1988,Listener (New Zealand Listener),81</i>)

### fizz bottle
 
 <i>n.</i> a returnable soft-drink bottle

>  <b>GASKELL</b> Boxes of pies stood near ... and rows of red and yellow fizz bottles along the wall. <i>(1978,All Part of the Game,86</i>) [NOTES: 1947 Gaskell `School Picnic' in 1978 All Part of the Game 86]

### fizzer
 
 <i>n.</i> something that fizzles out, esp. a cracker which does not explode, but, often, merely fizzes (ca1930 at least). [1953 14 M A20 Thames DHS 7 FIZZ,A -->1953 14 M A4 Thames DHS 7 FIZZER]

>  <b>TRAIL</b> A little lad had found a cracker without a wick in the grass near his gate and had coaxed his mother for a match to let the `fizzer' off. <i>(1984,Child of the Arrow,45</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> something which does not come up to expectations, or fulfil claims made for it (ca1935 at least).

>  Then blow me down if one of the Wellington papers didn't say that the demonstration was a fizzer because nobody disrupted, charged or got arrested. <i>(Jun 22, 1985,Listener (New Zealand Listener),24</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> an informer, decoy

>  The late Patrick McNally was in no way involved in drugs while he resided in New Zealand. In no way was Patrick McNally a `Police Fizzer'. <i>(Jun 12, 1983,NZ Times,8</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a gentle, noiseless (though stinking) fart (ca1935); (?= a fizzler). [(esp. schoolboys')]

### fizzled
 
 <i>adj.</i> drunk

>  <b>LORD</b> We say a man is .../ ... `fizzled', `fumed', `floored', `fizzed'; `full', and ah yes, `fixed' <i>(1921,Ballads of Bung 'Stunology',1976 ed.</i>)

### fizzler
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>LELAND</b> `It's a fizzler!' It's a FAILURE. As are most things that fizzle out. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,40</i>)

### fizzmegig
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Stepped there into the box Giuseppie Rossini, a fair, fleecy vision in a dream of a costume, crowned by an elegant fizzmegig called a hat, set off by a bird of plumage and some mauve colored [sic] fluff. <i>(Aug 18, 1906,NZ Truth,61/8/3</i>)

### fizzy
 
 <i>n.</i> a carbonated soft drink

>  ... milk for the children or fruit drinks other than `fizzies' ... <i>(Jun 1953,Here & Now,22</i>)

>  Fizzies are carbonated beverages, and spirits means liquor... Fizzies - soda. <i>(Jan 14, 1987,NZ Woman's Weekly</i>) [NOTES: 1986 B.G. Ostmann U.S. food-writer's written remarks: p.c.Tui Flower NZ Woman's Weekly (14 Jan 1987)]

>  "Come on inside - you like a cuppa? You like some fizzy, Josh? ..." <i>(Dec 22, 2005,Wairarapa Midweek,11</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a sparkling wine

>  Buyers of wine, particularly of affordable and drinkable Christmas fizzies, have never had it so good. <i>(Dec 14, 1996,Dominion,21</i>)

### fizzy drink
 
 <i>n.</i> carbonated soft drink [(mainly children's) From N.Z. English to U.S. English Dictionary at http://nz.com/NZ/Culture/NZDic.html] (See [also fizzy](fin-fly.md#fizzy))

>  It bottles mineral water from a spring at the foot of Tongariro - without the help of any multinational fizzy-drink manufacturers, or Auckland beer barons. <i>(Dec 1996,Mana,No. 14 : 9</i>)

>  There were always huge rolls of paper for all the tables, with little bottles of fizzy drink and piles of sweets for the kids. <i>(Apr 10, 1993,Listener (New Zealand Listener),54</i>)

>  I hated it there. My first time I hid in the dunny for about an hour. But she usually gave me a good feed - savs or snarlers, and sometimes a whole chip of strawberries. And fizzy drink instead of cordial. <i>(1988,University Entrance Board Bursaries Exam,6</i>)

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> The youngsters cried, "Atta boy!" as they raced to the store to buy bright red fizzy drinks and chocolate blocks. <i>(1938,Brown Man's Burden,66</i>)

>  <i>(1911,Triad, The,45</i>) [NOTES: [NB sense here is champagne] ca1920 Auckland R. Mason fizz; fizzy-drink.]

>  <b>FRAME</b> O the train ... and the bottle of fizzy drink that you could only half finish because you were too full, ... <i>(1951,Some Other Country,101</i>) [NOTES: 1951 Frame `Swans' in 1984 Some Other Country 101 [children's]]

>  <b>LELAND</b> A SOFT DRINK is a fizzy drink. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,40</i>)

>  Unsurprisingly, the kids buy what they can afford - and highly coloured strongly flavoured fizzy drinks are usually cheaper than other things, even water. <i>(Jun 23, 2004,Capital Times,2</i>)

>  Your article incorrectly states that fizzy drinks are the main Source of sugar for children. <i>(Nov 23, 2003,Sunday Star Times,C11</i>)

### FL
 
 <i>abbr.</i> French letter

>  <b>ARMFELT</b> Bob bought rubbers off the chemist - you know, F.L.'s - <i>(1971,Catching Up,15</i>)

### flag
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) of keas, to pull the wool on sheep's backs.

>  <b>ASPINALL</b> .. a kea silently came in and flagged fifteen of the sheep in the same paddoock. <i>(1993,Farming Under Aspiring,136</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The roof was thatched with raupo and strips of totara bark called "flags." <i>(1988,Tales of Pioneer Women,205</i>) [NOTES: Writing of 1940]



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) sheepdog's tail.

>  <b>MILLS ET AL</b> With his [the dog's] 'flag' outstretched and slightly waving, he shows no undesirable signs of unsteadiness or any desire to move in too close. <i>(1964,Practical Guide to Handling Dogs and Stock,54</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a lock of wool left unshorn on a sheep's rump

>  (Wallace Shearing Glossary) flag: Lock of wool left unshorn on a sheep's rump, and sometimes called a `chinaman'. <i>(1989,NZ English Newsletter,3/24</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) an old word for any of a number of rushes; bulrush, raupo, cooper's flag [ca1930-1950 Still in occasional use in Marlborough and Wellington (HWO, N.C. Small) with the more common <i>raupo, bulrush</i>.]

>  <b>ADAMS</b> On the north side of the harbour ... there is a small `paa' or village. The `warries' ... are generally low, built of mud or reeds, and sometimes thatched with `t&diero.i-t&diero.i, a kind of grass or small flag which grows to the height of three or four feet ... <i>(1853,Spring in the Canterbury Settlement, A,49</i>)

>  <b>SEWARD</b> First gentle Flora ... [12]/ Thin folds of *vegetable silk, behind,/ Shade her white neck, and wanton in the wind;/ Strange sweets, where'er she turns, perfume the glades,/ And fruits unnam'd adorn the bending shades. [fn] Vegetable silk.- In New Zealand is a flag of which the natives make their nets and cordage <i>(1780,Elegy on Captain Cook,11</i>)

>  <b>ELDER</b> ... our house or hut is made with flags by the natives. <i>(1934,Marsden's Lieutenants,97</i>) [NOTES: 1815 Feb 15 John King in Elder Marsden's Lieutenants (1934) 97]

>  <b>NICHOLAS</b> The herbaceous productions indigenous to the country are, wild celery, canary grass, wild parsley, plantain grass, a species of ray-grass, the <i>ensata</i> or flag, the phormium tenax or flax-plant, and a species of the fern. <i>(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),2/249</i>) [NOTES: [=RAUPO] [also 2/339]]

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> (And.) <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles)</i>)

>  [whalers' =Raupo] Boat in Pigeon Bay - cutting spars for house ... [Apr 16] 2 boats fetching Spars Flags &c. for a house, <i>(Apr 15, 1836,Piraki Log,33</i>)

>  <b>HEMPLEMAN</b> [=cooper's flag, raupo [for whale-oil casks]] We wish you to procure for us ... as many Flags ampersand Spars as possible, - Let these also come up next p. trip of the Bee. <i>(Aug 17, 1836,Letter,152</i>) [NOTES: 1836 Aug 17 Hempleman Letter in 1913 McNab Old Whaling Days 152]

>  <b>E.J. WAKEFIELD</b> ... the New Zealand flag ... <i>(Jun 3, 1840,Diary,1839 ed.</i>)

>  ... men employed bringing flags from the creek. <i>Ibid.</i> 123 [=raupo (esp. in whaling contexts)] Men employed in bringing grass and flags from the creek; Carpenter at work. <i>(Apr 16, 1841,Piraki Log</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> What we used to call flags then [in the 1840s], but now known as bulrushes or by the Maoris called rapo (or raupo), were used by the coopers then. They were inserted between the joints and kept the oil casks as tight as a drum. <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,29</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Arundo conspicua</i>, toetoe [1930-50 Manawatu (N.C. Small)]

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> ... the Thatch is the Toie Toie [<i>toetoe</i>] a kind of Flags that is used for the Huts, and an other one more like what our Coopers use for putting between the seams of a Cask, called Rappoo [<i>raupo</i>]. <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),35,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>ADAMS</b> <i>(1853,Spring in the Canterbury Settlement, A</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) [Botanical.]

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> At 3 the natives were served with their repast of beef potatoes and stirabout. As our boys have had some experience in this important duty ... our visitors were ... surprised at the order and expedition with which this assemblage of New Zealand rank was supplied, as the feast consisted of bout 800 dishes constructed of [?flax, ?raupo] a plant similar to the flag. <i>(May 17, 1833,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The,314,1961 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> =flag away; abandon [1984 17 F E59 Pakuranga Coll. 13 FLAG IT -->1984 16 M E72 Pakuranga Coll. 13 FLAG IT]

>  ... confessing [my] ignorance of the origin of the verb `to skull' (to drink quickly), the verb `to flag' (to waive something, to put it aside, not to bother about it), and the ... nouns `stir' and `rage' [adolescents' party names.] <i>(Dec 6, 1980,Listener (New Zealand Listener),56</i>) [NOTES: I.A Gordon]



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) danthonia and other green feed that has gone to seed.

>  <b>MCKENNY</b> Frequently the surplus grass runs to seed and afterwards there is a great deal of what graziers call 'flag.' This flag is neglected by the sheep; they prefer the shorter green grass. In danthonia country, therefore, as in rough bush country, it is profitable to run a limited number of cattle among the sheep. They clean up this rough 'flag', break through bushy and rushy obstacles and thus purify the pasture for the sheep. <i>(1927,Sheep-Farming, Fencing etc.,15</i>)

>  Pasture that is neglected or has gone to seed: flag. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> (Environment) (occas.) <i>Phormium tenax</i> flax [Botany.]

>  <b>HAWKESWORTH</b> (And.) <i>(1769,Voyage Round the World,70</i>)

>  <b>FORSTER</b> .. on these they had laid the bark of trees, and covered the whole with the rough fibres of the flag, or New Zeeland flax-plant. <i>(1777,Voyage Round the World,1/201</i>)

>  <b>FORSTER</b> ... he held a green plant of the New Zeeland flag in his hand, and now and then spoke a few words. [226] Many of their cloaks, made of the fibres of the New Zeeland flag (phormium), were new ... <i>(1977,Voyage Round the World,1/224</i>)

### flag away
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>SLATTER</b> He did not like radio commentaries because he did not have the type of mind that could readily visualise the game from such crescendic cliche[acute]s: the pouring into the twenty-five, the piling into the ruck, the try was on all the way. The words evoked no images in his mind: a ton of ball, buried in the tight, well and truly tackled, flagged away, favouring his leg, a flurry of fists, bundled into touch, ... <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,115</i>)

>  He doesn't take acid any more, but can never see himself flagging away marijuana. <i>(Apr 5, 1986,Evening Post,19</i>)

### flag fall
 
 <i>n.</i> an initial charge for the hiring of a taxi [also Australian; also now used in both countries of a connection fee for a mobile phone call]

### flag station
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  He considered it an insult for such a large district to be put off with a siding and a flag-station. <i>(Aug 16, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,7</i>)

>  <b>LEITCH</b> [Caption] Typical of the small country 'flag station' as built in the 1870s and 1880s. <i>(1972,Railways of NZ,123</i>)

### flag track
 
 <i>n.</i> a track marked with flags (cf. blaze) [(? obs rare)]

>  <b>REISCHEK</b> Over grass country or over snowfields the best thing to do is to make a flag track by cutting sticks and tying on one end a small piece of calico. <i>(1889,Story of a Wonderful Dog, The</i>)

### flag-man
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [early aerial weed-spraying] a flag-waving man on horseback used to direct a plane to boundaries of weedy areas.

>  Because of this [the height of the plane] the flag-men are important. At Kapiti Station six 'flag-men' were used, working in pairs. Two pairs gave the aircraft the marks to run in and out, and the this pair then had time to move into a new position. From eight to ten men were engaged on the job during the progress of the spraying, including some of the station shepherds who acted as flagmen to direct the pilot on each run. <i>(Jul 5, 1951,NZ Farmer,07</i>)

### flagged
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of a high country sheep] with wool ripped from its back by a kea or by rough handling.

>  <b>ASPINALL</b> .. sheep up to a kilometre away would run in a startled manner and many of these would be flagged. 'Flagging' is a name coined where a sheep has had the wool pulled up on its back as if grabbed by a hand or in this case pulled up by a kea's beak. One winter some 38 sheep died and another 200 flagged. <i>(1993,Farming Under Aspiring,131</i>)

### flagging
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the pulling up of a sheep's wool on its back by keas or rough handling.

>  <b>ASPINALL</b> 'Flagging 'is a name coined where a sheep has had the wool pulled up on its back as if grabbed by a hand or in this case pulled up by a kea's beak. <i>(1993,Farming Under Aspiring,131</i>)

### flaggy grass
 
 <i>n.</i> raupo

>  <b>NICHOLAS</b> Flaggy grass Roupo <i>(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),2/339</i>)

>  [Vocabulary of English and NZ Words] Flaggy grass Roupo <i>(1844,NZ Chief,25</i>)

### flagon
 
 <i>n.</i> a large bottle or container for beer or other liquor, or its contents, a half-gallon jar (See [also half G](h-hea.md#half-g))

>  I have no memory of who won, but recall a hearty open fire, flagons of beer and snacks, being fussed over by jovial men and perhaps the legendary rugby commentator Winston McCarthy calling on the crackly Atlantic feed: "Wait for it. It's a goal!" <i>(Jul 5, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),67</i>)

>  In some ways the Rakino property represents New Zealand the way it was, a rustic Kiwi bach built in the days when beer came in flagons and rugby tests were played in the afternoons. <i>(Aug 22, 2004,Sunday Star Times,C13</i>)

>  <b>FRANCES</b> [sc. of beer] He reached for the flagon. Dinny always had a flagon about the house somewhere, <i>(1964,Johnny Rapana,20</i>)

>  <b>HELMER</b> You could have knocked me down with a flagon when my mate Helmer put the acid on me for my opinion of a book he'd written about the time he spent in New Zealand with us. <i>(1964,Stag Party,5</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> Flagon. A HALF imperial GALLON BOTTLE OF BEER. (Now 2.25 liters). It is customary to buy a couple of flagons from the bottle store to take home; much as an American might pick up a six pack. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A</i>)

>  (Goldie Brown Collection) flagon <i>(Feb 1981,Avondale College Slang Words in Use</i>)

>  New Zealand's traditional take-away beer container - the flagon, also known as a `jar', `half-G' or `peter' - was yesterday defended by its manufacturer ... `The quality for the vinegar flagons is exactly the same. We are not out of production of half-gallon jars. <i>(Oct 6, 1982,Dominion,3</i>)

>  The association had not established that the cost of selling beer in flagons was greater than the cost of selling by other means. <i>(Jun 16, 1955,Dominion,14</i>)

>  <b>MILLER</b> They were sitting.. enjoying an occasional sip from a flagon when they heard a disturbance outside. <i>(1967,Ink on My Fingers,36</i>)

>  <b>McGILL & TILLEY</b> Old square gin bottles were filled with beer. They held a third of a pint [?gallon], forerunners of the flagon. <i>(1980,In Praise of Older Buildings,95</i>)

### flagon eye
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Many of us turned to it, simply to avoid the dangers of "flagon eye" from volatile half gees. <i>(Aug 29, 1999,Sunday Star Times,D5</i>)

### flagon wagon
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>WHEELER</b> At this stage I remember the many things that I have had no room to mention ... the `flagon wagon' arriving at Te Paki from Houhora, New Zealand's northernmost pub, ... <i>(1973,Historic Sheep Stations NI,102</i>)

### flagon-party
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> The flagon-parties, the crates of half-gees on the sink, men in singlets queued up at the keg; ... <i>(1974,Maori Woman,110</i>)

### Flagstaff War
 
 <i>n.</i> (historical)

>  <b>BARBER</b> From 1844 to 1847 the `red tribe' fought the first New Zealandwar, against Hone Heke, and his allies. The `Flagstaff War', as it is sometimes called, followed a build up of disappointment, frustration, and suspicion, amongst the northern tribes, in the years immediately following the signing of the Treaty of Waitangi. <i>Ibid.</i> 13 The "Flagstaff War" began on 8 July 1844 when Heke and his warriors ascended the hill above Kororareka and chopped down the flagstaff from which the Union Jack.. was flying. <i>(1984,Red Coat to Jungle Green,12</i>)

### flake
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Kapanga River Diggings,/ Coromandel harbour,/ 4th November, 1852. ... the annexed tabular report of the work at this Gold-field since my arrival. ... wherever I and the Messrs. Ring have `prospected' on the Kapanga ..., we have found gold, and that the greatest number of flakes in the prospecting dish, appeared upon washing some earth yesterday at a bar about half a mile lower down the stream ...; and below all the present diggings. <i>(Dec 10, 1852,NZ Government Gazette,V/30/183</i>) [NOTES: (Heaphy's Report) [sc. of gold]]

>  <b>SWAINSON</b> These parties also brought up from the same spot various other specimens; one parcel of which, consisting of large rich dust and flakes, and exceedingly auriferous quartz (perhaps as rich, gaudy specimens as have yet been seen), was sold by auction by the undersigned yesterday. <i>(Dec 21, 1853,Auckland,109</i>) [NOTES: 1852 Dec 21 Gold Circular in 1853 Swainson Auckland 109]

>  Though erosion had long buried the gold-bearing gravels, nuggets and flakes could be recovered from some stream beds where water had cut down to the original deposits. <i>(Apr 1991,NZ Geographic,106</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> gummy shark, school shark, flake perceived as fish-shop fillets

>  <b>SORENSON</b> SMOOTHOUND (a) Scientific name: <i>Mustelus antarcticus</i>. (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: Small Flake Fillets (restricted to this species); Smoothound. (c) Other common names: Kini; Pioke; Lemons; Lemonfish; Dogfish; Rig; Silver Strip (fillets); Sweet William; Flake. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,44</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> Of the many sharks found in New Zealand waters, the gummy and school sharks are the most commonly eaten. Both are relatively small in size, and are marketed as lemon fish or flake. <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,79</i>)

>  FLAKE FISH used in a fish-and-chip shop =? shark, dogfish <i>(1985,Personal Communication</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a person of questionable credibility

>  <b>HARGREAVES</b> They think I'm a flake because my name is Tangaroa Demant: I've got a Frog surname and a Maori first name. <i>(1998,On the Next Tide,57</i>)

>  <b>MAHY</b> 'Is he mad?' asked Tris, shivering and shaking. 'He's a real flake, said Winola. <i>(1992,Underrunners,145</i>)

### flake fillet
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SORENSON</b> SHARK, SCHOOL (a) Scientific name: <i>Galeorhinus australis</i> (principally). (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: Flake Fillets. (c) Other common names: B.B., Big Boys; Soupfin; Grey Boys; Tope or Topi. <i>Ibid.</i> 44 SMOOTHOUND (a) Scientific name: <i>Mustelus antarcticus</i>. (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: Small Flake Fillets (restricted to this species); Smoothound. (c) Other common names: Kini; Pioke; Lemons; Lemonfish; Dogfish; Rig; Silver Strip (fillets); Sweet William; Flake. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,42</i>)

### flakers
 
 <i>adj.</i> flaked out, unconscious; dead drunk

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> `Anybody seen Charlie?' `In the rubbity this arfto. He's probably flakers.' <i>(1978,Glory and the Dream, The,166</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> flakers drooping drunk and on the way out; eg `For Pete's sake don't buy Beech another round. Can't you see he's flakers?' <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,45</i>)

### flakey
 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i> to flake rope down [?from seamen's:]

>  <b>KITTO</b> To lay your headline on to the dredge, you should coil the [wire] rope ... into the boat. If ... it be too hard to lay in coils, you could `flakey' it down. This is done by simply letting it lie as it falls, and it will afterwards uncoil all right. <i>(1900,Practical Dredgeman's Manual,11</i>)

### flako
 
 <i>adj.</i> asleep

### flakoes
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  FLAKOES ... sugar ... butter ... cocoanut ... nuts ...egg ... cornflakes ... Bake. <i>(1936,Recipe Book [St Mary's],89</i>)

### flaky gold
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SWAINSON</b> The dust consisted of flaky gold, of a pale lemon colour, largely intermixed with auriferous quartz, the separation having been effected only by washing in the simplest manner. <i>(Dec 13, 1852,Auckland,106</i>) [NOTES: 1852 Dec 13 Gold Circular in 1853 Swainson Auckland 106]

>  <b>PFAFF</b> On looking ... shiny particles of flaky gold were revealed in unusually large quantities. <i>(1914,Diggers' Story, The,123</i>)

### flame vine
 
 <i>n.</i>

 <i>n.</i>

 <i>n.</i>

### flange
 
 <i>n.</i>

 <i>n.</i> [(freezing works)]

>  FLANKERS (Both sides) <i>(1975,Log of a Superfluous Son, The,42</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a dog which worries sheep's flanks

 <i>n.</i>

### flannel back
 
 <i>n.</i> cotted second shear fleece wool [(woolgrowers')]

>  Flannel Back. Cotted second shear fleece wool. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,55</i>)

### flannel flower
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  flannel flower n. perennial plant bearing daisy-like flowers with flannel-like bracts, Australian edelweiss. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),8</i>)

### flannel-leaf
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Verbascum thapsus</i>, Aaron's rod, golden rod, mullein, native tobacco, tobacco plant, velvet, wooly mullein (See [mullein](mob-mys.md#mullein))

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> FLANNEL LEAF (<i>Verbascum thapsus</i>) is commonly called mullein in England, and is frequently so called here as well. Its commonest local name is probably tobacco plant, or native tobacco, a name for which there is no justification in either appearance or use. Velvet mullein, woolly mullein, golden rod, and Aaron's rod are other appellations, but flannel leaf, one of the common sheep-station names is both descriptive and distinctive. The plant is found in dry soil and river beds ... in both islands. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,155,1967 ed.</i>)

>  VERBASCUM THAPSUS, a weed of poor land has many popular names. In New Zealand it is called native tobacco, velvet, flannel leaf and golden rod. <i>(Nov 22, 1974,Dominion,5</i>)

>  <b>ELL</b> The Woolly Mullein is often known in New Zealand as flannel-leaf for its leaves have a woolly feel to them. <i>(1983,Wildflowers and Weeds,14</i>)

### flannelweed
 
 <i>n.</i> bushman's friend

>  <b>LOCKLEY</b> Flannelweed, <i>Solanum mauritianum</i>, is the largest of the introduced nightshades ... growing very rapidly to a shrub two metres tall ... <i>(1980,House Above the Sea,199</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> flannel-leaf (mullein)

### flannery
 
 <i>n.</i> a policeman [(obs)]

>  A supporter ... who has been celebrating ... was now wending his uncertain way homewards, `it's our - hic - our - hic ...' `Oh is it?' grinned a tolerant Flannery who happened to be nigh <i>(Apr 29, 1925,NZ Tit-Bits,6</i>)

### flap
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  (Wallace Shearing Glossary) flap: This occurs when the angle of set on the blades is incorrect. Rather than cut the wool they `flap'. <i>(1989,NZ English Newsletter,3/24</i>)

### flap off
 
 <i>v.</i> to go away

>  <b>McGILL</b> flap off go away; eg `Why don't you flap off, eh?' [Jim Henderson] <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,45</i>)

### flapjack
 
 <i>n.</i> a fritter made of flour and water, a rough and ready pancake cf flapper n1, flipperjack (occas. used humorously for a pikelet) [ca1941-44 St Patrick's College, Silverstream Boys' use when improvising any mixture of flour and water, fried, and usually eaten spread with Golden syrup, sugar or jam. ca1940 Used humorously by old lady (70+) for any pikelet, fritter or pancake.]

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> am getting on better with my cooking. I will show you how to make `flap-jacks' and johnny-cakes' when I come home. <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,36,1935 ed.</i>) [NOTES: c1872 in c1875 Meredith Adventuring in Maoriland (1935) 36]

>  <b>BARKER</b> He should make some Flap-jacks. <i>(1872,Christmas Cake in Four Quarters</i>) [NOTES: 1872 Lady Barker Xmas Cake chIII]

>  <b>BARKER</b> There ain't not a drop of yeast about, anywhere, mum. - This entailed flap-jacks or scones or soda-bread or some indigestible compound for at least three days. <i>(1873,Station Amusements in NZ,124</i>)

>  <b>HARPER</b> [comic bush menu] ROTI Roast Weka ENTREMETS Flap-jack and Jam. <i>(1896,Pioneer Work in the Alps of NZ,196</i>)

>  <b>PFAFF</b> We made some flapjacks, [116] and fried the bacon, which frothed most beautifully in the pan. <i>(1914,Diggers' Story, The,115</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> Meantime ... Stuart ... was preparing the immediate luxury of flapjacks, mixing flour and yeast for the morrow's bread, ... <i>(1926,Tutira,151</i>)

>  <b>KENWAY</b> flapjack and a doughboy (dumpling) ... <i>(1928,Pioneering in Poverty Bay,7</i>)

>  Flapjack: A fritter made of flour, water and sugar, fried. A great dish in camp. (C.) <i>(Oct 14, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  Flap Jacks. Three tablespoons oatmeal, 1 tablespoon flour, salt, small teaspoon baking soda. Mix dry ingredients, then moisten to a batter with buttermilk. Let stand for ten minutes in order to swell. Fry in bacon fat and serve round bacon for breakfast. <i>(1936,Cookery Book of NZ Women's Institutes,105</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> After the bread ran out [in the musterers' camp] we would have flap-jacks. Firewood was ... picked up by the packman as he went along. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,121,1954 ed.</i>)

>  <b>LEE</b> ... [swaggers'] jugs of tea and hot flapjacks. <i>(1944,Shining with the Shiner,138</i>)

>  <b>HARPER</b> To make a flapjack ... <i>(1945,Camping and Bushcraft in NZ</i>)

>  1. a compact and puff for face powder. 2. a thick pancake. [few have heard of this outside Auckland: a local nonce or perhaps family unsage. <(?) nonce on pancake make-up ???] <i>(1979,Heinemann Dictionary of NZ Quotations,410</i>)

>  <b>GILDERDALE</b> flapjack Oatmeal biscuits in England, but first mentioned in [241] Lady Barker's Boys (1874) page 33 as `cakes of dripping and flour fried in a pan', and frequently used in that context in other books. <i>(1982,Sea Change,240</i>) [NOTES: (Note on Language)]



 
 <i>n.</i> a seaweed

>  <b>MARTIN</b> A little lower down where the seaweeds are permanently submerged we have such plants as the GRAPELET, the WIRE- WEED, and OX-TONGUE; the FLAP-JACK and GIRDLE WEEDS. In rather deeper water ... we have the (<i>Lessonia</i>) and the OARWEED, the GRAPEWEED, and numerous representatives of the red algae. <i>Ibid./</i> Flora 14 The commonest of the four species is (<i>C[arpophyllum] maschalocarpum</i>) which has been designated by the apt and appropriate name of FLAP-JACK from the habit of the plant in swaying first in one direction and then the other as the water enters or recedes from the surge channels between tidal rocks. <i>(1961,Flora NZ,5,1929 ed.</i>)

### flapper
 
 <i>n.</i> flapjack [(obs goldminers')]

>  <b>WALKER</b> You ask if we had pancakes on Shrove Tuesday [on the Molyneux diggings]. I do not remember what we had on that day, but if there was no meat to be had, I think it very likely what are called here flappers, that is flour and water mixed up thin and fried. in fat. They are very good with sugar. <i>(May 4, 1863,Journal and Letters (Walker)</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> the gen. English (possibly dialectal in Britain) sense `fledgling wild duckling' (1773) used in NZ since 1856; used also in the Chatham Islands ca1972 of fledgling albatross; also of fledgling swan

>  <b>MASTERS</b> On our return trip up the lake with Jack and Brian and their dogs in the boat, we followed up a flapper swan. <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,285</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a young girl.

>  FLAPPER. - A young girl. <i>(1917,New Zealand At the Front,xiii</i>)

### flapping
 
 <i>n.</i> A form of racing which is not subject to Jockey Club or National Hunt Committee regulations.

>  Small [race-] meetings are plentiful, where the crumbs to be picked up by `flapping' are anything but despicable. <i>(1877,Country Journal,1/5</i>)

### flaps
 
 <i>n.</i> tradename for skate wings [(usu. pl)]

>  <b>SORENSON</b> <i>SKATE</i> (a) Scientific name: <i>Raja nasuta.</i> (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: Skate. (c) Other common names: Smooth Skate (<i>R. nasuta</i>); Rough Skate (<i>Raja australis</i>); Stingray; Stingaree; Flaps (trade name for Skate `wings'). (d) Principal localities where taken: Napier, Wellington, Nelson, Picton, Banks Peninsula, Timaru, Greymouth. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,43</i>)

### flash
 
 <i>adj.</i> ostentatious, showy, sensational (US); used frequently in NZ coll in various senses most of which correspond to equivalent senses in gen. English, though many NZ uses have derog. overtones a. (i) =British posh: of appearance, dress: modish; smart; ostentatious, showy (NZ 1874: OED 1784); of an hotel: fashionable; expensive; smart (NZ 1930: OED 1840) also of people, `smart'; of meals `well presented, expensive') [From N.Z. English to U.S. English Dictionary at http://nz.com/NZ/Culture/NZDic.html]

>  Yes, it's pretty flash, but a flashness that will, along with its staff, have you all at ease. <i>(Apr 10, 2004,Dominion Post,E 6</i>)

>  Things are not looking too flash at all. The minister is very concerned. <i>(Oct 9, 1998,Dominion,2</i>)

>  The flash gear was deceptive though. <i>(Jun 1996,Mana,No.13: 63</i>)

>  "They got very flash language, those Land Court fellows." <i>(Jun 1960,Mate,00</i>) [NOTES: check pagination]

>  We know it wasn't a flash game and that we made a lot of mistakes. <i>(Mar 11, 2001,Sunday Star Times,B3</i>)

>  the flash new models / have given up the ghost / lacking the guts to make it <i>(May 12, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),63</i>)

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> [Miners' expressions include] ... `flash', an adjective, differing in meaning a little according to what it is applied to, but which may be interpreted generally by stylish. <i>(1874,Colonial Experiences,98</i>)

>  <b>THATCHER</b> And then she came out very flash, Like winking spent her husband's cash; <i>(1875,NZ Songster,3/21</i>) [NOTES: John Chinaman's Marriage]

>  `I know someone,' said Archie thoughtfully, `that knew her in Onehunga, and he says she's got a bloke in Auckland; with a lot of money, a flash sort of bloke.' <i>(Jul 10, 1915,Triad, The,415</i>)

>  <b>ANZAC</b> The Turkish ladies were as flash as they make them. <i>(1916,On the Anzac Trail,55</i>)

>  <b>DEVANNY</b> You will stop at a flash hotel. <i>(1930,Bushman Burke,22</i>)

>  <b>HASTINGS</b> Can you imagine what the dame did? She had the nerve to order me a flash dinner at that hotel ... <i>(1940,Digger's Diary,6</i>)

>  <b>BENNETT</b> A showy appearance is summed up as flash, as it was in Victorian England. <i>(1943,English as it is spoken in NZ</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> Bill was a bit of a dude in those days. Among things he had taken with him, with [sic] a flash walking cane. <i>(1959,Tales of the Mails,153</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> A few weeks later a couple of flash visitors called. <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,158</i>)

>  flash adj. vulgar, ostentatious, showy, swaggering. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),8</i>)

>  <b>MANTELL</b> Some tittered a little, arched eyebrows at the identities of some of the people who drove past in their `flash' car. <i>(1984,Murder in Vain,74</i>)

>  Diggers in every variety of costume might be seen; here the flash man with the scarlet shirt, white moleskin trousers ... <i>(Dec 16, 1861,Lyttelton Times</i>) [NOTES: 1861 Dec 16 Letter by CMO to Lyttelton Times in 1962 Jan 18 Otago Witness (Griffiths Collection)]

>  <b>ANTHONY</b> Mr Treadwell said it was all very well, me showing off my flash house to a few silly women, but he bet I didn't bother to point out that it was situated in a hollow, and that the borer was in the lining. <i>(1936,Follow the Call,104</i>)

>  I dreamt I saw.. A soldier on a base job who wasn't very flash. <i>(Aug 2, 1918,Chronicles NZEF,V/49 : 15</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> [[?any spec. sense here or merely extended use of a. (i)]]

>  I backed [c. got on the back of] the little flash horse again and was going on at a careless canter when he stoped, ... and gave 3 bucks ... <i>(Feb 23, 1863,Chudleigh Diary,79,1950 ed.</i>)

>  I returned to Hardecastle and he very kindly has put a flash little mare of his own at my disposal. <i>(Jun 4, 1863,Chudleigh Diary,90,1950 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> `fly', experienced

>  <b>SARGESON</b> `Cowpats' I'd come back from town a bit too flash for a trick like that. <i>(1940,Man & His Wife, A,30</i>)

>  <b>STRONACH</b> ... it was a common expression among the `flash' shearers of years ago, when wanting tar for a cut sheep, to say, `Tar, boy, and step back. I hate the smell of a rouseabout'. <i>(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,31</i>)

 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>MONEY</b> In the same tent with me was ... Tom Holland ... whom I had known when flashing about in Auckland; ... <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,130</i>)

### flash in the socket
 
 <i>phr.</i> (?from mining sense of socket: the hollow in which an unexploded charge lies)

>  Ultraprovincialism may flash in the socket but has not the power to dismember the Colony. <i>(Apr 11, 1858,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/383,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (J.C. Richmond)]

### flash note
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HARGREAVES</b> The [Governor Fitzroy's (1844)] Debentures, popularly known as `government rags', were given various nicknames such as assignats, `shinplasters', and `flash notes'. All these terms had reference to worthless paper money, some of which had been issued in the past. Assignats was the name given to large amounts of paper money issued in France during the French Revolution, but which depreciated to such as extent to be almost worthless. `Shinplasters'--sometimes written `skinplasters'--had its origin in North America, and again referred to depreciated paper money, while `flash notes' was a popular term in use for any counterfeit money or worthless forged paper money. <i>(1972,From Beads to Banknotes,40</i>)

### flash off
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> ... the boar flashed off, running `level' as a wild pig does, down the far slope. <i>(1955,By Reef and Range,7</i>)

 <i>phr.</i>

 <i>n.</i>

>  The finishing touch is to have the very fancy flasher rigs on the end of your line which we are told the fish simply can't resist. <i>(Apr 19, 2005,Independent (Auckland),17</i>)

 <i>adj.</i> very best

### flashing
 
 <i>n.</i> occas. wrongly (or vaguely) applied in NZ to roof-guttering; rather than to the standard sheet lead or iron, etc. used to make a waterproof- joint between, for example, a chimney and a roof

>  <b>SALMOND</b> Flashing. folded metal strips fixed around junctions in roof coverings to prevent entry of water; also around openings in walls for the same purpose. <i>(1986,Glossary of Building Terminology,232</i>)

>  Do they call it a wetback because the water runs down the inside of the airing cupboard?" our son asked the plumber who came to do things to the flashing. We wouldn't let him ask why they called it flashing. <i>(Jul 22, 1989,Listener (New Zealand Listener),32</i>)

 <i>n.</i> [[cf AND flashjack 1899, flashjane 1932; nf W3] a good-looking girl]

### flashpacker
 
 <i>n.</i> an upmarket backpacker

>  Instead, there is a new breed [of backpacker] with names such as "flashpackers", or experiential travellers. Older, more educated, armed with credit cards and unafraid to use them, they are an increasingly valuable part of New Zealand tourism. <i>(Nov 8, 2003,Dominion Post,A5</i>)

>  [Hdg] A new breed of tourist, called flashbackers, are finding exactly what they need at Wellington's Cambridge Hotel... At the economy end of the market Cambridge Hotel, in Cambridge Tce, offers a service for a new kind of traveller, the "flashbacker"... Typically, they're in their 30s or 30s, they want a nice, quiet and safe environment, and that's what we offer here"... <i>(Oct 27, 2004,Dominion Post Entertain Wellington,10</i>)

>  But then.. backpackers have gone upmarket and could be called flashpackers. <i>(Jan 13, 2004,Dominion Post,A 10</i>)

 <i>n.</i> an upmarket backpackers [-] (See [flashpacker](fin-fly.md#flashpacker))

### flat
 
 <i>n.</i> (usually plural) any area of level land, usually low-lying near water: `river flats'. -->[AND [spec. use of flat a piece of level ground] esp alluvial formation; and esp. as a Source of alluvial gold: see also river flat (river 2.) 1799 damp flats b. with a distinguishing epithet e.g. an appetree flat] note collocation with level [The OED Australian goldfields sense c. (see below) is unattested in New Zealand, except perhaps in place-names on goldfields.]

>  <b>McNAB</b> [sc. land] In the interior of the country and along the east coast ... there are extensive flats of rich alluvial land, and clear of timber. <i>(1908,Historical Records,1/535</i>) [NOTES: 1821 May Evidence of Ensign McCrae before Commissioner Bigge in 1908 McNab Historical Records 1/535]

>  <b>CHAPMAN</b> flats at the foot of the mountains [written by an emigrant at Cape of Good Hope]. <i>(1839,Letters,13</i>) [NOTES: 1839 Chapman Letters [TS] (T.M. Partridge) 13 ...]

>  <b>S & J MATHEW</b> We traversed a rather extensive flat where the natives had been digging up fern root. <i>(1840,Journal,89</i>)

>  <b>F MATHEW</b> There is a very beautiful flat, known as Thorndon Flat, having an area of probably 50 acres, forming the northern portion of the town. <i>(Oct 20, 1841,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1841 Oct 20 F.Mathew to Hobson in GBPP 1842 (No.569) 184]

>  <b>HEAPHY</b> The town is chiefly built on what are called the `Lambton' and `Thorndon Flats', which are two pieces of flat or gently sloping ground, extending from the hills at the back of the town to the beach. Each of these flats is about a mile square, ... <i>(1842,New Zealand (Heaphy),70</i>)

>  ... crosses over the toi toi flat [? =swamp] <i>(1843,NZ Journal,96/216</i>) [NOTES: (C.H. Kettle, Surveyor)]

>  <b>SELWYN</b> ... flax flats. <i>(1843,Journal,16</i>)

>  <b>DEANS</b> ... there are many miles of first rate soil in different places on the flat. <i>(Jan 10, 1844,Pioneers of Canterbury,79,1937 ed.</i>)

>  At the head of the bay is a flat of about 100 acres of very rich-looking soil; ... <i>(Jul 20, 1844,Nelson Examiner</i>) [NOTES: -1844 Monro Notes of a Journey (Nelson Examiner July 20 et seq.) in 1898 Hocken Contributions 234]

>  <b>PHARAZYN</b> Commenced burning on the flat on this side the creek up the Valley. <i>(1846,Journal,27</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> Between the bay and the valley at the back, spreads a flat, which contains between 200 and 300 acres of land ... <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,229</i>) [NOTES: [quoting 1839 Capt. Chaffers's Report]]

>  <b>BUTLER</b> We burnt the flats as we rode down, and made a smoke which was noticed fifty or sixty miles off. <i>(1863,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,iv/60</i>) [NOTES: 1860 Mar 24 in 1863 Butler First Year iv/60]

>  <b>HAAST</b> ... we again started ... swagging part of the provisions ...down the Buller, and camped six miles lower down on the flat. <i>(1861,Report on Topographical Exploration of Nelson,16</i>)

>  <b>BARRINGTON</b> Reached the head of the flat; got no gold; ... <i>(Dec 26, 1863,Diary of a West Coast Prospecting Party</i>) [NOTES: 1863 Dec 26 Barrington Diary of a West Coast Prospecting Party in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 394]

>  <b>BARKER</b> ... first it was necessary to build on a flat (as the valleys here are called), not too far off the main track, on account of having to make the road to it ourselves; ... and a good creek, or brook, which would not go dry in the summer, close at hand. <i>Ibid.</i> 102 ... an announcement `a horseman coming up the flat.' <i>Ibid </i> 122 ... between and among these hills stretchs immense valleys (or `flats' as they are called here) <i>(1866,Station Life in NZ,63,1874 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SMALL</b> Men working with a will/ On each gully, flat and hill; ... <i>(1866,NZ & Australian Songster,12,1970 ed.</i>)

>  <b>LINDSAY</b> Throughout the Province are to be found numerous remains of former extensive lakes, estuaries, and rivers in the form of `flats' and `terraces' of clay, loam, grass, or sand ... <i>(1868,Contributions to NZ Botany,31</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> Their best `flats' or vallies. <i>(1873,Station Amusements in NZ,90</i>)

>  <b>PYKE</b> He moved away up the plain, or `flat.' <i>Ibid.</i> (1889,1974) 3/iii/82 This completed, he ... moved away up the plain, or `flat'. <i>(1873,Wild Will Enderby,114</i>)

>  <b>BAINES</b> At this place there is a `flat', a mile or so wide, and then begins a range of hills ... <i>(1874,Edward Crewe,80</i>)

>  <b>CAIRD</b> The words `terrace', and `flat', so often used, rather aptly to describe the general nature of the country. A flat means a plain, but it is a rare thing to gallop along a flat for more than a mile without coming across a terrace, which terrace may be only 6feet, high or it may be a hundred ... [and] is certain to be as regular as if it formed part of some ornamental ground. <i>(1874,NZ Sheepfarming,26</i>) [NOTES: (Canterbury/Otago)]

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> ... I selected a tent-fly (a twelve-foot square sheet of calico), rolled it up, threw it over my shoulder, and proceeded to a bare glade on the Kohiwai flat. This flat is heavily covered with flax and toi-toi bushes. <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,62,1935 ed.</i>) [NOTES: [sc. land]]

>  <b>THATCHER</b> Held sales in the township, on the flat, too, as well, sirs, And seemed to appearances cutting a swell, sirs. <i>(1875,NZ Songster,5/50</i>) [NOTES: Life of an Auctioneer]

>  39. <i>Glaucopis cinerea</i>, Gml. Wattlebird or Crow, Kokako.- I first became acquainted with this bird on the flats of the Wilberforce river, west of the Rakaia gorge, on 20th April, 1856; it was abundant amongst the isolated beech trees ... <i>(Mar 1885,NZ Journal of Science,376</i>) [NOTES: (T.H. Potts)]

>  <b>HOCKEN</b> [cf also level here] Trending N. and N.E., a widespread level lies below, covered with flax and cabbage trees, toe-toe grass, and Maori-heads, intersected by sinuous, sluggish watercourses. Into this flat, the matau-kareao, descends a great semicircle of forest-clad spurs, ... <i>(1898,Contributions to the Early History of New Zealand,79</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> We cantered over the fern and manuka-clothed pumice flats ... <i>(1916,Bush Explorers,4</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> The name is apt enough, for the decayed little kainga sits near the tip of a long flat stretching out southward from the peninsula; ... <i>(1926,Travel in NZ,(Vol II South Island) 23</i>)

>  <b>LEVY</b> Lacebark, wineberry, konini, mahoe, and water ferns characterised the secondary growth in the gullies and on the flats. <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,16,1970 ed.</i>)

>  [trampers'] Midday boil-up was at the end of the first long flat, after which our route lay over river boulders to a small flat ... <i>(Sep 1958,Tararua,48</i>)

>  Flat. An area of level ground, usually near a river. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,5</i>)

>  flat ... NZ & Austral. area of flat land. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,285</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <b>MIDDLETON</b> Around and beneath us scrub-filled gulleys fell away to green flats ... <i>(1959,Stone, The,56</i>)

>  What should have been grassy flats in the main valley.. were covered to the exclusion of everything else for acres at a time with dense cushions of the common cotton-plant, Celmisia spectabilis, the leaves dark glossy green above and very woolly beneath. <i>(1933,Canterbury Mountaineer, The,II/43</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (often as flat with) to live in a flat, share accommodation (with).

>  Anyone who has flatted will recognised the scene in which the central character, Shifter, makes cups of tea only to find the last of the milk has been used up. <i>(Jul 19, 2000,Capital Times,4</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> flatfish [ca1944 fishermen's and fishery jargon for flat-fish.]

>  flat ... 54. <i>(1981,Macquarie Dictionary,679</i>)

>  <b>BROWN</b> `No flats' ... describing the goggle-eyed, square riggered flounders ... and soles. <i>(1939,Farmer's Wife, The,222</i>)

>  [species name] flats .. [scientific name] -- [other name(s)] if not specified. MIXED FLATS <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a tract of low lying, marshy land; a swamp 1610 [(New Zealand 1862) but probably mainly here as a MUDFLAT as in:]

>  [? Morris] ... the part joining the land is a flat about 1/4 of a mile broad. <i>(1852,Cook's Journals,1</i>) [NOTES: 1852 J. Cook Journal on Macquarie Island 1]

>  Each of these swamps, or flats, is in a kind of amphitheatre on a grand scale. <i>(1862,Otago: Its Goldfields and Resources,21</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SMYTH</b> Flat: a low, even tract land, generally occurring where creeks unite, over which are spread many strata of sand and gravel, with the usual rich auriferous drift mined overlying the bed-rock. <i>(1869,Goldfields Victoria,611</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [? is this merely the flat land above the beach; or specific to Piraki; or equiv. to modern `hard']

>  This day the Whaling Season ends, when the Boats were clear'd out, wash'd, oil'd, and taken on the Flat; likewise the Whaling Gear put up, Falls coiled away, &c. <i>(Oct 1, 1839,Piraki Log,99</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [[should this go with n1 b. above as extension of swamp]]

>  <b>SARAH MATHEW</b> We walked round the little valley, ... the hills rising ... at the back, covered with thick fern brushwood, and shut in on one side by a mangrove flat covered with water at high tide, ... <i>(Apr 2, 1840,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1840 Apr 2 SARAH MATHEW Journal in 1940 Founding of NZ 103]



 
 <i>n.</i> a (gullible) fool; sucker; dupe, John [(obs by WW1)]

>  <b>THATCHER</b> First get the flats in,/ Then get hold of their tin,/ Is their motto when there's a Bazaar./ <i>(1864,Songs of the War</i>) [NOTES: 1864 Thatcher Songs of the War in 1967 Bailey and Roth Shanties 19]

>  <b>THATCHER</b> And sharebrokers flourish, and lots of tin gain,/ From flats who're afflicted with `quartz on the brain' [from the new Thames goldfield]. <i>(1869,Wit and Humour: Local Songs...</i>) [NOTES: (Beautiful Auckland) [a dupe]]

>  Will you walk into my parlour/ Said the damsel to the flat. <i>(Mar 24, 1906,NZ Truth,40/5/8</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a level area of land or plain, often across several paddocks.

>  <b>WRIGHT</b> Over the tussocky flat, and down ... <i>(1897,Station Ballads,14</i>)

>  <b>MCCONOCHIE</b> After we had walked some miles, a wapiti bull bugled some distance up the hill opposite a ribbonwood flat on which were a number of cows. <i>(1966,You'll Learn No Harm from the Hills,134</i>)

>  <b>REA & FERGUSON</b> There was a big flat of bush down below. <i>(1993,Home and Away,57</i>)

>  <b>CAIRD</b> The words 'terrace' and 'flat,'so often used, rather aptly describe the general nature of the country. A flat means a plain ... <i>(1874,Notes on Sheepfarming in NZ,26</i>)

>  <b>HAMILTON</b> John and Duncan McLean arrived in 1883-84 and took up the flat which at that time was a bog full of niggerheads. <i>(1952,History of Northern Southland,70</i>)

>  <b>TAIT</b> The ewes were moved down from the hills to the flat. Thirty acres of swampy flat, only recently developed from niggerheads ... <i>(1956,Farms & Stations of NZ Vol I,103</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> He flew an Auster plane in and landed on a tussock flat not far from the hut. <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,20</i>)

>  Most of the flat is in tussock or low manoao scrub before development. <i>(Mar 9, 1967,NZ Farmer,3</i>)

>  <b>PORTER</b> the children rushed down to the flat for a ride on the horses. <i>(1970,Under the Nor'West Arch,29</i>)

>  <b>ASPINALL</b> .. but the boys take some [cattle] off the homestead flat, up through the bush and on to the Cattle Face where the cattle spend the winter. <i>(1993,Farming Under Aspiring,163</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  The soil is chiefly clay or light loam, with alluvial flats in the valleys, and all well watered. <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,3 ed.</i>)

>  <b>LEE</b> Here and there beside the rivers were rich, alluvial flats and plains of country, easily broken by plough, ... <i>(1934,Children of the Poor,11</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SCRYMGEOUR</b> `Te Rapa' was not of an investment purpose, gorse in golden bloom spread its noxious mantle across some beautiful river flats, many fences were railed for the Poverty Bay Hunt Club, who often held Drag runs, and this sporting process of `Hounds' with the etceteras that follow the vagaries of Hunt Clubs, soon decided against purchasing. <i>(1960,Memories of Maoriland,157</i>)

### flat as a tack
 
 <i>phr.</i> deflated, lacklustre

>  "He was as flat as a tack at Wanganui and you don't take on top Australian company after that..." <i>(Sep 5, 2003,Dominion Post RaceForm,2</i>)

>  <b>SYDER & HODGET</b> Hard work. Under pressure. .in 4;`I'm flat as a tack, but don't worry - hard work never hurt anyone.' <i>(1962,Australian & NZ English [TS],354,1974 ed.</i>)

### flat out like a lizard
 
 <i>phr.</i> an extension of flat out (P: ca1910) [ca1952 Wellington (R. Mason) Flat out like a lizard, heard in Wellington]

>  <b>SYDER & HODGETTS</b> Making a sustained effort in maintaining progress in work routine, or social commitments. .in 4;`I've been extra busy, this last day or two, I had to shift some young stock into another paddock; I found a couple of loose wires, and had to fix that. I had to go to town to pick up a part they were welding for me. I've been flat out like a lizard drinking.' <i>(1962,Australian & NZ English [TS],356</i>) [NOTES: ? Australian]

### flat pack
 
 <i>n.</i> a frameless pack; =kidney-rotter [(trampers')]

>  <b>R. MASON</b> Flat Pack: ca1942 I have only heard this term used by one or two trampers from Otago. Wellington trampers, so far as I have observed, used the term `frameless' pack' if they don't say `kidney rotter'. <i>(Jun 7, 1952,Letter</i>)

>  The two main varieties of packs are the <i>frame pack</i> and the <i>frameless pack,</i> sometimes called a <i>flat pack</i>. The frameless pack of course receives that name only on formal occasions, being more familiarly known by that elegant term,<i> kidney-rotter</i>, or less frequently as <i>kidney-buster, kidney-crusher, kidney-sweater or kidney-rider. </i> <i>(Sep 1958,Tararua,27</i>) [NOTES: Ruth Mason `Track and Trail']

### flat paddock
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an area of level fenced and farmed land.

>  Mares in foal should be put on flat paddocks with no drains or creeks, and looked at morning and evening. <i>(Apr 20, 1922,NZ Journal of Agriculture,221</i>)

### flat spin
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Flat-spin: Exercise 13A in the new PT syllabus. A condition commonly engendered by winning a snowball, receiving a hearty slap on the back from the sergeant, or a week's special leave without asking for it. <i>(Apr 20, 1942,NZEF Times,II/43 : 00</i>)

### flat stack
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <b>McGILL</b> flat stack as fast as possible; eg `Some of the older people on talkback radio really talk flat stack.' May be a combination of `flat strap' and `flat tack', with `flat stick' thrown in for good measure. [B. and M. Shephard] <i>(1989,Dinkum Kiwi Dictionary, The,30</i>)

### flat stick
 
 <i>phr.</i> flat out, foot to the floorboards [(? f. Air Force) 1986 a U.S. food-writer's written remarks: p.c.Tui Flower NZ Woman's Weekly (14 Jan 1987) Shearers stop for a smoko (coffee break), then work flat stick (at maximum effort). tdeverson]

>  Farmers were putting money into building projects. "We've been flat stick since October last year," he said. <i>(Aug 30, 2001,Taranaki Daily News,18</i>)

>  `We are flat stick on Friday night and I refuse to turn this business away' he said. <i>(Aug 20, 1972,Evening Post</i>) [NOTES: 1972 Aug ? 20-25 Evening Post [p?]]

>  <b>McGILL</b> flat stick as fast as the vehicle will travel; eg `He was going flat stick, but the cop was gaining on him.' <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,45</i>)

### flat tack
 
 <i>phr.</i> flat out

>  [Maori mechanic says in answer to complaint] `No time to do that.' `Flat tack, eh.' <i>(Oct 8, 1986,TV1</i>) [NOTES: Open House TV1 play]

>  Opera singer Wendy Robinson is flat tack. And she's going to get busier during the next six months. <i>(Mar 24, 1999,Dominion,3</i>)

>  All the cabs were flat tack and their phone lines jammed. <i>(Mar 5, 2000,Sunday Star Times,B2</i>)

>  "The world is going flat tack ahead, while I'm desperately heading full steam in the opposite direction." <i>(Mar 9, 2002,Dominion,13</i>)

>  <b>McGill</b> Bert said he was flat tack serving beer and she'd better return as soon as she could. <i>(1995,Gold in the Creek,178</i>)

### flat tap
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> A gang ... threw a bottle at the hotrod so we screwed flat tap into town to round up the boys. <i>(1971,Bullshit and Jellybeans,34</i>)

### flat to the boards
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <b>SLATTER</b> I work flat to the boards, never even cough till ten, slogging me guts out to buy luxuries for the wife and she goes butchers because me home-brew stained her posh water set. <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,150</i>)

>  see Adam's Ale <i>(Jun 29, 1976,Evening Post,14/4</i>)



 
 <i>phr.</i> (Rural) [shearing] working at a fast rate.

>  <b>MILLS</b> At a shed of some sixteen stands shearing 38,000 sheep, a long spell of fine weather had kept everyone 'flat to the boards.' <i>(1960,Sheep-O,221</i>)

### flat white
 
 <i>n.</i> [see also 2005 cit. at long black]

>  When Sir Edmund Hillary reached the top of Mt Everest, it's a wonder he didn't find a bunch of Ministry of Commerce staff up there enjoying an flat white. <i>(Feb 2, 2000,Evening Post,18</i>)

>  Flat white: this term has two meanings in New Zealand. Sometimes it means a caffe latte served in a cup rather than a bowl or glass, and sometimes it is a latte made with two-thirds coffee to one-third milk. <i>(Jan 22, 1994,Listener (New Zealand Listener),57</i>)

>  A flat white is my choice. <i>(Jan 4, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),60</i>)

>  When I walked into the Espresso Republic at eleven in the morning, there was a throng of people chugging down lattes, flat whites and espressos. <i>(Apr 24, 2002,Capital Times,20</i>)

>  <b>Ihimaera</b> 'And I'll have -' 'A flat white?' I laughed. 'Yes,' she nodded. 'A dead Pakeha.' <i>(2000,Uncle's Story, The,133</i>)

>  Even before they start on their flat whites in an Auckland coffee-shrine, Burte whips out his mobile phone to confirm a flight to Wellington. <i>(Jun 7, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener),20</i>)

 <i>adj.</i>
