### flatfish
 
 <i>n.</i> any of a group of side-swimming fish, such as the flounder, having a flattened body and both eyes on one side of its head.

>  see sole <i>(Mar 1770,Cook's Journals,2/8</i>) [NOTES: [nf OED,W3]]

>  <b>BELL</b> We never caught any Flat Fish - and the only Shellfish besides small Musells and Limpets were Cray-fish - - <i>(1791,Journal of the 'Chatham',51</i>) [NOTES: 1791 Nov E.Bell Journal of Voyage in H.M.S. `Chatham' (Vancouver expedition) (MS ATB) 51]

>  flatfish - battik [patiki] <i>(1835,Boultbee Journal,110,1986 ed.</i>) [NOTES: c1826-1827 list of Maori words in c1835 Boultbee Journal (1986) 110]

>  <b>BEGG</b> (|= macron precedes) flat fish, battik [patiki] <i>(1979,Boultbee Journal,282</i>) [NOTES: 1826-28 Boultbee Vocabulary in 1979 Begg 282]

>  <i>(Jul 20, 1844,Nelson Examiner</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Monro Notes of a Journey (Nelson Examiner July 20 et seq.) in 1898 Hocken Contributions 267]

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> The habouka is taken [in Otago] ...; flat fish and oysters in all the bays. <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,303</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> The Rhombus [sic] Plebeus, (patiki,) or flat fish, is speared by torch-light. <i>(1855,Te Ika a Maui,624</i>)

>  ... where in two hours 48 dozen of flat-fish, brill, and other good fish were trawled. <i>(1901,TrNZI,33/574</i>)

>  <b>DRUMMOND & HUTTON</b> The warehou, tarakihi, and gurnet are common in the north, and the moki, butterfish, and frost fish in the south, while the kahawai, hapuku (the groper of Dunedin), blue cod, barracouta, the little sea mullet, sometimes called the herring, and the flat fish are found all round the coast. - <i>(1902,Nature in NZ,71</i>)

>  <b>61/142</b> ...practically no trawling has been done at the Chathams, so our knowledge of the flat-fishes is rather meagre. <i>(1929,TrNZI</i>) [NOTES: (Young)]

>  flat fish: edible fish. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,173</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> NEW ZEALAND LEMON SOLE <i>Peltotretis flavilatus</i> Waite. The Lemon Sole, like the Common Sole, Flounder, Brill and Turbot is a bottom-dwelling fish with both eyes on the upper surface of the body, known as the right side. This fish and the others mentioned are known to fishermen, auctioneers, retailers and the public generally as Flatfish. Skate and Electric-ray are also Flatfish, the upper and lower surfaces being the back and belly. <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,210,2nd ed.</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> FLATFISH ... Inhabitants of sandy or muddy, shallow flats the flatfish are also occasionally taken in deep water. This is specially so of the fairly rare Turbot which grows to a considerable size and inhabits the deeper, southern waters. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,71</i>)

>  I've always called them `flatfish.' But I saw a big poster about NZ fish which declared they were called sole and never mentioned flatfish at all. <i>(Jun 30, 1986,NZ Woman's Weekly,45</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] flatfish 2278 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

### flathead
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Cheilodonichthyes</i> sp. =gurnard (cf flathead gurnard, see gurnard)

>  <b>SORENSON</b> GURNARD (a) Scientific name: <i>Cheilodonichthyes kumu</i>. (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: N.Z. Latchett, N.Z. Latchett Fillets; Gurnard. (c) Other common names: Flathead, Shoal or Latchett or N.Z. F.F. for the prepared state of frozen fillets for export. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,27</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> = Maori chief

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 39. NOTOTHENIA CORIICEPS. Rich.? Flat-head. <i>(1872,Fishes NZ,26</i>)

>  <b>SHERRIN</b> <i>Notothenia coriiceps</i> Rich. Flat-head, or Maori Chief <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,301</i>)

>  <i>flathead n.</i> common Australian edible fish, with flattened heads and bodies. See Maori-chief. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. (Aust.-NZ Suppl.),616</i>)

>  <i>flathead n.</i> common Australian edible fish with flattened head and body. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),8</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Geniagnus</i> sp. =spotted stargazer

>  <b>MORELAND</b> SPOTTED STARGAZER [<i>Geniagnus monopterygius</i>] ... Other names include monkfish, catfish, dogfish, and flathead. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,20</i>)

>  <b>SORENSON</b> MONKFISH (a) Scientific name: (i) <i>Geniagnus monopterygius</i> (ii) <i>Kathetostoma giganteum</i>. (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: (both) Whitefish. (c) Other common names: (both) Monkfish, Monks, Stargazer, Flathead (also applied at times to export gurnard); Churchills, Toebiters, Bulldogs, Spotted Stargazer, Maori Chief (Dunedin); Muddies. (d) Principal localities where taken: Napier, Cook Strait, Nelson, Banks Peninsula, Bluff-Stewart Island. ... Monkfish is a general term for species of the Families Uranoscopidae and Leptoscopidiae. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,37</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> any of various fish with a flattened head (See [also flathead gurnard](fin-fly.md#flathead-gurnard))

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Flathead</i> ... name given to several Australian marine fishes, <i>Platycephalus fuscus</i>, Cuv. and Val., and other species of <i>Platycephalus</i>, family Cottida. The Red Flathead is </i> P. bassensis</i>, Cuv. and Val., and the Rock F. is<i> P. lavig?stus</i>, Cuv. and Val. see also <i>Tupong and Maori-chief</i>.] <i>(1898,Austral English,147</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] flathead 1822, 1828, 1835, 1854 FLATHEADS 1227-9, 1782-95 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

>  There is a flathead with grey and black and white spots, and with pale blue edges to his fins and tail. <i>(Jun 15, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,14</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> (in full flathead nail) a nail with a wide flat (rather than a rose-shaped, or cylindrical (brad) head [ca1920]

>  flathead ... 2 N.Z. a flat-headed nail. <i>(1981,Macquarie Dictionary,679</i>)

>  That [the bach] strapped down a bit of paradise with corrugated iron and lead-head nails, fibrolite and flat-heads; that it achieved its redemption through ugliness. <i>(Apr 26, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),51</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Kathetostoma</i> sp.

>  <i>Kathetostoma giganteum</i> Haast. Anderton records one specimen of this fish - the `flathead' - as taken about eight miles from Taiaroa Head ... <i>(1913,TrNZI,45/229</i>) [NOTES: (Thomson, Otago)]

>  <i>KATHETOSTOMA GIGANTEUM</i> Haast. Flathead. Common, North Auckland Peninsula; but seldom utilized as a food fish. Occasionally seen in Christchurch markets. Distribution: New Zealand coasts (rarer in the extreme south). <i>(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,IV/123</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps Notes on the Edible Fishes]

>  <i>Kathetostoma giganteum</i> Haast. Flathead (Chinaman). <i>(1938,TrRSNZ,68/413</i>) [NOTES: Graham Fishes of Otago Harbour]

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> Flathead <i>Kathetostoma giganteum</i> Haast <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,409</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 409]

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> FLATHEAD <i>Kathetostoma giganteum</i> Haast ... Other names for the Flathead are Chinaman and Frenchman, but where these names originated and for what reason I do not know with certainty. One theory is that when Chinamen hawked fish about Dunedin they kept Flatheads for their own consumption, as they preferred them to all other fish. <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,286,2nd ed.</i>)

>  <b>PARROTT</b> THE FLATHEAD <i>Kathetostoma giganteum</i> Haast. <i>Ibid.</i>169 Flathead Kathetostoma giganteum Puffer-fish <i>(1957,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,143</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> Family URANISCOPIDAE. The flathead, <i>Kathetostoma giganteum</i> ..., is usually found on its own, often burrowing in the sea bed with just the eyes and mouth showing. It shares this way of lifeq (and its wide heavy heady, flattened on top, eyes staring upwards) with the stargazers and catfish. Some fishermen call it chinaman. <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,224</i>)

>  <b>SORENSON</b> MONKFISH (a) Scientific name: (i) <i>Geniagnus monopterygius</i> (ii) <i>Kathetostoma giganteum</i>. (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: (both) Whitefish. (c) Other common names: (both) Monkfish, Monks, Stargazer, Flathead (also applied at times to export gurnard); Churchills, Toebiters, Bulldogs, Spotted Stargazer, Maori Chief (Dunedin); Muddies. (d) Principal localities where taken: Napier, Cook Strait, Nelson, Banks Peninsula, Bluff-Stewart Island. ... Monkfish is a general term for species of the Families Uranoscopidae and Leptoscopidiae. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,37</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> FLATHEAD No relation to the ubiquitous Australian flathead who comes in several forms, most of which belong to the family Platycephalus. The New Zealand flathead is a gruesome, seldom-seen fellow called <i>Kathetostoma giganteum</i>. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,100</i>)

>  The hoki are sent down first and are by far the major species caught in this area designated G in New Zealand's exclusive economic zone. But there are also ling and school shark, headed and gutted before they are boxed, squid and silver dory, fat blue nose and silver warehou, hake and sharp-toothed southern kingfish, ugly green monkfish and slimy flathead, electric skates and others with saw toothed snouts. <i>(Aug 22, 1984,Marlborough Express,6</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Monkfish Kathetostoma giganteum</i> ... Also occurs in southern Australia. Known by a number of local names: giant stargazer, flathead, bulldog, boof, etc. The name monkfish is well established for this species in New Zealand, whereas overseas it is used for similarly shaped but unrelated anglerfishes. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,118</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> (two-up) ?a penny thrown up flat without spinning so that it, will come down `heads' cf float v. [(? obs)]

>  .. two `flat-heads' they `lob.' <i>(May 16, 1917,NZEF Chronicles,137</i>)



 
<b>4</b> <i>n.</i> [geology, mining]

>  The factors which have exerted the greatest influence on the position of the ore-shoots are (a) intersections - namely `flatheads', carbonaceous seams, faults, cross-courses, dykes; (b) the nature of the country rock; (c) depth. <i>(1907,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,No.4 (NS) 106</i>) [NOTES: (Fraser and Adams, Coromandel)]

### flathead gurnard
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Leptoscopus macropygus</i>, stargazer.

>  <b>WILSON</b> FLATHEAD GURNARD: See Spotted Stargazer <i>Ibid./i> 208 STARGAZER A distinctive estuarine fish which spends most of its life buried in mud up to the eyes. ... Comes in two varieties - the Common Stargazer and his tubbier cousin the Spotted Stargazer. ... Other names are River Gurnard, Mud Gurnard, Flathead Gurnard and Mud Grubber. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,100</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> Stargazer <i>Leptoscopus macropygus</i> Also occurs in south-eastern Australia. Alternatively called estuary stargazer, and mud, river, or flathead gurnard. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,119</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> STARGAZER ... Other names: <i>Leptoscopus macropygus</i>; river gurnard, mud gurnard, flat-head gurnard, mud grubber. <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,262</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] gurnard, flathead 1814 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

 <i>n.</i> [rare]

 <i>n.</i>

>  The rules state that each flatmate can leave the Auckland house for a maximum of four hours a week. <i>(Oct 1, 2000,Sunday Star Times,C9</i>)

### flats
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) areas of a farm property that are of mainly level land.

>  In Wellington extensive sheep farming swept up the east coast, first on the bracken-covered hills and tussock and scrub flats, and later on the bush clearings. <i>(Mar 15, 1947,NZ Journal of Agriculture,253</i>)

>  <b>STACEY</b> Slowly the flats assumed a grey tinge as 10,000 ewes emerged from the tussocks and strings of sheep joined the mob from the hills ... <i>(1996,Tales of a Tussock Jumper,81</i>)

>  I found a few flats up the river but they were too small and too high up, to be worth my while to take. <i>(1860,BLANK ENTRY,60</i>) [NOTES: in BUTLER First Year (1863)]

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The coastal flats of Homewood and the adjoining properties are great fattening country, producing big drafts of fat stock. <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island,48</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> With the emphasis on the flats, Makarora's whole management was turned round. It had been a traditional, extensively run property, but as the flats were fenced and grassed, it became intensive. <i>(1987,People, Places & Paddocks,177</i>)

>  A well-balanced unit comprising both river flats and easy terraces. <i>(Nov 25, 2000,Press, The,64</i>)

>  Prime finishing land with cropping flats. <i>(Nov 13, 2001,Hawke's Bay Today,31</i>)

>  The land area is made up of 50ha of strong fertile flats. [on the flats] <i>(Feb 22, 2002,Dominion,29</i>)

### flatsheet
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McARA</b> Flatsheet: A steel plate, usually four feet square and up to three-eighths of an inch thick, used on underground rail junctions instead of points and on the floor of the brace and shaft chambers to enable the handling and turning of ore-trucks. A considerable amount of skill and strength was required to handle the loaded trucks, which weighed 25 cwt, on these flatsheets. The trucks were built with two-thirds of the weight on the front axle to enable a man to lift the rear-end while turning the truck. <i>(1978,Gold Mining at Waihi (Glossary),322</i>)

### flatten the head
 
<b>1</b> <i>phr.</i> (Rural) [heading dog trials] to run short on a course.

>  <b>THOMSON</b> The long head is unusual in the sense that the dogs can either run up the creek, or up the guts but have a difficult piece of ground to cover to clear the sheep cleanly, and not flatten their head. <i>(1993,Taranaki Dog Trialing,8</i>)

### flattening paddock
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a fenced area on which scrub and fern has to be flattened by stock before cultivation. (See [hoof and tooth treatment](hom-hyp.md#hoof-and-tooth-treatment))

>  Several pens of big bullocks were yarded, but most of them had not the age or were not forward enough for the flattening paddock, and prices offered did not approach vendors' reserves. <i>(Apr 18, 1896,Hawera Star,2</i>)

### flatter
 
 <i>n.</i> a flat-stomached bad dive; belly flopper [(childrens')]

>  <b>HYDE</b> For a time Bede went out of her way to pass a swimming-bath ... where real boys splashed and yelled ... She listened to their commonplace talk. `Gee, that was a flatter.' ... `You're going off the ten-footer? Come and see, old Jenks thinks he can dive..' <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,176</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a person living in a flat

>  This must benefit all flatters. <i>(Mar 7, 1967,Critic,2</i>)

>  With its predecessor, <i>Food for Flatters</i>, one always imagined some provincial hearty (Kevin? Graham?) dashing in from rugby practice to see what might be lashed up from a pound of mince and a tin of something. <i>(Oct 15, 1990,Listener (New Zealand Listener),112</i>)

>  Some of it seems to come from the collective unconscious of a thousand Wellington flatters. <i>(Jul 19, 2000,Capital Times,4</i>)

>  `Feeding the `flatter' or the `roomer.' <i>(1948,NZ Dept Health Bulletin</i>)

>  At first glance, thy're a more sophisticated Food for Flatters. <i>(May 13, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),18</i>)

>  Varsity is drawing to a close and New Zealand Post wants student flatters to redirect their mail when they move back home. <i>(Oct 25, 2000,Capital Times,2</i>)

### flattie
 
 <i>n.</i> a flatmate [ex-flattie included here]

>  Every Tuesday at 7.30pm her flatties - who include a policeman, a manager and a telecommunications worker - scramble for a seat to witness a group of Aussie sheilas claw their way to the top. <i>(Mar 12, 2000,Dominion Sunday Times,F7</i>)

>  The flatties were surprised to see me home so early, expecting me to be out all night as I had been a lot. <i>(Aug 4, 1999,Dominion,10</i>)

>  He knows the gig, he explains, quickly shooting off a story about Dylan Tait interviewing his then-flattie musician Nathan Haines in New York. <i>(Oct 28, 1998,Capital Times,5</i>)

>  A bisexual flatmate joins the cast of This Life.. But not without dissension in the flatties' ranks <i>(Aug 24, 1998,Dominion,21</i>)

>  ...now that their mates and most of Wellington's students have seen it, will the well-worn themes of nightmare flatties pull the punters? <i>(Apr 11, 2001,Capital Times,7</i>)

>  Their presence provoked more humour than concern among the flatties. <i>(Oct 17, 1999,Sunday Star Times,B2</i>)

>  Clearly, you had to be there, but in lieu of such a privilege, the documentary had most of the old flatties on to reminisce about their "conceptual" freedom flat. ... All the ex-flatties remember it almost reverently, and frankly they all still look like characters out of <i>The Young Ones</i>. <i>(Dec 1, 2005,Dominion Post,B9</i>)

>  The three current "flatties" seem to get on peacefully enough. <i>(May 22, 2004,Dominion Post,E15</i>)

>  You don't want to get your flatties offside. <i>(Jun 20, 2002,TV2</i>) [NOTES: Shortland Street]



 
 <i>n.</i> flat tyre

>  Police believe thieves who stole a car which got a flattie, pinched another car rather than change a tyre. <i>(Aug 4, 1999,Norwester,5</i>)

>  Taking a stroll with your baby takes on new dimensions these days, especially as you get a flattie in the urban wilderness. <i>(Aug 12, 1998,Dominion,10</i>)

>  [Hdg] Flattie ends chase... Soon after, however, the case was closed because the car, stolen from Feilding, had a flat tyre. <i>(Feb 18, 1999,Dominion,3</i>)

>  "Lady", he said, "did you know you had a flattie on your rear wheel?" <i>(1999,Hubbard's 'Clipboard',44 : 3</i>)

>  However, he said dealing with flatties was easier - if you had no replacement tube you could stuff grass inside the tyre and continue driving. <i>(Oct 31, 2005,Horowhenua-Kapiti Chronicle,1</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Returning, I collect a flattie, rain begins, drenched I reach work, ... <i>(1983,Down from Marble Mountain,184</i>)

>  <b>McCAULEY</b> His own bike has a flattie. Maureen bought a new inner tube at Parenga, but though she's tried and Hennie's tried there seems to be no way of easing the wretched tyre off the rim. <i>(1986,Then Again,277</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> flattie a punctured tyre; eg `She's given the old dunga too hard a time on the corners, she got a flattie on the back left and flapped to a stop.' <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,45</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a flat spin, state of fluster, confusion (RNZAF variant of RAF `(go into) a flat spin' during WW2, and often in gen. colloq. use after) [WWII]

>  Don't race round in a `flattie' if you haven't finished your letter at 10.30, looking for candles. <i>(Dec 1944,Coral Dust</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (informal) a) a flat-bottomed rowing boat. [ca1920 Marlborough Sounds A flat-bottomed, and flat-sided, rowing-boat of simple construction, suited to shallows and mud-flats.]

>  A hard-working boatman who goes fishing in a flattie and one or two net fishermen are pestered with requests for fish. <i>(Nov 19, 1903,Otago Daily Times,10</i>)

>  You have to get aboard the launch by means of a "flatty" and that flatty has to do a bit of surf-riding. <i>(Jul 25, 1928,NZ Free Lance,7</i>)

>  <b>HOCKEN & FENWICK</b> [We] accepted the use of a flat-bottomed boat, of the species known in the district as `flatties.' 1892 ibid 6 `flattie' <i>(1892,Holiday Trip to the Catlins District, A,3</i>)

>  <b>MANNERING</b> One day a friend and I were blown out to sea in a flattie - not a nice experience. <i>(1943,Eighty Years,160</i>)

>  <b>HALL-JONES</b> Six years later, on 26 December 1898, Hall and Te Au set off from Snodgrass's hotel [Te Anau] in [112] a `flattie' ... <i>(1968,Early Fiordland,111</i>)

>  <b>MAY</b> I have just finished an article ... referring to flat bottomed boats (Flatties) used at Lake Ellesmere and Taumutu. In the early 1930s my Father bought a flattie which was used to go across to the Opihi mouth for whitebaiting season and also for eeling and floundering. <i>(1989,Te Karanga,3</i>) [NOTES: (Hera Graham)]

>  The latest class, the Takapuna, 12 ft 6 in flatties, are very handy little craft, and ideal for racing or knocking about the sheltered bays and beaches of Takapuna. <i>(Nov 26, 1921,Auckland  Star,18</i>)

### flatting
 
 <i>n.</i> [some citations below belong with the verb to flat]

>  I never went flatting, which wasn't the done thing in my time. <i>(Apr 1998,North & South,20</i>)

>  First time flatting is where the stories were for me. <i>(Apr 11, 2001,Capital Times,7</i>)

>  Anyone who has ever flatted, is flatting, or is thinking of flatting with other people, should see this play. <i>(Mar 5, 2001,Evening Post,16</i>)

>  If you were flatting with several other people, and one lost his job, and you all chipped in to give him some money to support him, well you'd all expect him to contribute to the household in some other way, wouldn't you? <i>(Dec 26, 1998,Listener (New Zealand Listener),27</i>)

>  At least there is no threat of being turfed out at the whim of a landlord as happened to me a number of times during my flatting days. <i>(Feb 3, 2001,Evening Post,8</i>)

>  Flatting brings you closer to the lies, loves and idiosyncrasies of complete strangers than you would otherwise choose to get. <i>(Jul 19, 2000,Capital Times,4</i>)

### flatulence tax
 
 <i>n.</i> a proposed tax on methane emissions by animals (See [also fart tax](f69-fin.md#fart-tax))

>  The Government has abandoned its plans for a flatulence tax in the face of fierce farmer opposition and international derision... "Animal fart tax puts wind up New Zealand farmers," the Canadian Broadcasting Corporation said on its website. "Farmers raise stink over New Zealand fart tax," said <i>The Guardian</i> newspaper in Britain. <i>(Oct 18, 2003,Dominion Post,A2</i>)

>  It's been a wonderfully retro experience watching the farmers get aerated about the government's new flatulence tax. <i>(Jul 19, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),16</i>)

>  So when the government proposed a "flatulence tax" on farmers - to make them pay for the methane emissions produced by their livestock - every fart joke in Christendom was wheeled out and dusted off. <i>(Sep 20, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),7</i>)

>  Mr Barton is not alone in his opposition to the Government's "flatulence tax". "Tell Helen she can stick her latest tax dream up her very own windtunnel," remarks a <i>Rural News</i> correspondent from Oamaru... The flatulence tax will see farmers charged about 9c a year for each mature sheep and from 54-72c a year for each mature beef or dairy animal... <i>(Jul 26, 2003,Dominion Post,F1</i>)

>  His lusty blowing on the conch shell sounds a perfect target for flatulence tax. <i>(Sep 13, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),56</i>)

 <i>adj.</i>

 <i>n.</i>

### flaw
 
 <i>n.</i> a williwaw q.v.

>  <b>HURSTHOUSE</b> [In Dusky Bay] it will generally be necessary to secure to the trees also, to prevent being drifted off from the steep bank by a flaw of wind. These flaws, or squalls, frequently blow with great violence off the high land, or down the gullies, during heavy gales outside. <i>(1857,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/246</i>)

### flax
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Linum monogynum</i>

>  (And) <i>Linum monogynum</i> <i>(Jan 1856,Edinburgh New Philosophical Journal</i>) [NOTES: Tancred Notes on the Natural History of ... Canterbury]



 
 <i>n.</i> of flax fibre, of dressed flax

>  <b>ANGAS</b> The `topuni,' or war mat, belongs exclusively to the man, and is only possessed by chiefs ... The `topuni' consists of a large flax cloak, into which is fastened, with every thread, a portion of dog's hair, assorted into various colours, having the exact appearance of a beautiful fur. <i>(1866,Polynesia,156</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [noun & attrib]

>  <b>GOVERNOR MACQUARIE</b> A proposal was some time since made to me ... of forming a settlement at their own expence on the northern island of New Zealand, for the purpose of collecting the flax plant of that country, and manufacturing it ... ...the projectors of the flax speculation on New Zealand still intend to prosecute their design; and ... send a party of men ... on board the brig Experiment, to New Zealand, where that vessel is to remain long enough to give them time to collect a quantity of dried flax to be sent Home for the inspection of His Majesty's Ministers ... <i>(Mar 12, 1810,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1810 Mar 12 (Sydney) Governor Macquarie to Viscount Castlereagh in 1908 McNab Historical Records 1/297 5.]

>  [These are 4 classes of land in the Canterbury Block] open grass land, that in which <i>fern</i> and <i>tutu</i> grow, the flax bottoms and the <i>reclaimable swamp land. </i> <i>(Jun 14, 1851,Lyttelton Times,6</i>) [NOTES: (Torlesse's Report)]



 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) a New Zealand fibre-yielding swamp plant. Also called harekeke, phormium. [check also BUSH(-)FLAX, FAIRY FLAX, MOUNTAIN FLAX; RAUHUIA <i>Phormium</i> spp. esp. <i>P. tenax, P. colensoi </i> often early native flax, New Zealand flax (see 4. below) (contrast also flax n2; and flax n3 for the exotic Australian flax and European (sc. linen) flax (or Linseed)) flax-lily, flax-plant, hemp (earlier and in more scientific works often called phormium); and its processed product]

>  Coastal shrub species such as flax, toetoe, hebe and manuka were planted. <i>(Nov 2006,Footnotes,26 / 7</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> <i>(1770,Cook's Journals</i>)

>  The flax plant is as common here as in any other part of New Zealand. [fn] Forster gave it its name, <i>Phormium tenax</i> - unless he appropriated this, like other things, from Solander's papers. <i>(May 11, 1773,Cook's Journals,2/136</i>)

>  <b>FORSTER</b> The stuff it was wove of was the common Flax. <i>Ibid..</i> Nov 25 3/428 .. a parcel of the prepared Flax. <i>(Jun 4, 1773,Resolution Journal of J.R. Forster, The,1982 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BAYLY</b> We bought great quantities of fine Flax of them, & great numbers of Ahooes or mantles made of the flax exceeding neat. <i>(Nov 9, 1773,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1773 Nov 9 Bayly's Journal in 1908 McNab Historical Records 2/212]

>  <b>GOVERNOR MACQUARIE</b> A proposal was some time since made to me .. of forming a settlement at their own expence on the northern island of New Zealand, for the purpose of collecting the flax plant of that country, and manufacturung it .. the projectors of the flax speculation on New Zealand still intend to prosecute their design; and .. send a party of men .. on board the brig Experiment, to New Zealand, where that vessel is to remain long enough to give them time to collect a quantity of dried flax to be sent Home for the inspection of His Majesty's Ministers ... <i>(Mar 12, 1810,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1810 Mar 12 (Sydney) Governor Macquarie to Viscount Castlereagh in 1908 McNab Historical Records 1/297 5.]

>  <b>McNAB</b> The Flax of New Zealand, more properly called hemp. <i>(1907,Murihiku</i>) [NOTES: 1813 Sep Robert Williams in 1907 McNab Murihiku]

>  <b>NICHOLAS</b> (And) <i>(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles)</i>)

>  <b>H.WILLIAMS</b> Messrs Davis and Shepherd .. landed from the Herald, which had brought a full cargo of potatoes and some flax. <i>(Apr 7, 1827,Journal,51,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BUTLER</b> How shall I describe everything - the little townlet .. the scattered wooden boxes of houses, with ragged roods of scrubby ground between them - tussocks of brown grass - the huge wide-leaved flax .. the healthy clear-complexioned men, shaggy-bearded, rowdy-hatted, and independent .. - the stores, supplying all heterogeneous commodities ... <i>(Jan 27, 1860,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,iii/28</i>) [NOTES: 1860 Jan 27 in 1863 Butler First Year iii/28]

>  <b>WHITWORTH</b> The hills rise very rapidly from the coast, being thickly wooded with hini-hini and other scrub, flax, grass-tree, cabbage-palm ... <i>(1870,Martin's Bay Settlement,5</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i> Phormium tenax</i> (The New Zealand Flax, or Flax-Lily). <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,102,1940, p. 106 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PINFOLD</b> Good business was often done with New Zealand hemp, the world's best fibre for binder-twine. It belongs to the lily family and is popularly termed `flax'. <i>(1930,Fifty Years In Maoriland,19</i>)

>  <b>15</b> <i>Flax</i>: Phormium Tenax. `The poor man's friend.' This plant, shaped like a giant tussock, was one of the most useful things that grew. The leaves or `blades' were ready-made straps and ropes for almost any purpose. The bush makes an excellent dog-kennel, and many a man has made a comfortable camp under one on a rough night. It is handy to tie a horse to and very good shelter for shorn sheep. The leaves are easily skinned; and the fibre was used for making crackers for stock and bullock whips. The individual plants are called <i>flax bushes</i> and the dried reed stalks (which Maoris and children tied into bundles to make rafts) are called <i>flax-sticks, koradi</i> ss. o[r] <i>kraddy</i> ss., I suppose a corrupted Maori word. On native country <i>flax</i> is always a sign of good land. ([1857 Paul Letters from Canterbury], p.96; [1898 Morris Austral-English]) <i>(Oct 14, 1933,Press, The</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  New Zealand hemp, or `flax' as it is termed locally, is one of the so-called `hard' or leaf fibres, as contrasted with the `soft' fibres from the bast of stems. <i>(1936,Agricultural Organization in NZ,537</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>(1940,Plants of NZ,106-111</i>)

>  <i>flax</i>: plant with large strong leaves used for rope making. <i>(1947,Reed Dictionary of Modern Maori,173</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> flax: a plant that grows in clumps of tall sword-like leaves with strong fibres. Strips of the leaves can be used like twine, among other things, for tying tomato-plants to stakes. <i>(1964,Collected Stories,299</i>) [NOTES: 1964 Pearson Glossary to 1964 Sargeson Collected Stories 1935-1963 299]

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Flax - harakeke, harareke, <i>Phormium tenax </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> flax: New Zealand flax, phormium tenax, a plant that grows in clumps of tall sword-like leaves with strong fibres. Strips of the leaves are woven to make the matting used in village churches and meeting-houses. The fibre had commercial use, and until the depression flax mills and flax-cutters' camps, temporary rough quarters for harvesters, would be found near swamps where the flax grew. <i>(1973,Brown Man's Burden,140</i>) [NOTES: (Pearson's Glossary)]

>  <i>flax </i>.. 4. N.Z. a swamp plant producing a fibre that is used by Maoris for decorative work, baskets, etc. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,424</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 286; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 187]]

>  <b>McCRAE</b> Next day went to Wy Whero a fine tract of cultivated land - Peaches - Taroo Flax, Meero - return to house ... <i>(1820,Journal,26,1928 ed.</i>)

>  <b>1/302</b> Flax (<i>harakeke</i>) .. O. Liliaceae; G. Rhipogonum; S. Ph. tenax. Profuse in swamps and elsewhere throughout the islands. Used as hemp and flax. <i>(1883,Ranolf and Amohia</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Astelia solandri</i> [check <i>solanderi</i> in mod usage?] =astelia, an epiphyte [[move this to separate entry TREE-FLAX and have x-ref see also .. at FLAX n1???] @n]

>  The shaggy leaf-bases of <i>Astelia Solandri</i>, the `tree-flax' of the settlers. <i>(1871,TrNZI,4/243</i>) [NOTES: (Kirk)]



 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Linum monogynum </i>

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> (Queen Charlotte Sound) Amongst the known kinds of plants are common and rough Bindweed, Nightshade and Nettles, ... rushes, ... Bullrushes, [ed. fn 5 Raupo, <i>Typha angustifolia</i>.] Flax, [fnd <i>Linum</i>. [ed. [<i>Linum monogynum</i>.] Allheal, ... <i>(Feb 1777,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1777 Feb Anderson's Journal in Cook Journals (1967) 3/804-805]

>  (And) <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Flax, native. <i>Linum monogynum</i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),764</i>)

>  <b>FEATON</b> Kaho [Maori name] .. Flax [Settlers' name] .. Linum monogynum .. Herb <i>Ibid.</i> 178 Rauhuia [Maori name] .. Flax [Settlers' name] .. Linum monogynum .. Herb <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,177</i>)

>  A true flax [fn <i>Linum monogynum</i>.] with handsome white flowers grows side by side with the usurper of its name, and occasionally one sees a scrubby bush of <i>Muehlenbeckia,</i> covered with its curious white berries. <i>(1900,Canterbury Old & New,183</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> (And) <i>Linum monogynum </i> <i>(1906,Plants of NZ</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Linum monogynum (The true New Zealand Flax)</i>. ... Native name Rauhuia. This is the true New Zealand flax; the plant which is usually so called being a lily. <i>(1940,Plants of NZ</i>)

>  Flax. <i>Linum monogynum</i> , Forst. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/40</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

>  <i>Linum monogynum</i> Flax <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/124</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the native <i>Phormium tenax</i>, of which many species and cultivars have been farmed commercially for fibre.

>  How shall I describe everything .. tussocks of brown grass - the huge wide-leaved flax. <i>(Jan 27, 1860,BLANK ENTRY,28</i>) [NOTES: in BUTLER First Year (1863)]

>  <b>FULLER</b> In noticing the waste land, it is observed that flax growing on it indicates a strong soil, toi-toi a wet one; while fern and tutu indicate a dry soil. <i>(1859,Five Years' Residence in New Zealand,132</i>)

>  Flax - Phormium tenax. The poor man's friend. <i>(Oct 14, 1933,Press, The  (Acland Glossary),15</i>)

>  With two six-horse teams he worked for 12 years breaking the land in from tall tea-tree and flax. <i>(1956,Farms & Stations of NZ Vol I,18</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> linen flax

>  An acre or so of European flax should be grown upon every farm where stock-rearing is carried on. <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,103</i>) [NOTES: 1899 Murphy in 1902 Settler's Handbook of NZ (Lands Department) 103]

>  As its Latin name suggests, the European, like the New Zealand flax, is a plant of many uses. <i>(Sep 28, 2002,Dominion Post,F15</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> (local =bluebell

>  <b>BOSWELL</b> [Mother and daughter c. 1890s] made pretty necklaces from the dried seed-pods of the field flax, which we called bluebells, tied with gay ribbons; ... and, most treasured of all, little peggy-bags of velvet, lined with bright silk or satin and threaded with silk cord for handles. <i>(1956,Dim Horizons,1955,146 ed.</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> Australian linseed

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> AUSTRALIAN LINSEED (<i>Linum marginale</i>), is also called wild linseed and wild or Australian flax. It is common all over the islands and is a twiggy and slightly branched perennial [up to 20 inches tall] much like the cultivated variety. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,117,1967, p. 119 ed.</i>)

>  <i>wild flax [=] Australian flax: rauhuia [s] </i> <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,86</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Linum marginale</i> Australian linseed, =wild flax, wild linseed

>  <b>SARAH MATHEW</b> ... not a flower did I see ... but the little scarlet pimpernel twice and one little blue flower like the flax in New South Wales. <i>(Apr 7, 1840,Austral English</i>) [NOTES: 1840 Apr 7 Sarah Mathew Journal in 1940 Founding of NZ 107]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> the fibre of a <i>Phormium</i> spp., here <i>P. colensoi </i>

>  (Queen Charlotte Sound) ... there is one [plant] which deserves particular notice, as the natives make their garments of it and it produces a fine silky flax superior in appearance to any thing we have and probably at least as strong. It grows everywhere near the sea and in some places a considerable way up the hills, ... [ed. fn 6 <i>Phormium colensoi</i>, obviously; not the swamp-loving <i>Phormium tenax.</i>] <i>(Feb 1777,Cook's Journals,3/805</i>) [NOTES: 1777 Feb Anderson's Journal in Cook Journals 3/805]

>  The Baron is requested to furnish these inquirers with estimates for the manufacture of <i>thirty</i> tons of gumless flax per week... Thus, then, we find two very considerable offers made for the employment of the Baron de Thierry's `ungummed flax;` ... <i>(Aug 12, 1857,Lyttelton Times,3</i>)

>  <b>DAWSON</b> The term `flax' for the genus <i>Phormium</i>, although firmly established, is yet another inappropriate common name as <i>Phormium</i> and true linen flax (<i>Linum</i>) have in common only the possession of useful fibres. Mountain flax is <i>Phormium cookianum</i> and its tufts of narrow, leathery leaves may be conspicuous in poorly drained places in tussock herbfield and shrubland. <i>(1988,Forest Vines to Snow Tussocks,181</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> Plant of the <i>Linum</i> sp (and its fibre), always so qualified in NZ to distinguish it from the more abundant, native flax, <i>Phormium tenax</i> (see OED 2b) (R.M.) [ca1920 (R. Mason) OED does not use the specific Linen flax [check]]

>  <b>MOORE & IRWIN</b> <i>LINUM MONOGYNUM</i>. The New Zealand linen flax is a plant of rocky outcrops, ... often with a profusion of white flowers. <i>(1978,Oxford Book of NZ Plants,54</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>LYSNAR</b> Soon the monster bird came stalking/ Proudly through the waving flaxes,/ <i>(1924,New Zealand (various authors/titles),100</i>)

### flax  whare
 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BATISTICH</b> Taddi was a good bush carpenter; he soon knocked up a rough cottage for the men to live in, better than the flax whare they agreed. <i>(1987,Sing Vila in the Mountain,33</i>)

### flax agent
 
 <i>n.</i> (historical) one who acted as a collector of prepared flax fibre for a (usu. Australian) principal

>  <b>H. WILLIAMS</b> I also wrote a letter to some of the leading men of Tauranga ..., and one to a Mr Scott [trader] who resided there as flax agent. <i>Ibid.</i> 351 In the afternoon called on the young man residing here [at Thames] as Flax agent <i>(Mar 15, 1833,Journal,296,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WILY & MAUNSELL</b> fn Mr Tapsell, a flax agent at Maketu, had his premises burned down and all his property either destroyed or taken away. <i>(1938,Robert Maunsell,37</i>)

### flax basket
 
 <i>n.</i> a basket made of flax (not for flax) (See [kete](kee-kiw.md#kete),[kit](kee-kiw.md#kit))

>  <b>YATE</b> half fill their flax-baskets with stones ... Can Europeans be surprised that the New Zealanders should pay them in their own coin; that they should half fill their flax baskets with stones to increase the weight, ... <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),31</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)]

>  <b>ANGAS</b> The woman, having the potatoes in a flax-basket or [238] `kit', with two handles, goes into the stream, and putting one foot into the basket with the roots, ... commences shaking them furiously, her foot acting as a scrubbing-brush; ... <i>(1847,Savage Life,1/237</i>)

>  <b>REISCHEK</b> The meal was now served up. It consisted of stewed eel and sweet potatoes, presented to us in little neatly woven, four-cornered flax-baskets. <i>(1933,Yesterdays in Maoriland,154,1933 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> The hangis had just ben uncovered and the food put into little freshly-made flax baskets called kono, one for each person. <i>(1948,Taina,15</i>)

>  <b>MASON</b> ... AROHA MATAIRA ... she wears a wide-brimmed straw hat. She carries a flax basket. <i>(1963,Pohutukawa Tree,20</i>)

>  <b>FULLER</b> Place in a coarse kete (flax basket) and stand in fresh water ... for two or three days. <i>(1978,Maori Food and Cookery,51</i>)

>  <b>SANSOM</b> The fresh fish were spread out on the grass for sale and housewives would be there with their woven flax baskets. <i>(1982,In the Grip of an Island,136</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [attrib. use]

>  <b>COWAN</b> These Ngati-Mutunga ... made up small casks of powder in flax-basket pikaus or back-loads, and transported them through all the forests and ranges of Pukerua and along the northern shore of the bay. <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,1/122</i>)

### flax bottom
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  ... there are four distinct sections ... most easily brought under cultivation. The open grass land, that in which fern and tutu easily grow, the flax bottoms, and the reclaimable swamp land. <i>(Jun 14, 1851,Lyttelton Times,6</i>)

### flax bush
 
 <i>n.</i> an individual bush or plant of New Zealand flax

>  Should the weather permit, by the tall flax-bush the candle is lit. <i>(Oct 11, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,7</i>)

>  With the best will in the world it is hard to find anything to praise about flax bushes. <i>(Aug 15, 2004,Sunday Star Times,C3</i>)

>  It is a perfect sea of brown grass-tree tops, only relieved by the occasional green of a flax bush or ti-ti growing along the sides of the canals, where the ground has some consistence. <i>(Jul 20, 1844,Nelson Examiner</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Monro Notes of a Journey (Nelson Examiner July 20 et seq.) in 1898 Hocken Contributions 246]

>  <b>GOLDER</b> (Introd) I had ... to pass the night ... under the shade of a flax-bush. <i>(1854,Pigeon's Parliament,v</i>)

>  ... he walked nine miles and saw a flax-bush, ... <i>(Aug 26, 1857,Lyttelton Times,4</i>)

>  <b>LADY CAMPBELL</b> ... so far regaining his strength as to crawl into a flax bush. <i>(1864,Martin Tobin,1/62</i>)

>  <b>BUTLER</b> It is volcanic, brown, and dry; ... and then a stiff, wiry, uncompromising-looking tussock of the very hardest grass; then perhaps a flax bush, or, as we should have said, a flax plant; ... <i>(Jan 27, 1860,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,iii/29</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> [The dog] returned to the flax-bush ..and killed several baby-wekas by successsive taps of his paw. <i>(1865,Station Life in NZ,38,1870 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MONEY</b> An ambush was laid by the Maories behind some flax bushes, ... <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,134</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> <i>(1873,Station Amusements in NZ,38</i>)

>  <b>CAIRD</b> ... cabbage trees and flaxbushes ... <i>(1874,NZ Sheepfarming,9</i>)

>  <b>WOOD</b> But with the promise which Maggie Ross gave him, that summer night amongst the whispering flax bushes by the creek, a change came over Dick. <i>(1875,Waiting for the Mail,12</i>)

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> This was twisted into a rope and piled into a pyramid behind a large flax-bush, ... A rope was extemporized out of flax-leaves, and one end tied to the top end of the pyramid of calico. <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,63,1935 ed.</i>)

>  <b>DOMETT</b> And the louder flax-bushes/ With their crowding and crossing/ <i>(1883,Ranolf and Amohia,1/230</i>)

>  <b>LADY MARTIN</b> A high bank above our garden had one or two tall flax-bushes growing on it, and many ferns. <i>(1884,Our Maoris,4</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> Flax-bush ... the bush of the New Zealand Flax. <i>(1898,Austral English,148</i>)

>  <b>PASCOE</b> The Paridise Duck ... in a flax bush. <i>(1957,Mr Explorer Douglas,235</i>) [NOTES: ca1899 Douglas in 1957 Pascoe Mr Explorer Douglas 235]

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> The settler [farmer] is never in want of a piece of twine with a flax bush growing near his home. <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,104,1940, p. 106 ed.</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> Suddenly a Hauhau sentry was surprised half asleep under a flax-bush. <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,2/294,1983 ed.</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> ... the tall korari stalks of the flax-bushes dipping to the water-side, [bear] abundant promise of honey liquor for the summer birds, ... <i>(1926,Travel in NZ,(Vol II South Island) 28</i>)

>  <b>LANCASTER</b> ... Darien went through the muddle of manuka, flax-bushes, tree-ferns, and tall trees careering over hills and up and down gullies ... <i>(1938,Promenade,78</i>)

>  <b>MASON</b> ... I first scooped a hole in the flaxbush and made a rough seat there. <i>(1981,Solo,88</i>) [NOTES: 1976 Mason `Not Christmas' in 1981 Solo 88]

>  <b>MILLER</b> Then there's those two drongoes. ... What's the bloody matter with them? Me I'd be into the flax bushes like a shot, ... <i>(1986,Gemina,23</i>)

>  <b>KNOX</b> Beside the track the last of the flax bushes ... clapped encouragement. <i>(1987,After Z-Hour,232</i>)

>  <b>LINDLEY</b> [OED] In New Zealand ... the Phormium Tenax or Flaxbush. <i>(1846,Vegetable Kingdom,iv/203</i>)

>  <b>JOHN GROVES</b> .. we are in the centre from Wellington to a township called Ahuriri. For all travellers ... are often obliged to sleep under the flaxbush. <i>(Nov 17, 1855,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1855 Nov 17 John Groves in 1971 Manson I Take up My Pen 31]

### flax dresser
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  It was not until two years later that King obtained his New Zealand flax dressers. <i>(May 6, 1924,65 Years of Guides,69</i>)

### flax fibre
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Flax fibre was also used for rope, string, measuring lines, bags, purses, thread, fishnet, bed sheets and sails. <i>(Sep 28, 2002,Dominion Post,F15</i>)

### flax gully
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>DAVIDSON</b> Rounding a large boulder on the side of a flax gully he came face to face with a large boar. <i>(1889,Stories of NZ Life,25</i>)

### flax gum
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>KINGHT</b> ... suddenly into his mind popped the soft translucent jelly substance found at the base of the flax stalks in the swamps. He'd often played with it - soft cool, even experimented with its edibility. ... Tumoti had gone to bed unwashed and unnoticed until the flax gum began to dry and contract <i>(1975,Poyntzfield,164</i>)

### flax honey
 
 <i>n.</i> the honey from bees feeding on flowers of the New Zealand flax; =korari honey

>  ...she has developed a unique range of seven products using New Zealand ingredients including avacado oil, Pacific Blue lavender, kawakawa, flax and manuka honey, that guarantee "no stink or sting". <i>(Aug 26, 2004,Dominion Post,D3</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> [`Taa-korari'] is to shake the flax-honey out of the flower cluster at the head of the stalk. This flax honey [sic] is sweet, and if there was not enough to make a drink it was sprinkled over certain foods. It was often shaken over the kauru. <i>(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,65</i>)

>  There's plenty of flax-honey ... the season has been too dry for clover honey. <i>(1950,Wind from the Sea, A,20</i>)

### flax kete
 
 <i>n.</i> -

>  Mr Davis.. will today lay a rock, contained in a flax kete, as a memorial to Jody at the place he died... The aim is the kete will gradually dissolve and go back into the land and the rock will be there. <i>(Mar 13, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),24</i>)

### flax kit
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [kete](kee-kiw.md#kete))

>  She carried with her a flax kit of kumaras, ... and struggled through the bush tracks, loaded with her heavy kits, taking three days to reach the school. <i>(1940,Tales of Pioneer Women,33,1988 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BALLANTYNE</b> ... two Maori women bending over and half a dozen kids putting the pippis in flax kits. <i>(1948,Cunninghams, The,44,1976 ed.</i>)

>  She [a Maori girl] ... carried a basket in her hand, a flax kit. <i>(1977,Listener Short Stories,48</i>) [NOTES: 1958 Phillip Wilson `One World' in 1977 Listener Short Stories 48]

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> We collected mussels and pipis in flax kits, ... and sunbathed the afternoon away. <i>(1959,New Zealanders, The,1986,22 ed.</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> An old flax kit, partly burned, contained the remains of a small child. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,101</i>)

### flax land
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> The chief part of the land ... yet cultivated near Nelson is flax or fern land. <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,258</i>)

>  <b>SWAINSON</b> The soil of the plain is of various character : a considerable portion consists of a light dry vegetable soil, well adapted for clover paddocks, or for the growth of barley; about an equal quantity is dark-coloured, good, strong flaxland, suitable for wheat and potatoes; ... <i>(1853,Auckland,38</i>)

>  <b>LINDSAY</b> Hence, next to bush and swamp-land proper (on alluvial clays), Flax-land is regarded as the most desirable for agricultural purposes. `Flax-swamps' are frequently as common as `Tussock-swamps,' though their soil is far from being so wet or watery. Like the `Tussock-swamps,' they are frequently very `tussocky' - causing walking thereon to be extremely ... fatiguing. <i>(1868,Contributions to NZ Botany,80</i>) [NOTES: [cf Bush-land]]

### flax lease
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) leasehold land on which flax is grown.

>  <b>CHINN</b> This river was the northern boundary of the flax lease. <i>(1963,Packtrack to Highway,113</i>)

>  <b>CHINN</b> Flax leases were being taken up in several parts of Westland and in some places mills were being erected. <i>(1963,Packtrack to Highway,110</i>)

### flax lily
 
 <i>n.</i> the New Zealand flax <i>Phormium tenax </i> [(obs, rare)]

>  <b>LINDSAY</b> [<i>P. tenax</i>] is the long-familiar `New Zealand Flax' plant or `Flax Lily' [fn `Treasury of Botany,' 1866]; terms which must now be held to include <i>P. Colensoi ... </i> <i>(1868,Contributions to NZ Botany,79</i>)

>  <b>VON MUELLER</b> (And) <i>Phormium tenax </i> <i>(1888,Select Extra-tropical Plants</i>)

>  <b>LINDSAY</b> (Andersen) <i>(1868,Ferns Which Grow in NZ,1875 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> Flax-lily, ... (1) An Australian fibre plant, <i>Dianella lavis</i>, var. <i>aspera</i>, R. Br., N.O. <i>Liliacea</i>. (2) <i>Phormium tenax </i>. See Flax, New Zealand. <i>(1898,Austral English,148</i>)

>  ... tall flax-lily (phormium tenax).... the vegetation may be observed in thickets of tea-tree [fn Leptospermum], gigantic clumps of the tall flax-lily [fn <i>Phormium tenax </i>] ... and that curious sedge known popularly as nigger-head [fn <i>Carex vergata</i>] ... <i>Ibid.</i> 183 ... here and there the monotony is relieved by clumps of native flax-lily [fn <i>Phormium tenax </i>.] with their broad sword-shaped leaves and tall flower-stalks, while the tall-stemmed palm-lilies [fn <i>Cordyline australis</i>.] in places lend an almost tropical aspect to the scene. <i>(1900,Canterbury Old & New,179</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Phormium tenax (The New Zealand Flax, or Flax-Lily). </i> <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,102,1940 p. 106 ed.</i>)

>  <i>flax lily n</i> 1 : NEW ZEALAND FLAX 2 : any of several plants constituting a genus (<i>Dianella</i>) of the family Liliaceae; esp : an Australian plant (<i>D. laevis</i>) that yields a long silky fiber formerly used by the aborigines for making baskets <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,867</i>)

### flax market
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HUNT</b> The flax market remained depressed from 1890 to 1898. <i>(1987,Foxton 1888-1988,117</i>)

### flax mill
 
 <i>n.</i> a plant or factory for dressing New Zealand flax

>  Two men, employed at the flax mills.. reported having seen on the beach.. twelve bodies.. washed up by the sea. <i>(Jan 17, 1871,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  [Rutherford's] flax-mill near Havelock in the Marlborough Sounds was driven by a steam engine. <i>(May 2000,AA Directions,23</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> Flax-mill and rope-walk in Suburban North [Nelson] ... <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,265</i>)

>  The native population of Witiangi is very small. There are no wharres on the south shore. ... [58] ... four miles from the mouth of the river [Mangrove] is a flax-mill, in good order, but not worked at present. <i>(Jun 13, 1853,NZ Government Gazette,1/9/57-58</i>) [NOTES: (Sailing Directions, Mercury Bay)]

>  <b>EARP</b> The number of manufactuaries already established in the [Wellington] province is as follows:- ... six cooperages, one flax-mill, four rope-walks, two sacking-looms, ... <i>(1853,New Zealand (various authors/titles),96</i>)

>  <b>LUSH</b> Went to the Orchards' to see their new flax mill. <i>(Aug 10, 1868,Waikato Journals,148,1982 ed.</i>)

>  <b>TINNE</b> ... we caught occasional glimpses on the bank of flax-mills, with their snowy fibre drying on the fern; ... <i>(1873,Wonderland of the Antipodes,4</i>)

>  <b>KNOX</b> Great quantities of flax are raised here nowadays, as you will understand when you know there are some forty and odd flax-mills in the colony, ... <i>(1888,Boy Travellers,210</i>)

>  <b>WALLACE</b> The Flax Mill, Whakaki. <i>(1891,Rural Economy of Aust. & NZ,249</i>)

>  <b>HUNT</b> The rope factory.. continued in operation until 1916, when it was converted into a flaxmill. <i>(1987,Foxton 1888-1988,125</i>)

>  Flax-mills .. 177 <i>(1892,NZ Official Handbook,104</i>)

>  The flax mill is to be pushed, house added to and new stables. <i>(May 13, 1907,Chudleigh Diary,441,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> ../ some bushmen have an idea that the juice of the leaf acts as a poison to a cut, and aver that cuts obtained in the handling of the leaves in a flax-mill usually fester. <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,106,1940, p. 109 ed.</i>)

>  <b>DEVANNY</b> Sawmills, flaxmills, sheep-stations and cattle-runs encircled it in <i>(1926,Butcher Shop, The,9</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE</b> Many of those who had poured into New Zealand during the gold rush had remained there, and were ... now ... working flax mills or felling timber. <i>(1930,NZ Memories,151</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> [The cut flax was] hauled by horses to the smoother ground where the waggons for Finnegan's flax-mill could pick it up. <i>(1936,Passport to Hell,47</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> As he thought of his growing family he could see no openings for them in the Halfway Bush and Whare Flat districts, where the only outlook was a few flaxmills which must sooner or later cut all the available flax. <i>(1947,Pioneer Recollections,4,1956 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HUNT</b> The considerable fall in these many waterways made it possible to install waterwheels, which were freely used for power for flaxmills. <i>(1951,Confessions,23</i>)

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> flax: New Zealand flax, phormium tenax, a plant that grows in clumps of tall sword-like leaves with strong fibres. Strips of the leaves are woven to make the matting used in village churches and meeting-houses. The fibre had commercial use, and until the depression flax mills and flax-cutters' camps, temporary rough quarters for harvesters, would be found near swamps where the flax grew. <i>(1973,Brown Man's Burden,140</i>) [NOTES: (Pearson's Glossary)]

>  <b>STUART</b> [photo caption] A flaxmill with waterwheel on William Stuart's property, Otapiri Gorge, before the turn of the century. all directions. <i>(1982,Satyrs of Southland,15</i>)

>  <b>HARTLEY</b> ... we children were constant visitors in spite of the fact that we had to pass the flaxmill, for we had an irrational fear of the Hindu mill workers. <i>(1987,Swagger on Our Doorstep,57</i>)

>  Sunday Morning at a Flaxmill Camp. [caption] <i>(Jul 1905,NZ Illustrated Magazine,251</i>)

>  <b>WAITE</b> The musterer and station owner ... forsook their flocks; the bushman put away his crosscut and axe; the flaxmill hand left swamp and mill and hurried to the nearest railway station. <i>(1919,New Zealanders at Gallipoli,2</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR & HARREX</b> Seiferts owned the seven-stripper 'Miranui' mill at Shannon in the Manawatu, the largest flaxmill ever operated in New Zealand. <i>(1978,Looking Back,166</i>)

>  <b>OWEN & PERKINS</b> I know one man who was arrested on a flax mill, and he was just dropped into camp in his flax-working clothes - unshaven and so forth. <i>(1986,Speaking for Ourselves,120</i>)

### flax moth
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Mr. Simmonds suggests that the marsh-tit would help in the eradication of the flax-grub. But ... the caterpillar of <i>Persectania steropastis</i> (the species which does most of the damage in the South) hides during the day in the sheaths at the bases of the leaves, where no bird could capture it. I believe that the larva of the <i>Xanthorhoe praefectata</i>, the northern flax-moth, also conceals itself during the day. <i>(1918,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,1/320</i>)

>  [caption] Graph illustrating the ...life-cycle of the New Zealand flax-moth (<i>Orthoclydon praefectata</i>). <i>(1930,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,11/278</i>)

 <i>n.</i> pupuharakeke (<i>Placostylus hongii</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> SNAIL, FLAX or PUPUHARAKEKE (<i>Placostylus hongii</i>). This is a tall-spired, solidly built chocolate to reddish-brown land snail up to 3 in. in height. [Northland] <i>Ibid.</i> 3/705-12 Flax Snail - pupuharakeke, <i>Placostylus hongii</i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/267</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> PUPUHARAKEKE or FLAX SNAIL (<i>Placostylus hongii</i>). ??? to reddish-brown coloured snail about 3 inches in height. These snails are vegetarian and feed largely upon fallen karaka leaves. They are found hidden under leaves and around sedges(?), but only in flax when there is no other cover. <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The</i>)

>  <b>LOCKLEY</b> At the present time some light <i>Placostylus</i> flax snails, so called because they feed on the fallen leaves of karaka and other shrubs and den up in thick flax, are reduced to a few specimens in some northern localities; ... <i>(1980,House Above the Sea,241</i>)

>  FLAX SNAIL: Now found in only a few isolated spots. <i>(Oct 18, 1987,Sunday News,17</i>)

### flax swamp
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>YATE</b> The Puriri is everywhere surrounded with flax-swamps; and the banks of the fresh-water stream present but little else. <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),206</i>)

>  <b>SELWYN</b> [I] was interrupted by a flax swamp, ... <i>(1847,New Zealand (various authors/titles),Part I (The Church in the Colonies) 58</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Selwyn Journal Letter in 1847 NZ Part I (The Church in the Colonies) 58]

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> Te Rehe now became so impatient to reach home ... that we lost sight of him in a flax swamp, and had to find our way out as best we could. <i>(1851,Southern Districts of NZ,229</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Jan 19 in 1851 Shortland Southern Districts of NZ 229]

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> We met a large number of the natives returning from a great committee to determine their respective right to certain flax swamps. <i>(Mar 15, 1850,Journal,7/79</i>)

>  <b>EARP</b> At the point where the high [Nelson] bluffs cease, a very irregular bank of boulder-stones detaches itself from the main land, and, at the distance of about half a mile, runs parallel with it for eight or nine miles, nearly to the bottom of the bay. The space between this boulder-bank and the main land ... is filled by a flax swamp ... <i>(1853,New Zealand (various authors/titles),134</i>) [NOTES: [Nelson]]

>  <b>CARBERRY</b> The Battle of Rangariri (no date) ... they drove the Maoris like sheep before them over a deep Gully at the bottom of which lies an extensive Flax swamp, and the slaughter of the Evening was sharp ... <i>(1863,Journal</i>)

>  If the weather has been very dry ... we attempt to burn a great flax swamp. <i>(1867,Station Life in NZ,195,1870 ed.</i>)

>  <b>LINDSAY</b> Hence, next to bush and swamp-land proper (on alluvial clays), Flax-land is regarded as the most desirable for agricultural purposes. `Flax-swamps' are frequently as common as `Tussock-swamps,' though their soil is far from being so wet or watery. Like the `Tussock-swamps,' they are frequently very `tussocky' - causing walking thereon to be extremely ... fatiguing. <i>(1868,Contributions to NZ Botany,80</i>)

>  The Korari is the name of the flower stalk, often, but wrongly, used as the name of the plant. The edible root is the Aruhe. The word `Muka' is commonly applied to the dressed fibre; it is the correct name for the inner leaves. Cleared bush-land is suitable for the cultivation of the flax plant, and even swamp-land is improved by drainage. In the various flax swamps near nelson ...the finest plants grow upon the soil that has been thrown out of the ditch ... <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/117</i>) [NOTES: (Heaphy NZ Flax)]

>  <b>BAINES</b> After tramping many miles over steep fern ranges, through flax swamps, and tangled forest ... <i>(1874,Edward Crewe,195</i>)

>  <b>BATEMAN</b> The tussock land abounds in the Middle Island, although the richest land was originally what are termed `flax swamps.' <i>(1881,Colonist, The,186</i>)

>  <b>LANCASTER</b> There was evil in the wild, sensuous smell of flax-swamp that rose to windward . .. <i>(1987,Happy Endings,101</i>) [NOTES: 1902 Lancaster Our Lady of the Plains in 1987 Happy Endings 101]

>  <b>MANSFIELD</b> Then the rain fell heavily drearily - on to the river and the flax swamp - <i>(1907,Urewera Notebook,39,(1978 Gordon) ed.</i>)

>  <b>GORST</b> After dinner we had to cross a flax-swamp. <i>(1908,New Zealand Revisited,121</i>)

>  ... whereas in New Zealand the flax swamps could be easily converted into dairying-land. <i>(1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,4/36</i>)

>  Mr. Oliver caught a clear view of <i>Porzanoidea plumbea</i>, the swamp-rail, in a flax swamp near the sea-coast. <i>(1925,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,8/15</i>) [NOTES: (Hamilton Poor Knights)]

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> [OEDS] <i>(1926,Tutira</i>)

>  <b>LANCASTER</b> He ... would stop ... to listen to the blue-feathered scarlet-legged pukeka calling in a warm flax-swamp ... <i>(1938,Promenade,249</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> In some of the heavy flax swamps near the sea there were quite a number of really wild cattle ... <i>(1940,Te Waimate,143,1954 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [see Maori flax]

>  The first arrivals were linen workers from Glasgow and Paisley who had heard about the vast flax swamps of New Zealand, and arrived here with all their linen-making machinery only to find that the flax was "Maori flax" and not suitable for making fine linen. <i>(Jul 1998,NZ Free Lance,No. 39/41</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a marshy area in which Phormium tenax freely grow.

>  <b>PASCOE</b> On 22 February [1891] Douglas took two weeks' supplies up the valley, through flax swamps and lawyer tangles. <i>(1959,Great Days in NZ Exploration,139</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

 <i>n.</i>

 <i>n.</i>

### flax-grub
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  [Hdg] The Flax-Grub. Undoubtedly the most serious injury to flax swamps at the present day is caused by the larvae of a native moth called by millers the 'flax-grub.' <i>(Jan 20, 1915,Journal of Agriculture Cookery Book,10 : 5</i>)

>  ... the marsh tit of Europe would doubtless help considerably in the control of the flax-grub which is at present causing heavy losses to the country. <i>Ibid.</> 1/320 Mr. Simmonds suggests that the marsh-tit would help in the eradication of the flax-grub. But ... the caterpillar of <i>Persectania steropastis</i> (the species which does most of the damage in the South) hides during the day in the sheaths at the bases of the leaves, where no bird could capture it. I believe that the larva of the <i>Xanthorhoe praefectata</i>, the northern flax-moth, also conceals itself during the day. <i>(1918,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,1/132</i>)

>  ... the dry conditions and accumulation of dead leaves are favourable as well to the flax-grub. <i>Ibid.</i>.283 Kirk, T.W. and Cockayne, A.H. The Pests and diseases of NZ Phormium. NZ Dept Ag Ann Report 1909 pp286-289 <i>Ibid.</i> Cockayne, A.H.: The Flax-grub. N.Z. Jour. Agric., x, pp. 1-7 (1915). <i>(1930,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,11/280</i>)

>  <b>GILLESPIE</b> The ones attracting most attention were the owlet moths, the caterpillars of which destroyed the first pastures and cereal and root crops. There were also porina moths (<i>Oxycanus</i> spp.) whose caterpillars also destroyed pastures; the bag moth (<i>Oeceticus omnivorus</i>) which left cigar-shaped cocoons hanging from shrubs and tree [sic]; and the ghost-like white moth ... of the flax grub (<i>Orthoclydon praefectata</i>) which eats longitudinal slits on the surface of [flax] leaves ... <i>(1958,South Canterbury,10,1971 ed.</i>)

### flax-hook
 
 <i>n.</i>

### flax-house
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>POLACK</b> and a flax-house at [186] Uwoua, eighty feet long by twenty-four broad and forty feet high to the ridge-pole, was built by a chief and a large company of natives for a small keg of powder 25lbs. in weight. The house was extremely water-tight and warm, and several streams and marshes had been denuded of their rushes (raupo) to cover the roof and sides; ... <i>Ibid. 1/210-211 The largest public works undertaken are those of flax-houses, some of which are above one hundred [211] feet in length, thirty feet in width, and forty feet in height. These are built less for ornament than being durable and water-tight, as any rain pouring on the flax would discolour and finally rot it. <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/185</i>)

 <i>n.</i> the blade of the NZ flax

Richmond)]

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> The huts were all locked up - that is, the doors were tied up with a strip of flax-leaf, a quite sufficient `barrin' o' the door' to keep out all intruders save enemies on the war-path, ... <i>(1881,Poenamo,Bk2/5/100</i>)
