>  <b>MORRIS</b> ~ the blade of the New Zealand Flax (q.v.). <i>(1898,Austral English,148</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> Love-tokens are said to have been made by the Maoris in the early days from strips of flax-leaves. <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,106,1940 p.109 ed.</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> Quite accidentally the fugitives discovered a pass between the Alps ..., and toiling on ... shod with paraerae, or sandals of flax-leaves, they crossed the divide. <i>(1935,Hero Stories of NZ,253</i>)

### flax-leaf basket
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> ... the steam was just arising from their hangis as these were being uncovered, and we were all soon served, each with a little freshly-plaited flax-leaf basket filled with most deliciously cooked kumeras, potatoes and peppies. <i>(1881,Poenamo,Bk2/6/107</i>)

### flax-like
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>COWAN</b> Some of the Maoris climbed the tree, and from the cover of the thick flax-like growth of wharawhara, or astelia, in the forks of the main branches, fired over the log wall. <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,1/281</i>)

### flax-line
 
 <i>n.</i> an extemporised line of knotted flax-leaves

>  <b>PYKE</b> Loosing the flax-line, he made a careful survey of the locality. <i>(1873,Wild Will Enderby,3/iii/83,(1889,1974) ed.</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> The end was fastened with a flax-line carried across the track so laid that any suspecting invader coming along the track ... would release the trap ... <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,2/150,1983 ed.</i>)

### flax-mat
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BAKER</b> Flax mats softened an already well-worn floor as they padded across. <i>(2000,Kokopu Dreams,64</i>)

>  Without an export an import trade, the New Zealanders might not have to go back to "the pipi and flax mat" standard, but out standard of living and comfort would be considerably lowered. <i>(Jun 21, 1943,NZEF Times,II/104 : 6</i>)

>  <b>MALCOLM</b> The women spent the morning making flax mates as plates for the guests. <i>(1982,Where it all Began,134</i>)

>  <b>POWER</b> In the interior of the Pa the Wahines[e-acute], or matrons, are busy weaving flax-mats, cleaning potatoes or fish, or engaged in the superintendence of a Maori oven, or a huge gipsy-looking cauldron, called a `go-ashore', and can only afford to greet the visitor with a whining `tena koe pakeha', sighing as though they were very much to be pitied. <i>Ibid.</i> 172 ... the women were cooking, gossiping, or making flax-mats; .. <i>(1849,Sketches in NZ,160</i>)

>  Whares with bullet proof flax mats, for roofs, were built all along inside the rifle pits, ... <i>(1864,Von Tempsky Memoranda,76</i>)

>  <b>GORST</b> ... the service was European fashion. We sat at a table with a white cloth, forks and spoons. I felt a lingering longing for the old hospitality of former days, when the company sat on the ground, upon clean flax mats spread over the floor, when each guest had a separate portion handed to him by a Maori girl in a little green flax kit, woven for the occasion, potatoes and meat and vegetable marrow together, to which he had to help himself with his fingers. <i>(1908,New Zealand Revisited,307</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> Besides the whare (houses), there were whata (futtahs) standing there when I was a boy. They stood on two posts, and each post had a niche cut round it to stop the rats from getting up to the food stored on them. There was no roof on them, the food being covered with flax mats. <i>(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,33</i>)

### flax-matting
 
 <i>n.</i> matting, woven from dressed flax; =(Maori) mat(ting)

>  <b>JOHNSTONE</b> The Maori cooks then place flax-matting upon the hot stones. <i>(1874,Maoria,2</i>)

### flax-milling
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>CARR</b> Sheep-raising, flax-milling ... and timber-milling are the main industries. <i>(1913,Country Work,10</i>)

### flax-owner
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  As a result of their investigations, the Committee urged that [154] flax-owners should see that the flax is cut in such a manner as to leave the heart of the flax-fans uninjured <i>(1892,Official Handbook of NZ,153</i>)

### flax-plant
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>J.R. FORSTER</b> ... two Nets made of the split leaves of the flaxplant ... <i>(Mar 29, 1773,Resolution Journal of J.R. Forster, The,2/242</i>)

>  <b>G. FORSTER</b> Here [at Dusky Bay] we found a double canoe hauled upon the shore ... The canoe ... consisted of two troughs or boats joined together with sticks, tied across the gunwales with strings of the New Zeeland flax-plant. <i>Ibid.</i> 1/202 ... the New Zeeland flax-plant (<i>phormium</i>), [grew] round some old abandoned huts of the natives. <i>(1777,Voyage Round the World,1/132</i>)

>  <b>KING</b> The flax-plant of New Zealand grows spontaneously in many parts of the [Norfolk] island, but mostly abounds on the sea-coast, where there is a very great quantity of it. <i>(Jan 10, 1791,Letter,1/120</i>) [NOTES: 1791 Jan 10 Lieut.-Governor Phillip King Description Norfolk Isd in 1908 McNAB Hist Records 1/120]

>  <b>ELLIS</b> The native flax-plant, <i>phormium tenax</i>, ... is by no means like the flax or hemp plants of England, but resembles, in its appearance and manner of growth, the flag or iris. <i>(1816,Polynesian Researches,1/ii/27,1829 ed.</i>)

>  <b>NICHOLAS</b> The plant which grew in greatest profusion, ... was the flax-plant, ... <i>(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/111</i>)

>  <b>McCRAE</b> The hill was covered completely with the flax plant which is called Corade[acute] Mauvre, and which grew in great luxuriance. [Andersen fn.: ... <i>korari</i> is usually the tall flower-stalk of <i>harakeke</i> ..., but in the North sometimes used to designate the plant itself. Here McCrae uses the word <i>maori</i> in its original sense signifying `normal, usual, ordinary'. <i>(1820,Journal,22,1928 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SHEPHERD</b> The harbour here is bordered by large rocks ... much overhanged [360] by weeping trees and flax plants. <i>(Mar 10, 1826,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1826 Mar 10 Shepherd Journal in 1940 Howard Rakiura 359-360]

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> They cut a few sticks ... tie them together with the flax plant, and thus form the frame of their shed, and cover it with <i>toetoe</i>, a kind of long grass ... <i>(Jan 4, 1832,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The,212,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>W.WILLIAMS</b> The plain is swampy, and contains a large portion of the flax plant, which has called the attention of Europeans to this quarter for three or four years past. <i>(Dec 1, 1832,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1832 Dec 1 W.Williams's Journal in 1940 Matthews Matthews of Kaitaia 28-29]

>  <b>McDONNELL</b> The phormium, or flax-plant, grows in wild luxuriance throughout the three islands of New Zealand: it is indigenous to the country, and perennial; the leaves averaging from six to ten feet in length. <i>(1834,Extracts From a Journal,18,1979 ed.</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> ... by the aid of some flax-plants that grew indigenous on the sea-born isle, he formed some ropes which he attached to various stars, ... <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/12</i>)

>  <b>BURNS</b> [Vocab of English and NZ Words] Flax-plant Koraddee <i>(1844,NZ Chief,23</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> ... the indigenous flax-plant and ... the Cabbage-tree or Ti-palm. [fn] <i>Dracaena Australis. </i> <i>Ibid.</i> 149 [The kiekie] has a bunch of leaves, growing from the root like those of the flax-plant, but seldom exceeding two feet in length. <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,45</i>)

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> Our natives soon constructed [a mokihi] ... from the dry stalks of the flax plant (<i>phormium</i> tenax), a material light as cork, ... <i>(1851,Southern Districts of NZ,168</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> Travellers talk of the solitude of the forests, but there is society in trees which men miss on immense plains; it is on the prairie alone that the solitary traveller has a sensation of loneliness ... On the coast plains in the North Island ferns and flax plants supply the place of grasses. <i>(1859,Story of New Zealand, The,1/17</i>)

>  <b>BENNETT</b> (And) [<i>Phormium tenax </i>] <i>(1860,Gatherings of a Naturalist</i>)

>  <b>MONEY</b> The roll of blankets was made as tight as could be, and tied with the blades of the flax plant, ...; a couple of strings, also made from flax blades, secured the swag to the shoulders of the bearer; ... <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,23</i>)

>  Muka of the natives, as the dressed fibre of the Harakeke, or Flax Plant) <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/48</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North Isd)]

### flax-planter
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  He described briefly what had been done ... by the [Hawaiian] sugar-planters' experimental station, and advocated the formation of a similar station by the flax-planters. <i>(Mar 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,4/36</i>)

### flax-producing
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>FITTON</b> Although New Zealand has long been celebrated as a flax producing country, ... very little New Zealand flax has found its way to this side of the world. <i>(1856,New Zealand (various authors/titles),204</i>)

### flax-root
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>COWAN</b> Boiled flax-root water poured on the wounds, and also dock-root (runa), well scraped and boiled, were our favourite remedies for gunshot and bayonet wounds. <i>IBid.</i> 2/396 Te Kaka was suffering agony from his shattered jaw; he had contrived to pound up some flax-root, and make a dressing of it, which he had tied under his terrible wound. <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,2/26,1983 ed.</i>)

### flax-stalk
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BAINES</b> When I had four made I fixed one to the top of a korari or flax-stalk, ... <i>(1874,Edward Crewe,209</i>)

>  <b>HOCKEN</b> ... their sole shelter [was] a little hut or breakwind constructed of koraris or flax stalks. <i>(1898,Contributions to the Early History of New Zealand,37</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> The heart ... was carried up country, stuck on the end of a korari stick (flax-stalk), and was taken to the Kuiti district. <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,2/470,1983 ed.</i>)

>  <b>KNIGHT</b> .. suddenly into his mind popped the soft translucent jelly substance found at the base of the flax stalks in the swamps. He'd often played with it - soft cool, even experimented with its edibility. ... Tumoti had gone to bed unwashed and unnoticed until the flax gum began to dry and contract ... <i>(1975,Poyntzfield,164</i>)

### flax-stick
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Got some dead flax sticks to boil a kettle with, and turned into our tent for the night. <i>(1856,Journal of Henry Sewell,2/215,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HARPER</b> In amongst the trees nestled the Maori village, huts built of Toi reeds, flax sticks, well thatched; <i>(Sep 1, 1857,Letters from Canterbury, NZ,27,1914 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> the river fell, and by tying bundles of flax sticks together on each side of the canoe in which the provisions had been brought up, we ventured down the stream with the flood. <i>(1861,Report on Topographical Exploration of Nelson,29</i>)

>  <b>HEINZ</b> The [at the Poerua River, West Coast] we found that the mokihi craft made of flax sticks, put together by a large party of diggers, was on the other side. <i>(1974,Bright Fine Gold,62</i>) [NOTES: >1866 June 9 Von Haast in 1974 Heinz Bright Fine Gold 62]

>  <b>BARKER</b> We ... look out for the last year's tall blossom of those horrid prickly bushes called `Spaniards,' or a bundle of flax-sticks, or ... dry Ti-ti palm. <i>(1867,Station Life in NZ,195,1870 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MONEY</b> ... we began to collect dry flax-sticks out of the swamp, ... and carrying them in bundles to the bank of the river, where the mogueys or rafts were to be launched when completed. [fn] Maori name for a raupo or flax-stick raft. <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,52</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> ... a flax-stick ... <i>(1873,Station Amusements in NZ,38</i>)

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> Affixing the plug of dynamite to the end of a big, slender flax-stick, ... it was carefully pushed along under the edge of the bank until it reached the scene of bobbing operations, and poked down about a foot below the surface of the water. <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,44,1935 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HART</b> ... the most delicious honey in the world ... was obtainable from the flax sticks. <i>(1886,Stray Leaves,31</i>)

>  <b>MOFFATT</b> [I] next day started building a moki to sail down to the outlet [of Lake Rotoroa]. I collected twelve big bundles of flax-sticks during the day, ... <i>(1896,Adventures by Sea and Land,68,1979 ed.</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> A flax-stick, or dried flower stem, is known to colonists as a korari, koradi, or kaladi. The first name is of course, the correct one ... <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,106,1940 p. 109 ed.</i>)

>  <b>DOBSON</b> Just before the gorge began there were many acres of flax, the dead flower stalks (flax sticks) of which made excellent rafts. <i>(1930,Reminiscences,85</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> The [Maori] tops would be whipped by means of a lash of flax tied to a bit of koradi (flax stick) and soon the fun grew fast and furious. <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,12</i>)

>  <b>HUNT</b> As far as the eye could see over the country on the western bank was a vast sea of flax sticks. The Lily tied up at the bank within a few yards of the mill. <i>(1951,Confessions,37</i>)

>  He will then ... mount to the very top of the flax-stick ... like a mouse or a house-fly. <i>(1895,TrNZI,28/373</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MONEY</b> ... we began to collect dry flax-sticks out of the swamp, ... and carrying them in bundles to the bank of the river, where the mogueys or rafts were to be launched when completed. [fn] Maori name for a raupo or flax-stick raft. <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,52</i>)

>  . a suggestion to build a mokihi (flax stick raft) was quickly put into execution. <i>(Apr 1904,NZ Illustrated Magazine,23</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> When we made that flax-stick raft and floated/ Down the Oreti creek, ... <i>(1979,Collected Poems,252,1962 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> He had sailed flax-stick boats on it often enough, and gaffed eels ... <i>(1985,Horse,11</i>) [NOTES: c1962 Baxter(Ghost in Trousers)]



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a dry flower stem of Phormium tenax, used by Maori and early European settlers for fences, raft-making and fire building. (See [korari](kiw-kwi.md#korari))

>  <b>WOODHOUSE</b> .. there was no boat, so they collected bundles of dry flax sticks, tied them together into a 'moki' and crossed the river on that. <i>(1988,Tales of Pioneer Women,119</i>)

>  <b>MONEY</b> .. we began to collect dry flax-sticks out of the swamp ... <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,52</i>)

>  <b>PASCOE</b> As they crossed the Karamea their flax sticks absorbed water and they had to throw their dog overboard to keep their mokihi above the Plimsoll line. <i>(1959,Great Days in NZ Exploration,44</i>)

### flax-trade
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Tauranga was in former times an important place for the pig and flax trade ... But from different causes the flax-trade has dwindled ... away <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/407</i>)

### flax-trader
 
 <i>n.</i> [(hist.)]

>  <b>MANING</b> Trading in flax in those days [1822-1826] was to be undertaken by a man who had his wits about him; an old flax trader of those days, with his 150 ton schooner `out of Sydney', cruising all round the coast of New Zealand, picking up his five tons at one port, ... and so on ... <i>(1863,Old New Zealand,(xiv) 211</i>)

>  <b>MILLER</b> This concentration of power in the Cook Strait region and the acquisition of firearms from the whalers and flax-traders at Kapiti encouraged Te Rauparaha to embark on the conquest of the South Island. <i>(1958,Early Victorian NZ,18</i>)

>  The first European visitors to Wairoa ... were flax traders. <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/522</i>)

>  <b>BOYD</b> European flax traders and whalers began to visit the Bay in the 1830s and several shore-whaling stations were established <i>(1984,City of the Plains,4</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

 <i>n.</i> flax-cutter; also, a worker in a flax mill [(obs) ? ca1951 Farmers' Weekly [p?] [Noted by R. Mason as a worker in a flax-swamp]]

>  <b>DEVANNY</b> <i>(1928,Dawn Beloved,29</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> flaxie flax worker, as obsolete as the industry making rope from flax. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,45</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a flax-cutter [later used to cover workers in flax mills.]

### fleece
 
 <i>n.</i> body wool shorn from a sheep [(station)]

>  Fleece: (1) The whole of a sheep's wool. (Canterbury Rhymes) (2) The main part of a sheep's wool, which is picked up in one, skirted and rolled. This is sold as fleece wool, as opposed to locks, bellies and pieces. (C.) <i>(Oct 21, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  Fleece: Body wool shorn from a sheep. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,55</i>)

>  <b>OGONOWSKA-COATES</b> Fleece. The main part of the sheep's wool when shorn. It is picked up in one piece and carried to the wool table, where it is skirted and classed. The small bits and pieces that are cut off separately by the shearer are the locks, bellies and pieces. <i>(1987,Boards, Blades & Barebellies,96</i>)

>  fleece: On the sheep the fleece is all the wool, but once shorn it does not have the `belly wool' and other locks which are packed separately. <i>(1989,NZ English Newsletter,3/24</i>) [NOTES: (Wallace Shearing Glossary)]



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i>

>  The blade shearers fleece the sheep quickly <i>(1945,High Country,24</i>) [NOTES: >1945 Pascoe Canterbury High Country NZ Geol 24]

### fleece boy
 
 <i>n.</i> a fleece-picker

>  Commenced shearing. Meikana and Piripi Nehu as press men stuck me up. I gave them five minutes and sacked them. ... I put on young Wood as pressman as he was near at hand ... and put on my two station boys as fleece boys. The shed did not stop one minute. <i>Ibid.</i> 4 Nov 5 p. 425 I am to keep and pay a cook ... 1 roller who expects to roll 1000 fleeces a day ..., one fleece boy at 5/- per diem, 2 press men ... <i>(Nov 4, 1889,Chudleigh Diary,368,1950 ed.</i>)

### fleece king
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an owner of a large flock of sheep.

>  There are no fewer than 189 fleece kings in Napier-Gisborne who own flocks of from 5000 to 10,000 sheep, ninety-one who own from 10,000 to 20,000 sheep and thirty-one who have over 20,000 head of sheep. <i>(Jul 1911,NZ Farmer,761</i>)

### fleece picker
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a shedhand who picks up shorn fleeces from a shearing board. (See [picker](per-pop.md#picker))

>  <b>DEANS</b> Two fleece pickers handled the wool from the twenty shearers, a considerable feat ... <i>(1964,Pioneers on Port Cooper Plains,113</i>)

>  <b>SWANTON</b> There are the boys to pick up the fleeces, one fleece-picker to every four or five shearers. <i>(1892,Notes on NZ,96</i>)

>  In addition to three shearers, there are two baling, two at the wool table, a fleece picker, one sweeping and a man penning. <i>(May 15, 1945,NZ Journal of Agriculture,502</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> This meant long carrying for the fleece-pickers at the bottom end ... <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island,29</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The woolroom and tables are situated at one end, and the fleece pickers must have to cover a few miles in the day. <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island,209</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> fleecie or fleece-o or fleecepicker: in the small sheds he may also act as broomi.e. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,31</i>)

>  <b>MARTIN</b> In 1876 Tripp wanted twenty-four shearers, two fleece-pickers, three fleece-rollers and several shedhands. [fleece-picker] <i>(1990,Forgotten Worker, The</i>)

### fleece roller
 
 <i>n.</i> [cf wool roller]

>  Paid Wm. Gardiner, fleece roller, [pounds]11 7s 0d. <i>(Mar 1868,Puketoi Station Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1868 March Puketoi Station Diary in 1947 Beattie Early Runholding 104]



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a member of shearing gang who works on the wool table.

>  <b>CHUDLEIGH</b> eight shearers, one packer, one fleece roller, one picker-up. <i>(Dec 23, 1868,Chudleigh Diary</i>)

>  <b>RIDDOLLS</b> They [necks and first pieces] should be placed where the classer or fleece roller can throw them straight into the bins without turning. <i>(1958,Farm Engineering,354</i>)

>  <b>MARTIN</b> In 1876 Tripp wanted twenty-four shearers, two fleece-pickers, three fleece-rollers and several shedhands. [fleece-roller] <i>(1990,Forgotten Worker, The</i>)

### fleece rot
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a discoloration in fleecewool that is not actually caused by rot.

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> The name fleece rot for this condition [banded discoloration on back wool] in the fleece is rather an unfortunate one because it is unusual for fibres to be 'rotten' or otherwise damaged. <i>(1968,Growing Better Wool,59</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> There is evidence also that sheep differ in their degree of susceptibility to fleece rot even when the skin is wet. <i>(1968,Growing Better Wool,60</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> These banded stains are sometimes found in association with fleece rot. <i>(1968,Growing Better Wool,72</i>)

>  High open country with plenty of wind also reduces the likelihood of excessive moisture and fleece rot. <i>(Dec 14, 1988,NZ Farmer,31</i>)

### fleece wool
 
 <i>n.</i> [1891 Wallace Rural Econ 1951 Acland]

>  When a clip is received by the store for classing, all the fleece wool goes to the fleece classing-room which in most stores is on a different floor from the `bin room' where all the pieces and oddments are sent. <i>(1936,Agricultural Organization in NZ,711</i>)

### fleece-carrier
 
 <i>n.</i> a fleeco

>  <b>WOODHOUSE</b> [fn] In the 90s ... about 55 men were employed [on Benmore] in the shed: 28-30 shearers, 2 penners-up, 4 pickers-up, 2 broomies, 6 rollers, 1 classer, 1 fleece-carrier, ... <i>(1950,Farm & Station Verse,60</i>) [NOTES: (George Meek)]

### fleece-pick
 
 <i>v.</i> to work as a fleece-picker [1933 Acland see fleece-picker]



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to pick fleeces from a shearing board to throw on to the wool table.

>  <b>ACLAND</b> fleece-pick: the verb to pick means 'to be a fleece-picker'. In most sheds the fleece-picker also sweeps. <i>(1951,Early Canterbury Runs,378</i>)

### fleece-picker
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HARPER</b> Shearers, fleecepickers.. cooks and slushies.. answered to their names <i>(1967,Kettle on the Fuchsia, The,91</i>)

>  He smiled benignly on the fleece-picker. <i>(Dec 2, 1897,Otago Witness,58</i>)

>  <b>CARR</b> Wool Rolling. - When the fleece is thrown on the table by the fleece-picker, the wool rollers (generally there are two) skirt - i.e., take off the dirty wool - the neck (in some sheds) and legs, ... turn in the sides and roll up, from each end. The Classer, by feel, sight, and general knowledge, decides what class it belongs to. <i>(1913,Country Work,15</i>)

>  <b>WOODHOUSE</b> There were two fleece-pickers, one for each board of shearers. <i>(1982,Blue Cliffs,89</i>)

### fleece-picking
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>CARR</b> Fleece-picking. - As soon as a shearer has taken the fleece off and let the sheep go through the port-hole to the counting-out pen, the picker gathers the fleece up in such a manner that she or he can on getting to the rolling table, throw it out with the breech part at the right end. <i>(1913,Country Work,14</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the picking up of fleeces from a shearing board.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> .. I took on my first proper job. This was fleece-picking for Charlie Leech at Wai-iti ... <i>(1971,Ten Thousand Dogs,18</i>)

>  <b>STACEY</b> I had learned quite a lot about shed work when we were at Kekerengu and was enjoying penning up, fleece picking, skirting and rolling when Whit asked if I would like to have a go. <i>(1996,Tales of a Tussock Jumper,51</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Lloyd Ewing first worked on Cattle Flat in 1931, fleece-picking ... <i>(1975,Sixty Thousand on the Hoof,133</i>)

>  <b>WATERS</b> Fleece-picking during shearing operations was a job I loved doing ... <i>(1976,Mostly a Farmer,11</i>)

### fleecer
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an unshorn sheep.

>  <b>Kerr</b> Duncraig's big fleecers shed wool like wild sheep. <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country,81</i>)

### fleecie
 
 <i>n.</i> a person who picks up fleeces immediately after shearing. (shearers') spg also fleecy (See [also fleeco](fin-fly.md#fleeco))

>  <b>WILSON</b> Meanwhile the shorn fleeces are carried to a large table in the wool-shed by a man appropriately named `Fleecy', and are spread out by four wool-pickers, who tear away the bad parts and fold the fleeces square, passing them to the classer, who, in turn, after a rapid examination hands them on to his attendant, and he places them in their respective bins according to their quality. <i>(1894,In the Land of the Tui,244</i>)

>  <b>LANCASTER</b> The fleecies pattered the lines unendingly. <i>(1904,Sons o' Men,78,1905 ed.</i>)

>  Fleecy: Short name for fleece picker. (C.) <i>(Oct 21, 1933,Press, The,15</i>)

>  <b>STRONACH</b> The `fleecies', whose job was to pick up the fleeces, to sweep the board, and to apply tar to the cut sheep, clattered up and down. <i>(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,30</i>)

>  fleecie n. N.Z. a person who collects fleeces after shearing and prepares them for baling. Also called: fleece-oh. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,424</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 286, under FLEECE-PICKER; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 187]]

>  <b>OGONOWSKA-COATES</b> Fleecy. Shortened name for fleece picker; also known as `fleece-oh'. <i>(1987,Boards, Blades & Barebellies,96</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> fleeco/fleecy shearing hand who picks up the shorn fleeces; late C19. ANZ. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,46</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [variation of fleeco] a fleece-worker in a wool-shed.

>  <b>MOMATIUK & EASTCOTT</b> A tap of a small hoof and the footstep of 'fleecees' carrying snowy bundles to the classing-table. <i>(1980,High Country,73</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The call of 'wool away' had lagging fleecies dashing ... <i>(1949,High Country Days,53</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> .. the shorn fleeces are carried to a large table in the woolshed by a man appropriately named 'Fleecy ' ... <i>(1894,In the Land of the Tui,244</i>)

>  <b>STRONACH</b> The fleecies whose job was to pick up the fleeces, to sweep the board, and apply tar to the cut sheep, chattered up and down. <i>(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,30</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> As in the Lagoon Hill shed the fleecies and broomies take turns about doing the bottom end ... <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island,210</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> The fleecie picks up the fleece in such a way that he can throw it out on the wool-table, shorn side down, completely spread out for skirting and rolling. [fleecee; fleecy] <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,32</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McKEE WRIGHT</b> They're going sheep and sheep, .../ And the fleecy boys are kept upon the run. <i>(1897,Station Ballads</i>) [NOTES: 1897 McKee Wright Station Ballads `Shears' in 1950 Woodhouse Farm and Station Verse 33]

### fleeco
 
 <i>n.</i> a person who handles freshly shorn fleeces in a shearing shed (See [also fleecie](fin-fly.md#fleecie))

>  At five the engine throbbed into sudden silence, and the shearers, leaving the Maori fleece-oh's to cope with the disorder of the great shed, streamed on. <i>(Dec 7, 1935,Christchurch Star-Sun,Magazine Section 1</i>)

>  managed to shear a few, mostly by the kindly aid of the "fleeco". <i>(Jan 28, 1943,NZ Farmers' Weekly,4</i>)

>  I watched in silent admiration as the "fleeco" gathered up the fleece, took it to the table and, with a toss and a flip, laid it flat across its width. <i>(Oct 15, 1947,NZ Observer,21</i>)

>  <b>MARSH</b> I reckoned the fleecies had got into the shed when we were over at the dance. <i>(1945,Died in the Wool,153</i>) [NOTES: 1948 EDITION]

>  What really gave <i>The Learner's Stand</i> lift-off, however, was a conversation Geary had with actor Hori Ahipene (<i>Skitz</i>) who said they had drag queens for fleecos in Hawke's Bay. <i>(Apr 22, 1995,Listener (New Zealand Listener),44</i>)

>  <b>OWEN</b> A young Maori boy acted as `Fleece-oh!' ... He [?] flicked the fleece up in his arms when it was cut from the sheep, grasped it by its four ends, and flung it out for Loveluck to fold. <i>(1909,Philip Loveluck,173</i>)

>  Shearing was in full swing at the station and each intended obtaining a job as `fleece-o' and remover of `dags' or some such occupation. <i>(Jan 1, 1910,NZ Truth,236/6/1</i>)

>  <b>LUSK</b> ... the wife of Kuru (Airini) to act as `Fleeco.' <i>(1934,Waimana,195</i>)

>  <i>(Sep 10, 1942,NZ Farmers' Weekly,35</i>)

>  <b>BOWEN</b> A common fault is for a wool-table to be too high, which makes harder work for the table hands and the `fleeco'. <i>Ibid.</i> (glossary: Shearing Terms) 156 FLEECO. The man who picks up fleeces and who is sometimes abused by shearers. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,92</i>)

>  <b>MILLS</b> Two shearers ... were ... acting as fleece-os - picking up the newly shorn fleeces and sweeping the board clear. <i>(1960,Sheep-O,27</i>)

>  Fleece-o: A person who picks up fleeces from the shearing board and delivers them to the sorting table. <i>(Feb 26, 1965,Listener (New Zealand Listener),15</i>) [NOTES: (H.A.L. Laing]

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> She was a fleeco in a shearing shed. <i>(1965,Among the Cinders,206</i>)

>  FLEECE-O: A person who picks up the fleeces from the shearing board. <i>(Feb 21, 1968,Straight Furrow,20</i>)

>  <b>FERNANDEZ</b> Not to worry that they were short of two fleeco's, that a truck had broken down right on a bad bend of the Gentle Annie . <i>(1973,Tussock Fever,4</i>)

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> I remember last summer ... we had a gang on our place and the fleeco was a girl of fifteen or sixteen, ... <i>(1974,Maori Woman,245</i>)

>  <b>MIDDLETON</b> [He] told them I was the new fleeco ... <i>(1959,Stone, The,42</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> fleeco/fleecy shearing hand who picks up the shorn fleeces; late C19.ANZ. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,46</i>)

>  Acting as fleeco, being able to scoop up and fold a fleece in the correct manner, approach the table, throw it so it spread well, to settle perfectly was satisfying. <i>(Jun 2001,NZ Memories,No. 30/43</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a member of a shearing gang who scoops up shorn fleeces and throws them on the wool table.

>  <b>ALLEN</b> I was fleeco, billy-boy and rouseabout. <i>(1980,Farm on the Wanganui River, A,39</i>)

>  <b>RIDDOLLS</b> The bins for locks, second pieces, and bellies should be as close as possible to the board, because the 'fleece-o' puts these straight into the bins. <i>(1958,Farm Engineering,354</i>)

>  <b>SALISBURY</b> As each shearer shore off the belly a fleeco would pick it up and put it in the belly fadge. <i>(1994,I Was So Lucky,25</i>)

>  <b>HINDMARSH</b> ... several of the family joined the gang as sheares or fleecos and Bill took over the role as 'ganger.' <i>(1997,Come Be a Pioneer,46</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i> to work as a fleeco

>  <b>MIDDLETON</b> Last time I heard of you, you were fleecoing for Dad. <i>(1964,Walk on the Beach,204</i>) [NOTES: (Not for a Seagull)]

### Fleet
 
 <i>n.</i> [attrib. use]

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> ... the evidence seems to indicate that the bow and arrow may have been known to our early Moa-hunter people and later rejected by the Fleet immigrants. <i>(1966,Maori Life and Custom,105</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> the Great Fleet q.v.

>  <b>LYSNAR</b> The calabash, the kumara, the taro, and the yam, were carried in the fleet. <i>(1924,New Zealand (various authors/titles),20</i>)

>  When the `fleet' of canoes `Arawa,' `Tainui.' and others coming from Hawaike arrived at the East Cape about 1350 A.D. many of them came on to Hauraki ... <i>(1936,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,17/465</i>)

>  <b>BUICK</b> [fn]The accepted tradition is that the migration of `the fleet' ... took place about A.D. 1350, not A.D. 1524 as Mr. McKay assumes. <i>Ibid.</i> 226 That the glamour of migration continued down to historical times is suggested ... by the coming, in approximately A.D. 1350, of what is known as `the fleet' of eight canoes. <i>(1937,Moa-Hunters of NZ,75</i>)

>  <b>WAITE</b> [S. Percy Smith's Polynesian dates] 1250 to 1325 A.D. - Voyages of the second Kupe, Ngahue, and others. 1350 A.D. - New Zealand settled by the `Fleet'. <i>(1940,Port Molyneux,28</i>)

>  <b>BUCK</b> It is thus five and a-half [sic] centuries ago or approximately A.D.1350 when the Fleet crossed the Great Ocean of Kiwa and made their landfall at Aotearoa. <i>(1949,Coming of the Maori, The,8</i>)

>  <b>MILLER</b> When the famous canoes of the fleet - Tainui, Tokomaru, Aotea, Kurahaupo, Takitimu - made their landfall in New Zealand about the time of the Battle of Crecy [e-acute], ... <i>(1958,Early Victorian NZ,13</i>)

### Fleet Maori
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HALL-JONES</b> The Ngai-tahu were descendants of the Fleet Maoris ... <i>(1945,Historical Southland,64</i>)

### fleet up
 
 <i>v.</i> [sc. timber]

>  <b>HARGREAVES</b> When you've got a heap of mud in a trawl, you can't get the whole net in because of the weight, so you've got to put a strop round it, lift a bit up, empty that, then put another strop on, and get that bit out, and so on. It's called fleeting up, and you've got to do that about five times to get it all on board. <i>(1998,On the Next Tide,42</i>)

>  <b>MOORE</b> Sometimes it was milled in a bush mill at that point [sc. the hauler]. Ofte[n] it was `fleeted up' on the skids ready for loading on to a bush tramway, the motive power for which was supplied either by draught animals or small steam locomotives. <i>(1969,From Forest to Farm,38</i>)

### flenser
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>GRADY</b> [glossary] Flenser - Man who strips the blubber off. flesh -->to remove flesh from hides for the processing of leather. <i>(1982,Perano Whalers,227</i>)

### flesh-footed shearwater
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  By far the most plentiful amongst the bird community at Karewa is the flesh-footed shearwater (<i>Hemipuffinus carneipes</i>), or taonui of the Maoris. <i>(1924,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,7/183</i>) [NOTES: (Sladden)]

>  FLESH-FOOTED SHEARWATER, or TAONUI (<i>Puffinus carneipes</i>). <i>(1925,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,8/208</i>)

>  <i>Flesh-footed Shearwater Puffinus carneipes</i> GOULD, 1844 ... OTHER NAMES:<i>Pale-footed shearwater, toanui.</i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,95</i>)

### fleshfish
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Dermatopsis macrodon</i>

>  <b>AYLING</b> FLESHFISH <i>Dermatopsis macrodon</i> ... The fleshfish is a small cod-like fish ... They have been found in New Zealand and southern Australia around rock reefs at low tide level and in shallow coastal waters. <i>(1982,Collins Guide,1984,153 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] fleshfish 861 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

### fleshing
 
 <i>n.</i> [(freezingworks, fellmongers')]

>  The process of fleshing is usually carried out by machines operating a right-and-left spiral knife-cylinder ... flesh side to the knife cylinder. A process similar to fleshing is also carried out in order to remove as much as possible of the hair root-sheaths and fat glands upon the grain side of the pelt. This process is known as `scudding', the skins being fed into the machine, grain side to the knife cylinder. <i>(Aug 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,4/159</i>) [NOTES: (Wright)]

>  Here the hide is fleshed or flayed by hand to remove any surplus fat and meat left on in butchering ... These fleshings are sent to the tallow department ... <i>(Sep 15, 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,258</i>)

### fleshy-leaved lobelia
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Lobelia roughii. </i>

>  (And) 2. fleshy-leaved lobelia, <i> Lobelia Roughii. </i> <i>(1910,AJHR,C-14</i>) [NOTES: COCKAYNE Botanical Survey Waipoua Kauri Forest]

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> <i> Lobelia Roughii </i> <i>(1910,NZ Plants,2nd ed.</i>)

### flex bed
 
 <i>n.</i> hospital jargon for beds occasionally used

>  Contrary to what the name suggested, "flex beds" were not a new tool for physiotherapists, but the "converse to resource beds". Eh? That is beds occasionally "flexed" into use, versus those always available for use. <i>(Feb 21, 2002,Dominion,3</i>)

### flex-price
 
 <i>adj.</i> flexible price

>  Why hasn't a generator/retailer introduced such a flex-price option already? <i>(May 10, 2003,Dominion Post,C2</i>)

### flick
 
 <i>n.</i> friend, mate

>  <b>THATCHER</b> No doubt you frequently will meet, A Chinaman going down the street, With slang expressions then to greet This cove to yield you joy. Say to him, `How are you, old flick?' <i>(1875,NZ Songster,No.5/63</i>) [NOTES: The Leary Boy]



 
 <i>v.</i> to abandon, dump (See [give the flick to](g-go.md#give-the-flick-to))

>  That doesn't mean that you flick Changing Rooms, for example, because there is an audience for programmes like that. <i>(Feb 16, 2002,Evening Post,25</i>)

>  <b>Owen Marshall</b> 'They'll get a three-bedroom, brick place in one of the hill suburbs, and flick friends like us.' <i>(2009,Living As A Moon,154</i>)

### flick one's wick
 
 <i>phr.</i> to get a move on, hurry up

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Plenty of times they flicked me with wet towels in the changing shed at the baths ...] <i>Ibid.</i> 189 `Another one won't hurt,' I say, but he doesn't take the hint. `When we get there perhaps,' he says. `Come on boy. Flick your wick. Can't waste it'. He pours me another. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,11</i>)

### flickers
 
 <i>n.</i> constr. with the, the pictures, cinema [ca1933 Southland]

### flicks
 
 <i>n.</i> ?? [schoolchildren's]

>  I have also had a report of `creases' and what to me were two novelties, `flicks' and `skinch'. <i>(Feb 5, 1983,Listener (New Zealand Listener),72</i>) [NOTES: [I.A. Gordon]]

### flier
 
 <i>n.</i> an itinerant or extra conductor to service rush-hour crowds; a flying conductor q.v. [(tramways now obs) ca1951-6 Wellington]

### flies eyes
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) protective goggles worn by pighunters to protect their eyes from gouging.

>  <b>SWAN</b> Light, wire mesh goggles, 'flies eyes,' prevent one's eyes being gouged. <i>(1992,Grunter Hunters, The,11</i>)

### flimsy
 
 <i>n.</i> an aerogramme



 
 <i>n.</i> a one-pound note

>  Following are other local money-names: - 1 pound note - flimsy, rag or carpet ... <i>(Jan 14, 1899,Sydney Bulletin,(Red Page)</i>) [NOTES: (Letter from Loafer, Tauranga) [Bill Pearson]]

>  The pound note of pre-decimal days was varously [sic] known as a quid, nicker, smacker, flimsy, rag, and carpet. The latter term was apparently peculiar to New Zealand and Australia. <i>(Oct 1977,NZ Numismatic Journal,16</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a pigeongram message

### flinch
 
 <i>v.</i> var. of flench/flense, to strip the blubber from a whale or seal.

>  <b>GRADY</b> [glossary] Flense - To strip the blubber off a whale. Flinch means the same thing. Flinch - Same as flense. <i>(1982,Perano Whalers,227</i>)

>  <b>BELL</b> If the fishing is to be carried on by a shore party, the try pots and huts are erected on the beach ... The boats are sent out at daylight every morning, and when ... they kill a fish it is towed ashore and flinched and boiled upon the beach. <i>(Nov 1831,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1831 Nov Bell Letter in 1913 McNab Old Whaling Days 9]

### flindersia
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>flindersia n </i>[NL, fr. Matthew Flinders + NL -ia] 1 cap : a small genus of pinnate-leaved Australasian trees (family Meliaceae) having white flowers followed by woody capsular fruits and yielding strong hardwood lumber, often with an excellent figure, that is used in cabinetmaking and construction - see FLINDOSA 2 -s : any tree of the genus <i>Flindersia</i> <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,870</i>)

### fling
 
 <i>n.</i> person one has a fling with

>  I want to go back to Europe to see my fling. I met him in Melbourne and we had a fling for four days. <i>(Apr 28, 1997,Salient,14</i>)

### flinty
 
 <i>n.</i> a vein of flinty quartz [goldmining; as flinty leader/vein, one indicating a gold-bearing reef (? mainly Thames-Coromandel fields) 1907]

>  This country is traversed by "flinties" (small veins of cherty quartz). <i>(1898,AJHR,C-9/22</i>)

>  At the intersections of cross-veins, flinties, and `flatheads', the mingling of waters that have come from different Sources ... has resulted in precipitation of the gold and its associated minerals ... [173] ... In the lower levels the patches of highly auriferous ore generally occurred at the intersectons of `flinties' with the veins ... <i>(1907,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,No.4 (NS) 106</i>) [NOTES: (Fraser and Adams, Coromandel)]

>  The nearest parallel to the `flinties' or `indicator' veins so common at Thames is seen in the Tairua Broken Hills Mine. Here a small flinty leader ... intersected certain of the reefs ... [82] ... The richer material occurred ... also ... where `cross-heads' or `flinties' joined the main reef. <i>(1912,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,No.15 (NS) 64</i>) [NOTES: (Bell and Fraser, Waihi-Tairua)]

### flip
 
 <i>exclam.</i> [(schoolboys' exclam.) ca1961 June (University Stage I English student's essay) 1960 17C F B10 Napier Girls H.S. 5 FLIP]



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Q. If you ordered a "flip" in a South Island takeaway bar, what would you get? A. A potato fritter. <i>(Dec 27, 1994,Evening Post,13</i>) [NOTES: This was a question on 27 Dec., answer given on 28 Dec. p. 14]



 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to break up hard soil pan by turning up underlying free-draining soil.

>  One way in which it [the development programme] will do this is to 'hump and hollow' some of the pakihi soils and to 'flip' pasture. The plan at this stage is to turn all those properties that are able to be flipped or humped and hollowed into dairying. <i>(Oct 25, 2001,Taranaki Daily News,19</i>)

### flip-flap
 
 <i>n.</i> the spotted shag

>  Spotted Shag <i>Stictocarbo punctatus</i>(SPARRMAN, 1786) ... Spotted Shag<i> S. p. punctatus</i> (SPARRMAN, 1786). Blue Shag <i>S.p. steadi</i> (OLIVER, 1930). ... OTHER NAMES: Parekareka, ocean shag, crested shag, flip-flap, blue shag. <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,125</i>)

>  The spotted shag, or flip-flap, well known to our shore folk, is stated by ornithologists to be peculiar to New Zealand; its active movements enliven many a bluff headland or rocky inlet of our island coast line... In calm autumn days often have we watched the still waters of our shallow bays flash with the swift motion of the flip-flap. <i>(1873,TrNZI,V/201</i>)

### flipper
 
 <i>n.</i> a flap-jack [Otago]

>  <b>AYSON</b> Thomas tried his hand at baking flippers, with flour, water and a little salt for the ingredients, and treacle or sugar to sweeten the taste after the cooking was complete. <i>(1937,Thomas,108</i>)

### flipperjack
 
 <i>n.</i> a flap-jack [(? rare, obs)]

>  <b>FOSTON</b> He has now become a crack hand at making `doe-boys' and `Flipperjacks.' <i>(1911,In the Bell Bird's Lair,47</i>)

### flipping
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the breaking up of hard soil pans by bringing the underlying free-draining soils from 1 metre below ground to the surface.

>  I have recently done a small amount of work with Agriculture NZ and some of the dairy farmers in the Cape Foulwind area looking at the changes in soils from flipping. <i>(May 31, 2002,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: pc with Dr Craig Ross, Landcare Research:]

>  The most innovative soil improvement technique to be used in the conversions will be pasture flipping - a practice pioneered at Cape Foulwind to bring free-draining soils to the surface and turn sour wetland into lush pasture .. A group of Cape Foulwind dairy farmers, together with Agriculture New Zealand consultant Richard Reynolds, and Landcare Research Scientist Dr Craig Ross, are evaluating flipping or breaking up the pakihi iron pan to bring free-draining soil to the top. The technique was developed by a local farmer, the late Fred King, and his son Alex, and costs between $2500 and $5000 a hectare. <i>(Oct 25, 2001,Taranaki Daily News,19</i>)

### Flit
 
 <i>n.</i> a brand of insect spray

>  <b>KEITH</b> Flies could be kept at bay with Flit in a patent, pump action spray. <i>(1991,Lovely Day Tomorrow, A,44</i>)

>  [Advt] Flit is sure death to insects because it is a combination of potent killing agents which cannot be excelled... Flit spray will not stain and is harmless to humans. <i>(Feb 1, 1940,Otago Daily Times,15</i>)

### Flit-gun
 
 <i>n.</i> a dispenser of Flit q.v.

>  <b>MASON</b> ANA. What's that in her hand? Looks like - Yeh! a flit-gun. PERA. Flit-gun? Sure does look like a flit-gun! <i>(1969,Awatea,19,1978 ed.</i>)

### flitch
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>CARR</b> Old fashioned `Breast' benches (where flitches from the breaking-down saw are cut into commercial sizes) are still used in many mills. <i>(1913,Country Work,40</i>)

>  <b>BIRD</b> As the first slice, known as a flitch, comes off the saws, the off-bearer holds it with a cant hook until it has passed the pony bench conveyor chains. <i>(1998,Viaducts against the Sky,38</i>)

### flitcher
 
 <i>n.</i> [(sawmillers')]

>  <b>MARRIOTT</b> Face cuts, by the way, are the first boards to come off the flitcher. <i>(1981,Life in the Gorge,85</i>)

### flitching
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  [Les Cleveland] FLITCHING: a method of draining blood from slaughtered cattle after they have been PITHED. The animals become unconscious after spinal cord is severed. <i>(Mar 13, 1906,NZ Farmers' Weekly,9</i>)

### float
 
 <i>n.</i> loose pieces of quartz

>  Float: Loose pieces of quartz shed from a reef outcrop by weathering and found some distance away. <i>(1978,Gold Mining at Waihi (Glossary),322</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> sc. pennies, in two up (? Aust.)

>  <b>LAWSON</b> Well, everything was going well till ... spun two coins very high - trying to `float' them - that means land them without spinning them more than twice. <i>(1851,Gold in their Hearts</i>) [NOTES: [? 1870s]]



 
 <i>n.</i> a vehicle for transporting horses [horse float Jim Cameron writes: Collins gives this as <i>Austral & NZ</i>. NSOD defines <i>float</i> in this context as a broad level shallow means of transportation and also as a low bodied battery-powered vehicle for delivering milk ("also <i>milk float</i>"). The NZ horse float is a high and narrow trailer. The first NSOD definition describes what in the 309s and 40s was known to me as a milk float - a low flat horse-drawn trailer.] (See [horse float](hom-hyp.md#horse-float))

>  <b>KEITH</b> One suburban sound of the 1940s, now completely vanished from New Zealand streets, was that of food vendors: the rabbito and the whitebait seller and the clip-clopping hooves of the milko's horse-drawn float - as the milk waggon was called. <i>(1991,Lovely Day Tomorrow, A,41</i>)

>  float ... 15. Austral. & N.Z. a vehicle for transporting horses. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,426</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1986 NZPOD; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 188]]

### float her
 
 <i>exclam.</i> A watersiders' term to lift a sling of cargo just high enough to enable men to inspect the sling or to swing it into a favourable position. [1951 Letter from Pugh-Williams]

### float up to
 
 <i>v.</i> (to a person, group) to accost casually

>  ... `ducks' breakfast,' `to float up to,' `to blow up to,' `to sleep in the Star Hotel' need no explanation. <i>(Apr 4, 1938,Press, The</i>) [NOTES: McNab NZ Slang]

### float-up
 
 <i>n.</i> a casual approach

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> Float-up. A person's casual approach: New Zealanders': C. 20. Ex: v to float up to <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,287</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> float up casual approach, unexpected arrival or departure; ref. Gordon Slatter's A Gun in My Hand. NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,46</i>)

### floater
 
 <i>n.</i> specks of gold thin enough to float in water in the pan /cf flake/ [goldmining(Otago)]

>  <b>WASHBOURN</b> .. the gold ... [was] so much of a gold-leaf character ... I have frequently seen large specks floating in and out of the dish when panning out there, and these in digging terms are `floaters.' <i>(1933,Reminiscences of Early Days,4</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a fragment of ore (one of many) detached and carried away from the main reef

>  The loose auriferous rubble and `floaters' of rich stone mined on the Lord Brassey claim were part of this great mass of slumped material. <i>(1948,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,No.42 (NS) 41</i>) [NOTES: (Gage, Reefton)]



 
 <i>n.</i> a hard bar, a bar of non-gold-bearing rock intersecting a vein of ore [goldmining(Thames)]

>  Such occurrences, however, are but analogous to the `hard bars' or `floaters' of the Thames mines, and to the cores of fairly fresh andesite and dacite found inset in masses of highly altered rock. <i>(1912,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,No.15 (NS) 55</i>) [NOTES: (Bell and Fraser, Waihi-Tairua)]



 
 <i>n.</i> a teacher without an allocated classroom

>  ... But classroom construction hasn't kept pace, so the teachers become `floaters' or teachers without classrooms. <i>(Nov 13, 1974,Dominion,6/4</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a pie floating in gravy

>  <b>McGILL</b> floater a pie floating in gravy served up traditionally by piecarts and cafeterias along with peas and lumpy mashed potato. NZA. - a turd that will not flush away. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,46</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> an unflushed piece of faeces

>  "Your "Currantly Bran" is marvellous for me - I'm now producing the most regular Fluffy Floaters!"... In case you haven't cottoned on, "Fluffy Floaters" are linked to bran consumption in - to put it delicately - a "cause and effect" manner. <i>(2000,Hubbard's 'Clipboard',[4]</i>)

>  Then there are the little things that annoy some cleaners but amuse others: used condoms under the bed, "floaters" in the toilet that refuse to flush, arriving early to find the householder or their teenager in the bedroom with a lover. <i>(Jan 28, 1991,Listener (New Zealand Listener),13</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [bush-felling] [see sailer] a branch that breaks off and falls.

>  <b>STACEY</b> As a tree was falling away from the others these 'floaters' would be released to drop down on top of you. We never spoke to each other when working, only to warn of floaters or any other danger. <i>(1996,Tales of a Tussock Jumper,49</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  He hadn't won a match since May 2003. What a dangerous floater he proved. <i>(Feb 21, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),37</i>)

### floating crane
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Initially there were problems mooring the floating crane <i>Hikinui</i> because a pile snapped when the crane strained against it. <i>(Aug 2004,NZ Memories,No. 49/55</i>)

### floating duckweed
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Lemna minor </i>

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> (And) <i>(1919,NZ Plants & Their Story</i>)

### floating grazing unit
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a group of sheep or cattle that is moved frequently to eat surplus feed.

>  <b>STEVENS</b> In some areas cattle will be carried as a floating grazing unit or 'set-stocked' to deal with surplus growth. <i>(1958,Sheep.  Part 1, Sheep husbandry,59</i>)

### floating line
 
 <i>n.</i> a strong cored fishing line

>  Floating line: Also 'Shooting Head' and 'Slow Sink'. A strong cored line that the tippet attaches to; most fishermen have all three on separate spools in their kit. <i>(Nov 28, 2004,Sunday  Star Times: Escape,12</i>)

### floatstone
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>NOLAN</b> Floatstone: loose quartz in a river bed, usually remote from its parent lode. <i>(1983,Gold Fossicker's Handbook,112</i>)

### floatwood
 
 <i>n.</i> driftwood

>  <b>McLEAN</b> Some driftwood is found on the banks of the Awatere, sufficient to keep some of the Stations in firewood. Others get floatwood on the beach. <i>(Jan 31, 1856,Journal,IV</i>)

### flock
 
 <i>n.</i> (of sheep) as a collective for the total sheep of a farm etc. -->1981 the official name for a group of two or more sheep with a single owner. (flock to be distinguished from mob - a number of sheep running together.)

>  <b>REVANS</b> I am not yet confident of the mode in which flock and stock musters will be dealt with by the natives <i>(1841,Letter,90</i>) [NOTES: 1841 Revans to Chapman 90]

>  <b>WEEKES</b> I obtained [the Cotswold rams], and my island produced the first long-woolled flock in New Zealand. <i>(1846,My Island</i>) [NOTES: c1846 Weekes My Island in 1940 Establishment of New Plymouth Settlement]

>  He had selected a sheep from a flock travelling from Motenau to Rhodes'. <i>(Feb 6, 1850,Torlesse Papers,129,1958 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WELD</b> The produce of 2000 ewes soon amounts to a large flock; <i>(1851,Hints to Intending Sheep-Farmers in New Zealand,5</i>)

>  A sheep-owner need not reside on his run, but live in a more civilised district, and visit his flock at times. ... It would take [pounds]150 to set the sheep-station going well, [184] with wool-shed, &c.; <i>(Mar 1855,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1855 Mar Letter in 1856 Fitton NZ 183-184]

>  <b>FITTON</b> This increased accommodation, for their growing flocks, will be of great benefit to stockowners of Wellington ... <i>(1856,New Zealand (various authors/titles),?p[84-88]</i>)

>  Duncan's flock buisted and ear marked. <i>Ibid.</i> 1860 Mar 9 [cf -->mob] Duncan mustered home flock [10] Duncan's flock in & counted. <i>(Apr 30, 1859,Puketoi Station Diary</i>)

>  <b>BUTLER</b> ... my own [rams] have already been in my flock eighteen months and I have just bought forty new ones. <i>(Jun 1862,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1862 June Butler to Acland in 1960 Maling Samuel Butler 57]

>  When two or three adjoining runs are in one man's holding he must have as many flocks as establishments as runs to fulfil the exact letter of the regulations ... <i>(Jul 3, 1863,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,2/54,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (J.C. Richmond)]

>  <b>BARKER</b> It is impossible to estimate our loss until the grand muster at shearing, but we may set it down at half our flock ... <i>(1867,Station Life in NZ,173,1870 ed.</i>)

>  ... after we drafted out some fine wethers ... I counted the whole flock, 1649. <i>(Feb 1, 1868,Chudleigh Diary,215,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>TINNE</b> It is customary to invite all the neighbours to your `muster,' as they can then pick out their missing sheep which have strayed from the adjoining runs. As they complete each flock, it is turned over to a shepherd, who would drive it off with the aid of his dogs to a beat, possibly ten or twenty miles distant ... <i>(1873,Wonderland of the Antipodes,38</i>)

>  <b>KENNAWAY</b> It needs incessant care to prevent stragglers breaking away from the head and sides of the mob, and the rear of the flock, made up of the weaker sheep, requires an equally constant lookout. The horses are led or ridden, as the ground allows; ... but in driving through unknown country, the halts are made very much where the explorers find themselves at sundown. <i>(1874,Crusts: a settler's fare due south,46</i>)

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> [cp mob] A little further up the [East] coast the Maoris have a flock of very `scabby' sheep, which roam over a large area of land. These sheep are all long-tailed, and have never been marked, except on their ears. <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,145,1935 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MACDONALD</b> When the flock is small this is all the wool classing that is necessary. <i>(1915,NZ Sheepfarming,69</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> Instead, therefore, of collecting the sheep from every part of the run and counting them in the yards, we took delivery `off the books' - that is, we accepted the flock on the previous shearing and lambing tally. <i>(1926,Tutira,153</i>)

>  Flock: (1) The whole of the sheep on a farm or station; (2) the whole of the ewes or wethers on a station are sometimes spoken of as the ewe or wether flock. A number of sheep running together are a mob (q.v.). `Such a large flock as this - about 50,000.' <i>(Oct 21, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <b>BARKER</b> 3. Adjectival use. - Ordinary as opposed to stud sheep, which are registered and kept for breeding rams and show sheep. (C.) <i>Ibid.</i> 33 Such a large flock as this - about 50,000. <i>(1874,Station Life in NZ,33</i>)

>  <b>AYSON</b> My flock was the dry flock wethers. One day I got notice to ... put my flock of 3,000 sheep away back on the hill country of `Lochindorb', near the head of the Owaka river. It was very much infested with wild dogs and wild pigs, ... but it was very good sheep country. <i>(1937,Pioneering in Otago,94</i>)

>  <b>SCOTTER</b> He founded a flock of Border Leicesters from which [his sons] ... bought two rams and six ewes and developed a flock at `Rocklea', Ngapara, which became known throughout New Zealand. <i>(1948,Run, Estate and Farm,38</i>) [NOTES: [= stud]]

>  Its purpose is to divide a mixed flock ... into any required number of smaller mobs, ... <i>(Jul 15, 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,7</i>) [NOTES: [cf MOB]]

>  <b>OGONOWSKA-COATES</b> Flock. The sheep on a run. <i>(1987,Boards, Blades & Barebellies,96</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>FITTON</b> NEW ZEALAND. - Scoured fleece, 1s. 8-1/2d. ...; average flocks, 1s. 5d. ...; pieces and locks, 1s. 1d. ... unwashed, 10-1/2d. ... <i>(1856,New Zealand (various authors/titles),234</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  Ordinary as opposed to stud sheep, which are registered and kept for breeding rams and show sheep. <i>(1933,Press, The</i>) [NOTES: Acland]



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) the sum total of a farmer's sheep or sheep-type.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Ivan blade-shore the whole wether flock. <i>(1975,Sixty Thousand on the Hoof,180</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> .. the hogget flock of 6000 or 7000 was autumned and wintered on self-sown un-topdressed suckling. <i>(1921,Tutira,313</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> Flock: the total of an owner's sheep. It may also mean the total of his sheep of any particular breed or any kind. eg Corriedale--, ewe--, or wether flock. Townspeople may incorrectly term a mob they see being driven along the road as a 'flock.' <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,32</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [of non-stud sheep] used for breeding.

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> flock: of breeding sheep other than stud animals. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,32</i>)

>  After weaning the flock ewes are mob-stocked through to crutching and eye-clipping in April. <i>(Dec 14, 1988,NZ Farmer,19</i>)

### flock book
 
 <i>n.</i> [(station)]

>  Flock Book: The official register of stud flocks. (C.) <i>(Oct 21, 1933,Press, The</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

### flock of bats
 
 <i>phr.</i> (humorous) a block of flats (spoonerism) [ca1958 Wellington Those cokestackers in the flock of bats ...]

### flock-holder
 
 <i>n.</i>

### flock-owner
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>COLONEL W. WAKEFIELD</b> The vicinity of snowy eminences is highly estimated by flock-owners, particularly where the downs are round-topped, ... so that the gradual tricklings from the melting snows go to nourish the roots of grasses. <i>(Aug 31, 1844,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Aug 31 COLONEL W. WAKEFIELD Letter in 1898 Hocken Contributions 268]

>  [Notice] TO THE FLOCKOWNERS AND OTHER INHABITANTS OF CANTERBURY ... <i>(Aug 12, 1857,Lyttelton Times,5</i>)

>  NOTICE TO FLOCKOWNERS <i>(Feb 2, 1861,Otago Witness,9</i>)

>  Of the several native grasses ..., several are considered by the flockowners, to possess high nutritive powers. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/16</i>) [NOTES: (Monro)]

>  The quantity [711] of lamb's wool produced by one flock owner is usually too small to warrant its being classed into many lines. <i>(1936,Agricultural Organization in NZ,710</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> When I first read this inscription [in the Memorial Church, Cave], the word `sheep-men' made me involuntarily flinch. Years later, when I had forgotten it, I suddenly met it again, and again I recoiled. So aghast was I that I spent weeks collecting every old term for `runholders' I could, and I found thirteen, viz., `flock owners,' `flock masters,' `pastoralists,' `run holders,' `run owners,' `sheep owners,' `sheep holders,' `sheep masters,' `squatters,' `station holders,' `station owners,' `stock holders,[sic] and `stock owners.' The word `sheepmen' is a modern Americanism, and using this anachronism mars a splendid memorial. ... [12] Of all the thirteen terms found in old documents, the word `runholder' was the most used, and it is the word that I think should be substituted. <i>(1947,Early Runholding in Otago,11</i>)

>  <b>SHARPE</b> A hill-country flockowner keeps all his ewe lambs, turning a percentage of eighteen-month-old ewes - two-tooths, as they are called - into his main flock every year ... <i>(1962,Country Occasions,51</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a sheep farmer or flockmaster.

>  To presume laying down rules for wool-washing, which every flock-owner, overseer or manager imagines that he understands better than all the world ... <i>(Aug 20, 1864,Weekly News,8</i>)

>  As cotted or matted wool is a loss to the flock-owner, the cause of this condition of the fleece is often discussed. <i>(Apr 1920,NZ Farmer,452</i>)

### flock-station
 
 <i>n.</i>

### flockmaster
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a sheep farmer.

>  If the flockmaster decide [sic] to shear his sheep in the grease, like care ought to be taken to separate carefully the locks and oieces from the fleeces ... <i>(Sep 1882,Northern New Zealand Settler,31</i>)

>  <b>HOBHOUSE</b> The most advanced flockmasters are now enclosing on a much greater scale to retain large 'mobs' of sheep when mustered. <i>(Nov 1859,Letter,4</i>)

>  The waste lands were sold by the Government - of sheep - to the flockmasters about them. <i>(Aug 27, 1864,Weekly News,9</i>)

>  Will you kindly inform me what is used most by experienced flockmasters as a lick for sheep? <i>(Apr 1920,NZ Farmer,558</i>)

>  <b>HARTLEY</b> Flockmasters shudder to watch the uneducated efforts of a young dog endeavouring to work sheep in a 200 acre paddock of today. <i>(1949,Shepherd's Dogs, The,12</i>)

>  There is an abundance of water, enabling the flock-master to wash his wool thoroughly; ... <i>(Jul 20, 1844,Nelson Examiner</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Monro to Nelson Examiner in Hocken Contributions 260 (Griffiths Collection) 1844 Monro Notes of a Journey]

>  ... not only our flockmasters, but the interests of every one of our fellow-colonists is most deeply concerned. <i>(Jun 28, 1851,Lyttelton Times,6</i>)

>  So, too, the wild dog (Canis familiaris) is now unknown. For some years after the settlers arrived here the wild dog was the terror of the flockmaster ... But the bulk of the wild dogs were not domestic animals gone wild, but the true old Maori wild dog. I know that this statement will be questioned by many who have never believed that there were genuine old-identity wild dogs in New Zealand before Europeans ... <i>(1878,TrNZI,10/321</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> When I first read this inscription [in the Memorial Church, Cave], the word `sheep-men' made me involuntarily flinch. Years later, when I had forgotten it, I suddenly met it again, and again I recoiled. So aghast was I that I spent weeks collecting every old term for `runholders' I could, and I found thirteen, viz., `flock owners,' `flock masters,' `pastoralists,' `run holders,' `run owners,' `sheep owners,' `sheep holders,' `sheep masters,' `squatters,' `station holders,' `station owners,' `stock holders,[sic] and `stock owners.' The word `sheepmen' is a modern Americanism, and using this anachronism mars a splendid memorial. ... [12] Of all the thirteen terms found in old documents, the word `runholder' was the most used, and it is the word that I think should be substituted. <i>(1947,Early Runholding in Otago,11</i>)

>  <b>BOWEN</b> It is acknowledged of course, that the Fantail method is still widely practised and is preferred by some flockmasters. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,54</i>)

>  <b>ELDRED-GRIGG</b> A similar social season surrounded the spring races in Dunedin and attracted what Charlotte Murison called the 'great flockmasters' of Otago. <i>(1980,Southern Gentry,102</i>)

### flog
 
 <i>v.</i> to steal, take without permission [ca1933 Dunedin Schoolchildren (A. Campbell); ca1959 Wellington Training College e.g. he flogged a book from the library...]

>  <b>SLATTER</b> `You know me. Fred Croxley. In the QM Store' ... `I remember now. You reckon I flogged those blankets.' <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,96</i>)

>  <b>CRUMP</b> I shot deer along the roadside in the early mornings till the mill-workers complained about my flogging all the hand meat. <i>(1960,Good Keen Man, A,26</i>)

>  Several of the boys wanted to flog [the poster] and present it to the latrine attendant at the Railway Station. <i>(Jun 18, 1963,Salient,2</i>) [NOTES: [El Crud: Student commentator]]

>  <b>DAVIS</b> I'll nip back to Waiouru [Military Camp] and flog a bren gun. <i>(1964,Watersiders,111</i>)

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> I flogged a fresh bottle from Jim's crate without him noticing. <i>(1965,Among the Cinders,263</i>)

>  <b>WATSON</b> We bought sweet woody-scented incense sticks to burn and flogged wood for the fire off a section along the road ... <i>(1965,Stand in the Rain,43</i>)

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> `That joker won't want to go telling anyone about it. He's likely to have flogged the stuff himself, knowing him.' <i>(1967,Empty Hills,157</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> It won't be long before he'll be on about the time he landed in the boob for flogging blankets ... <i>(1970,On the Ball,137</i>)

>  <b>CRUMP</b> And somehow he still went on flogging their diesel. They never found out how he was doing it as far as I know. <i>(1971,Bastards I Have Met,19</i>)

>  <b>FERNANDEZ</b> Could get them perhaps for killing sheep or flogging our stores. <i>(1973,Tussock Fever,59</i>)

>  <b>AGNEW</b> `You can't flog that,' I protested. `Them turbans are considered to be sacred by the Indians. ...' <i>(1974,Loner, The,104</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> Flog. TO STEAL or to SELL. A salesman may flog (sell) you a car while a thief may flog (steal) the hubcaps. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,41</i>)

>  Flog: steal or misappropriate Flog off: sell usually in an unconventional manner <i>(1981,Auckland Savings Bank</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]



 
 <i>v.</i> [ca1949 He flogged his new V-8 until it was ruined. (probably a variant of thrash @v in same sense)]

### flog it
 
 <i>v.</i> to absent oneself illegally; play hooky (ca1950)

### flog off
 
 <i>v.</i> to depart hurriedly; to sell (often in an unconventional way)

>  AAPG Bulletin v. 70, no. 8, p. 970-976 "Seismic Data Obtained Using .50-Calibre Machine Gun as High-Resolution Seismic Source" - Michael D. Seeber and Don Steeples.- not flogged from Geolog. (Ed.) <i>(Aug 1987,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,77/4</i>)

>  <b>MORRIESON</b> Flog off that section easy. No more scratching for a dollar. <i>(1976,Pallet on the Floor,117</i>)

>  Mr Maxwell: Here is a man who took advantage of a school situation to flog off [sell ?illicitly] a narcotic. <i>(Apr 3, 1979,Evening Post,5</i>)

>  Flog: steal or misappropriate Flog off: sell usually in an unconventional manner <i>(1981,Auckland Savings Bank</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

>  <b>HALL</b> It's been on the market for eight months. Every ex deer culler in the country's got a machine he's trying to flog off. <i>(1983,Hot Water,17</i>)

>  <b>BINNEY</b> You're just suckholing 'cause he flogs off those cookers, or whatever they are, of yours. <i>(1985,Long Lives the King,26</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> You'll go down the road if ya don't wake ya ideas up, I said. Was gonna give him a buncha five but I flogged off instead. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,91</i>)

### flog the cat
 
 <i>phr.</i>

 <i>n.</i> [(obs ? local) cf. pilot horse]

### flood-dam
 
 <i>n.</i> [cf wing (dam)]

### floodbank
 
 <i>n.</i> [see also "stopbank"]

>  The floodbank - "levees," they would call them in America, skirt the streams. <i>(May 5, 1938,NZ Observer,9</i>)

>  Flood banks were built on the river in this area at the expense of the ratepayers to keep the water out. <i>(Jul 12, 1939,Te Aroha Times,5</i>)

### floodgate
 
 <i>n.</i> a gate used across a stream so that it rises and falls with the water level

>  <b>NEWTON</b> [Five foot manuka poles laced with two wires] ... suspended from wire cable in a gorge so that the foot of the poles rested lightly on the creek-bed - [the fence] at flood-time would swing out and driftwood [etc.] would float underneath. <i>(1949,High Country Days,120</i>)

>  <b>CASEY</b> ... the wires are all undone, and the flood-gate's wide open. <i>(1963,As Short a Spring,218</i>)

>  <b>WHEELER</b> The 300-yard-long floodgate in the foreground is one of fourteen on White Rock [Station] rivers. `The `gate',' Jim explains, `is really a batten-and-wire fence suspended from a heavy guy wire strained right across the riverbed. It is build this way to swing freely ...' <i>(1973,Historic Sheep Stations NI,15</i>)

>  Flood Gate: Used across a stream so that it rised and falls with the water level. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,28</i>)

>  [I learned how] to ... erect a flood gate across a river. All the skills of the fencer were shown to me. <i>(Jan 5, 1984,Dominion,5</i>)

>  floodgate n. ... NZ free-standing fence made to rise and fall with the level of the water <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,288</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a heavy cable strung across a creek or river bed from which is hung a series of battens [to prevent stock moving from one paddock to another.]

>  <b>MCKENNY</b> When the storm water goes down, the flood-gates fall back into their proper position and thus close up the opening in the fence again. <i>(1927,Sheep-Farming, Fencing etc.,104</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> flood gate: Device used on a fence line where it crosses a dry watercourse. It hangs in the hollow and is so built that it can float when water rises and recede back to the bed when the flow ceases. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,Glossary Add. 2</i>)

>  <b>BALFOUR</b> To Hampden to see Matthews about floodgates. <i>(Jan 26, 1873,Balfour Diaries</i>)

>  The flood-gate .. is designed to prevent small stock from passing ... <i>(Dec 1904,NZ Farmer,962</i>)

>  The main outlet drains, together with stopbanks and flood-gates, have been put in by the Local Drainage Branch of the Lands and Survey... <i>(Jan 15, 1945,NZ Journal of Agriculture,10</i>)

>  <b>RIDDOLLS</b> Where a fence line crosses a creek or watercourse that is dry or semi-dry for most of the year, but may develop into a torrent during heavy rains, it may be necessary to erect a flood gate to prevent stock getting under. <i>(1958,Farm Engineering,425</i>)

>  <b>BARDSLEY</b> And they rebuilt our longest floodgate below Mount Adams. <i>(2000,Land Girls, The,101</i>)

>  <b>KERR</b> One morning after heavy rain Omarama Creek flooded. It was a big one: three floodgates were washed out ... [flood-gate] <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country,199</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

>  The floodplain management plan proposed spending $78 milli0on on upgrading defences over the next 40 years. <i>(Mar 9, 2001,Evening Post,2</i>)

 <i>n.</i> [mining]

### flounder
 
 <i>v.</i> go floundering



 
 <i>n.</i> any of various flatfish with a large mouth and both eyes usually occurring on the left side of the head; aka patiki [see also greenback (flounder) 1770 B.J. 2/8 see cit. for SOLE]

>  <b>POLACK</b> Among the piscivorous tribes, are those well known in Europe under the appellations of snapper, tench, haddocks, pollock, salmon, gurnets, john dory, hakes, bream, sword-fish, elephant-fish, diodons, pipe-fish, cod, parrot-fish, flounders, skate, cat and dog-fish, sting-rays, eels. - Among the larger tribes that sport in shoals in the vicinity of the shore, are, sperm-whales, called <i>para</i>[as-acute] <i>paraua</i>[a3-acute] (physeter macrocephalus); black whale, <i>mungu nui</i> (black physeter); right whale, <i>tohora</i> (balaena mysticetus); finback, (balaena physalis); sun-fish (diodon mola); large-lipped whale (balaena mysticetus); large-lipped whale (balaena musculus); hump-back (balaena gibbosa); shark, <i>mango</i> (squalus); hammer-head (zygaena); pilots, (scomber); flying-fish (exocaetus volitans); and an infinity of others, besides a great variety of testaceous and crustaceous fishes that almost cover the rocks under water, such as clams, muscles, oysters, crays, cuttles, limpets, wilks, gigantic muscles, a foot in length, cockles, sea-ears, sea-eggs, star-fish, mutton-fish, &c.; from the latter the pearl is procured which they insert in the eyes of their carved images and boxes. <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/202 fn</i>)

>  (Queen Charlotte Sound) We observ'd already that the principal fish caught by the seyne were Mullets [ed. fn Aldrichetta forsteri.] and Elephant fish [ed. fn Callarhyncus milli.] & a few soles [ed. fn Peltorhamphus novaeseelandiae.] and flounders, [ed.fn Rhombosalea spp.] but those which the Indians mostly supply'd us with are a sort of sea Bream of a silver colour with a black spot on the neck, [ed. fn Tarakihi, ...] ... <i>(1967,Cook's Journals,3/807</i>) [NOTES: 1777 Feb Anderson's Journal in Cook Journals (1967) 3/807]

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> Fish of all sorts and sizes abound on every part of the sea-coast. The only sorts similar to ours are the Conger-eel, Sole, Plaice, and Flounder. <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,160</i>)

>  English Name Native Name ... ... Flounder Patiki ... ... <i>(1849,NZ Journal,(Vol. IX) 125</i>) [NOTES: (quoting Anglo Maori Warder Oct 5)]

>  <b>POWER</b> The flounders are a splendid fish, the finest of their tribe; spearing them by torchlight is an excellent sport. <i>(1849,Sketches in NZ,77</i>)

>  Many ... varieties of sea-fish are caught round the coast, such as barracouta, groper, rock-cod, ling, flounders, soles, gar-fish, and others, all which are plentiful; ... <i>(1868,Province of Otago in NZ, The,32</i>)

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 83. <i>RHOMBOSOLEA MONOPUS</i>. Gunth. Flounder. Patiki. <i>(1872,Fishes,51</i>)

>  That no flounders should be sold under nine inches long; no red cod under ten inches; no mullet under nine inches; and no garfish under fourteen inches -... <i>(1879,TrNZI,11/381</i>) [NOTES: (P. Thomson, Otago)]

>  <b>SHERRIN</b> <i>Rhombosolea monopus</i> Gunther Patiki, or Flounder <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,304</i>)

>  Catlin's River - Mullet (sea), flounders, sole, sardines <i>(1892,TrNZI,24/204</i>) [NOTES: (Thomson) 20]

>  Flounder or Patiki (<i>Rhombosolea monopus</i>). Probably the yellow-belly (R. leporina)is included among the returns. <i>(1892,TrNZI,24/213</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> ~ The Flounders in Australia are - In Sydney, <i>Pseudorhombus russeli</i>, Gray; in Melbourne, <i>Rhombosolea vistoria</i>, Castln.; in New Zealand and Tasmania, <i>R. monopus</i>, Gunth. Maori name, Patiki; family Pleuronectida. They are all excellent eating. <i>(1898,Austral English,148</i>)

>  `Patiki (flounder).are in the market all the year.' - <i>(1876,TrNZI,9/487</i>) [NOTES: Thomson]

>  In other parts king-fish in abundance, red cod, soles, and flounders were found, and large numbers of elephant-fish, gurnard, and schnapper, ... <i>(1901,TrNZI,33/574</i>)

>  <b>DRUMMOND & HUTTON</b> Among the flat-fish, there are three or four different varieties of flounders, but they are usually all clubbed together. <i>(1902,Nature in NZ,73</i>)

>  <b>HAMILTON</b> ... with the following results: 581 eels (from 1ft. to 4ft. in length); 8 dozen flounders, of various sizes, large numbers of aua, or kataka (mullet); about 70lb. or 70lb. weight of pilchard, or mohimohi; a few snapper and kahawai; hundreds of red cod and rarii; and a number of kokopu or rawaru. <i>(1908,Fishing and Sea-Foods,64</i>)

>  <b>SPEIGHT</b> The four species of <i>Rhombosolea</i> are distinguished by form and colour. The best known is the Sand flounder, or simply Flounder, (<i>R. plebeia</i>) (Plate II. fig. 2) wider across than the other, while the Greenback (<i>R. tapirina</i>) with white undersurface, the Yellowbelly (<i>R. leporina</i>) and the Black flounder (<i>R. retiaria</i>) have their distinguishing colours recorded in their common names. <i>(1927,Natural History of Canterbury,201</i>) [NOTES: 1927 Archey `Native Fishes of Canterbury' in Speight et al. Natural History of Canterbury 201]

>  FLOUNDER (four species) (a) Scientific name: (i)<i> Rhombosolea plebeia</i> (ii)<i> Rhombosolea retiaria</i> (iii) <i>Rhombosolea tapirina</i> (iv) <i>Rhombosolea leporina</i>. (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: (i) Flounder (ii) Freshwater Flounder (iii) Flounder (iv) Yellowbelly Flounder. (c) Other common names: (i) Square, Dab, Plaice, Diamond or Diamond-back, Tin-plate, Three Corners. (ii) Blacks, Black Flounder (iii) Greenback [25 `Not common but occasionally taken in southern waters.'] (iv) Yellow Flounder. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,24</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] flounder 2278, 2283, 2301 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

 <i>n.</i> a glass-bottomed box through which flounders can be seen on the sea floor

 <i>n.</i> a spear used in floundering

### flounderer
 
 <i>n.</i>
