### floundering
 
 <i>n.</i> fishing for flounder

>  <b>McGEE</b> TUPPER: Lovely evening. Be ideal ... for a spot of ... floundering... TUPPER: Floundering? Romantic? Pig's arse. About as much romance in floundering as two Irish sailors on leave on St - sorry Myrtle, do apologise. <i>(1981,Foreskin's Lament,73</i>)

>  <b>Grace</b> At night they'd go floundering with lamps and toasting forks or table forks tied to sticks or wire forks that they made with number-eight borrowed from fences. <i>(2001,Dogside Story,114</i>)

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> `... He's going [12] floundering tonight - if he's lucky we might have a taste of fish for breakfast.' <i>(1967,Empty Hills,11</i>)

>  <b>MAY</b> I have just finished an article ... referring to flat bottomed boats (Flatties) used at Lake Ellesmere and Taumutu. In the early 1930s my Father bought a flattie which was used to go across to the Opihi mouth for whitebaiting season and also for eeling and floundering. <i>(1989,Te Karanga,3,73 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Hera Graham)]

### flour and blanket policy
 
 <i>n.</i> (historical) [-] (See [also flour and sugar policy](fin-fly.md#flour-and-sugar-policy))

>  <b>LYON</b> The [Maori] policy of that date [? 1840s, 50s after seizure of Rauparaha] was called the `Flour and Blanket policy' ... and the wily savage ... did not fail to exhibit a spirit of aggression. <i>(1952,Faring South,76</i>)

### flour and sugar
 
 <i>phr.</i> (See [flour and sugar policy](fin-fly.md#flour-and-sugar-policy))

>  He had become the possessor of a fine new double-seated buggy, presented to him by the beneficent "flour and sugar" Government. <i>(Dec 1912,NZ Free Lance,Xmas Annual : 35</i>)

>  With Judge Butler.. goes one more of the old school of native officials, the men who received their earlier raining in the "flour and sugar" days of Donald McLean. <i>(Feb 6, 1904,NZ Observer,5</i>)

>  At first, there was an injudicious mixture of philanthropy and curiosity, which petted and pauperized the native people. ... The chiefs were invited to dinner, ...; they were gratified with doles of flour and sugar, and presents [38] of clothing ... <i>(1851,New Zealand (Selwyn),37</i>) [NOTES: 1848 June 23 Selwyn in 1851 NZ Part V (Church in the Colonies XX) 37-38]

>  <b>S. MATHEW</b> ... some [Maoris] were very threatening and unpleasant [at Porirua, c1840s], but they were soothed with fair words and presents of Flour and sugar and plenty of Tobacco, and the Governor was persuaded that all would come right enough. <i>(1873,Autobiography</i>) [NOTES: 1873 S.Mathew Autobiography in 1940 Founding of NZ 205]

>  As for the `special settlements" for natives, the plan is simply the old `flour and sugar and blanket' system of the earlier history of the colonists. <i>(1903,TrNZI,35/182</i>)

### flour and sugar policy
 
 <i>n.</i> (orig. journalese-political catchphrase, now historical reference only) an 'appeasement' policy towards Maori (See [also flour and blanket policy](fin-fly.md#flour-and-blanket-policy),[sugar and flour policy](spa-sys.md#sugar-and-flour-policy))

>  <b>TROLLOPE</b> The flour-and-sugar-policy is the nickname given to the practice by which the Government bribes the tribes into submission. <i>(1874,New Zealand (various authors/titles),156</i>)

>  <b>CRAWFORD</b> Flour-and-sugar policy.... <i>(1880,Travels in NZ,315</i>)

>  <b>HOCKEN</b> A bare-faced attempt was made to enrol the Maoris, who ... were considered to be householders ... within the meaning of the Act. To secure their votes the `flour and sugar' policy was resorted to. It was known that the names of many persons of the right colour - or stamp as it was then called - but ineligible would be forwarded for enrolment, ... <i>(1898,Contributions to the Early History of New Zealand,144</i>)

### flour bag
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>COWLEY</b> It was feudal, the way they made silage in the 1930s when Pa was a kid wearing flour-bag shirts. <i>(1979,Growing Season, The,59</i>)

>  <b>THORN</b> Most women in those days bought flour in 28-pound bags... The flour bags were made of good unbleached calico and the salt bags a lovely fine soft cotton. Most bakers had a pile of washed hundred-weight flour bags for ninepence and a shilling. I made every article of underclothing and sleeping garments for all of us out of flour bags. <i>(1997,Stick Out, Keep Left,40</i>) [NOTES: [HWO : flour was sold in 25, 50 and 100 pound calico bags]]

>  <b>BARLOW</b> They all brought their evening clothes with them, not in portmanteaus, but in flour bags. [103] It is most surprising to a new chum to see the manifold uses to which flour bags are put to here. <i>(1888,Kaipara and Waitemata Echo,102</i>)

### flour bin
 
 <i>n.</i> (in prison slang) a Pakeha

>  Much labelling is done on the grounds of ethnic identity, so terms such as <i>flour bin</i> (a Pakeha), <i>Bruce</i> (an Asian - from Bruce Lee) and <i>T.H. Lowry</i> (a Maori) are in use. <i>(Oct 2000,NZWords,2(2) : 2</i>)

### floured
 
 <i>adj.</i> [mining]

>  <b>MAY</b> The mercury plates were seldom perfect: the finest gold was lost by flotation while with cemented gold a thin coating of iron oxide (`rusty gold') often prevented amalgamation. The mercury itself became `sick' or `floured'; both sodium and nitric acid were employed to improve its amalgamating qualities. <i>(1967,West Coast Gold Rushes,238,1962 ed.</i>)

### floured mercury
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MAY</b> FLOURED MERCURY: mercury that has lost its amalgamating properties because of impurities in the ore or the wash. The mercury is then `sick'. <i>(1967,West Coast Gold Rushes,527,1962 ed.</i>)

### flouring
 
 <i>n.</i> [mining]

>  While on an official visit to the Thames Goldfield I ...[observed] the ... effect of Cyanide of Potassium, in preventing the flouring of mercury used in working the blanketings. These blanketings I found have ... a decidedly acid reaction, due ... to the presence of ferric and ferrous salts ..., and it is to the former ... that what is commonly known as [335] `flouring,' is mainly due, in the process cited above; such ferric salts being able to either oxidize or chloridize the surface of any mercury ..., thus enfilming it with a compound, which being practically insoluble in water ... prevents that metallic contact taking place between detached mercurial globules, which is necessary to amalgamation. <i>(1876,TrNZI,8/334</i>) [NOTES: (Skey)]

### flow-on
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  flow-on n. Austral. & N.Z. a. a wage or salary increase granted to one group of workers as a consequence of a similar increase granted to another group. b. (as modifier) a flow-on effect. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,428</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1986 NZPOD]]

### flower boy
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  At High School I used to get hassled for being a flower boy, for wearing supposedly strange clothing and not being a real bogan, basically. <i>(Oct 30, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),36</i>)

### flower of Hades
 
 <i>n.</i> the wood rose, <i>Dactylanthus taylorii</i>

>  Unfortunately, cages also prevent the native pollinators such as pekapeka, the short-tailed bat, and as yet other unknown reptile or bird propagators from reaching the flowers. As a consequence, every April/May season the so-called "flowers of Hades" must be laboriously hand-pollinated. <i>(Jan 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 55/28</i>)

### flue
 
 <i>n.</i> [(nonce?) cf. flaw?]

### fluence
 
 <i>n.</i> (constr. the fluence) a delicate or subtle influence [(obs)]

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> Fluence (or 'fluence) the.Delicate or subtle influence: Australians' and New Zealanders': from ca. 1930. Ex the next, q.v. Neville Cardus, Good Days, 1934, `Grimmett's fingers are always light and wonderfully tactile; when he passes the salt at dinner he imparts the "fluence"'. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,289</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> (prob the original of usage 1.) put the fluence on to persuade

>  put the fluence on to persuade; eg `He respects you. If you put the fluence on, he'll join. Guarantee it.' Originally to hypnotise; c.1900, from earlier Cockney phr. for subduing or attracting. ANZ. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,89</i>)

### fluff
 
 <i>v.</i> a. (sc. an argument, a task, an object) to bungle; ruin ca1944 . b. fluff one's lolly mess things up, about ca1952

### fluff about
 
 <i>v.</i> to fuss about; potter about aimlessly [ca1945 e.g. fluff about like an old hen.]

### fluff off
 
 <i>v.</i> to depart [(1942) 1944]

>  <b>FULLARTON</b> [The two-pounders] darted in and out like scorpions and then fluffed off. <i>(1944,Troop Target,121</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (?euphemism) to fart

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> Some of the [slang] expressions listed by children in two schools are: `Up the shoot, going eyes out, bullswool, go jump in the lake, ... shivery dick, shiver me timbers, ... you're telling I, got it on the brain, like smoke in a wheelbarrow' (Kaitaia), `Sheila, crow, bud, frig it, grub, holy moses, blast it, mole, balls to you, shut your face, ... nerts to you, go bite your back, have a roll, fluff off, scram, ants in your pants, rag bag, ... you're bats, dronk, drongo, dill, dip, dippy, ya big lug, greetings gruesome, who dented your beak. (South Wellington). <i>(1953,Unorganized Games,2/677</i>)

 <i>n.</i> a schoolboys' farting contest [(mainly schoolboys') ca1938 Havelock]

>  <b>SLATTER</b> He always won the fluffing contest in the Prefects' room. <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,160</i>)

### fluffy
 
 <i>n.</i> a hot frothed milk drink particularly for children

>  The curse of the "fluffy" is another owners' bane. They're meant to make the kids feel part of the gathering, but they actually wind them up, says the person who has served too many of the mini steamed milk and chocolate drinks. <i>(Apr 27, 2003,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),15</i>)

### fluffy ducks
 
 <i>n.</i> a drinking game

>  Other well-known [drinking] games are Hokonui Swindle, Bottles, Fluffy Ducks, Bunnies are Hovering, Cardinal Huff, All Blacks, Matches, and The Amazing Grimaldi Brothers. All these games involve either a complicated intellectual exercise, tongue twisters, mathematical calculations or aerobatics in a confined space; High Candelabras for example, involves intoxicated individuals attempting to traverse the four walls of a room without touching the floor. <i>(Mar 13, 1988,Dominion Sunday Times,16</i>)

### flugellate
 
 <i>v.</i> variant of flagellate, to thrash, beat [(humorous nonce word ? local ? rare) ca1950 Wairarapa rugby teams (K. Cullinane)]

### flukey
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of land and sheep] infested with liver-fluke.

>  The areas on which "flukey" sheep may be found have certainly extended <i>(Oct 20, 1926,NZ Journal of Agriculture,230</i>)

### fluking
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>GRADY</b> Fluking - The diving of a whale. <i>(1982,Perano Whalers,227</i>) [NOTES: [glossary]]

### flume
 
 <i>n.</i> an artificial water channel for industrial use or for carrying logs. [Latin flumen a river]

>  <b>MONEY</b> Our object was to make a `flume', or aqueduct, which should carry the water of the creek above our heads, and allow us to work in the bed of it below. <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,15</i>)

>  <b>G.M.WILSON</b> A good breakfast, and off we start on our tramp. ... On the hill side sometimes as high as 1000 feet up, we see numerous races or flumes as they are called used by diggers to take water to sluice the gold out of the stuff the gold is found in. <i>(Jan 30, 1873,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1873 Jan 30 G.M.Wilson Diary in 1973 Wierzbicka Wilson Family 158]

>  <b>HINGSTON</b> At these [West Coast] diggings ... we could not but notice the use made of flumes - long gutters of wood, supported on sticks twenty feet or more in height, bringing and taking away water for dozens and dozens of miles. <i>(1879,Australian Abroad, The,293</i>)

>  <b>BLYTH</b> Flume: Boxing or piping used to convey water from higher ground to a lower level. Some companies, working on River Claims ... <i>(1935,Gold Mining Year Book,11</i>) [NOTES: (Glossary)]

>  <b>MAY</b> FLUMES: the more artificial portion of a water-race, consisting of piping, hose or wooden boxes; most commonly applied to wooden boxes used for conveying water to the claim. <i>(1967,West Coast Gold Rushes,527,1962 ed.</i>)

>  <b>McARA</b> Flume: An aqueduct. Several of these were used to carry water-races over streams and gullies. There were two large arch-supported flumes across the Ohinemuri River carrying the 12 ft by 4 ft low-pressure race. <i>(1978,Gold Mining at Waihi (Glossary),322</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [[is this separate from 1?]]

>  <b>BAINES</b> The stream at the dam was forty-five feet wide and at the centre of the structure I made a `flume' sixteen feet wide, from the floor of which to the top was also sixteen feet. It was, indeed, an opening [175] through the dam, the sides and floor of which were four-inch planking. <i>(1874,Edward Crewe,174</i>)

### flume dam
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [stringer dam](../S#stringer-dam))

### fluming
 
 <i>n.</i> a flume, or system of flumes: a (wooden) trough (or pipe) which carries water to the gold workings; or a system of such troughs and pipes.

>  ... bringing water to bear on the [drifts] by a system of `fluming.' <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/372</i>) [NOTES: (Hector)]

>  ... bringing water to bear on the [drifts] by a system of `fluming.' <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/372</i>) [NOTES: (Hector)]

>  <b>MONEY</b> ... we worked on the Creek ... getting very fair gold, though the great labour and expense necessitated in fluming off the water consumed more time and money than we could recover out of the claim. <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,15</i>)

>  <b>THOMPSON</b> From the base of the fluming that makes a bridge/ To carry the head-race from ridge to ridge./ <i>(1909,Ballads about Business,76</i>)

>  <b>VEITCH</b> By 1864 many races, some of them as long as 15 or 20 miles, were under construction, waters from distant creeks being carried in wooden troughs called fluming. OED flume n US an artificial channel for a stream of water to be applied to some industrial use from 1784. <i>(1976,Clyde on the Dunstan,47</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> colloquially (and ? loosely) applied to the guttering and down-piping system used to channel rainwater from house-roofs [ca1930 at least]

### flumonia
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  [Hdg] Tame Nikau. New Zealand has welcomed back with generous warmth a body of splendid young Maori warriors... In some cases Tame returns to a home desolated by the dire "flumonia". <i>(Apr 12, 1919,NZ Observer,4</i>)

>  I find that all inhabitants of the country through which the Main Trunk line runs earnestly believe the flumonia was brought per the train. <i>(May 17, 1919,It Was So Late</i>)

>  If flumonia must come back - and most people seem to expect it - they will want to know how to cope with the terror... Eating onions all day keeps the 'flumonia' quite away." <i>(May 24, 1919,NZ Observer,16</i>)

>  During the horrible flumonia epidemic of a year ago.. Stanbrook "held the fort". <i>(Nov 15, 1919,NZ Observer,10</i>)

### flunks
 
 <i>n.</i> [(child marble players')]

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> Some of the typical terms used in marbles referred to the act of shooting and the movement of the marbles. Those related to ways of shooting marbles were cribbing, firsts, fudging, funks, funnicks, funnigans, flunks, forces, knee-high, knuckle-down-stiff, knuckles, and lasts. Terms connected with the movement of the marbles were: cannons, clears, dead-stick, double-hits, fires, funs, gees, haystacks, in smug, keeps, kills, kisses, lay-ups, manyes, ones, pink, ringer, rolls, scat, screws, skinned, skun, spiders, sported-off, stays, stops, tracks, throughs. <i>(1972,Folkgames of Children,175</i>)

### flurry
 
 <i>n.</i> go into a flurry to go into death throes (of a whale) 1838, 1845 EJW see also death flurry

 <i>n.</i> a williwaw q.v.

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> The N. and N.E. winds, which draw up the harbour [at Port Ashley : ?Lyttelton], are seldom either violent or enduring; from the appearance of the hills, I supposed it subject to flurries in N.W. and S.W. gales; ... the former wind draws up Port Cooper, ... and the latter draws down ... <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,1842</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Capt. Smith's report (p292) in 1848 Wakefield Handbook for NZ 328]



 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to scatter stock from within a mob.

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> The Otakaramu run was very bad for tutu and care had to be taken not to flurry the sheep. <i>(1947,Pioneer Recollections,94</i>)

### flush
 
 <i>v.</i> to give a ewe extra feed, in order to stimulate ovulation.

>  flush vt. fatten (ewes) before mating <i>(1985,Collins Compact Dictionary,190</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; nf. 1986 NZPOD]]



 
 <i>n.</i> busy period of the lambing or killing season [ca1930]

>  <b>ANTHONY</b> The flush of the season was over, most of the cropping was done, and I decided to do some stumping. <i>(1936,Follow the Call,53</i>)

>  <b>COLIN GORDON</b> This is a seasonal job and gives rise to phrases like: `Chaps winter at a certain job (e.g. shearing) in the off-season, and come back regularly. The season is divided into the flush when lambs are coming in (two flushes `grass-fed' and `rape-fed'). After the first flush of lambs, a lot of sheep come in, wo-olly iwis [wou li: |i:wi:z] as they are called. they are tougher than lambs and the tally is therefore less - 80 instead of 100 say; and they are often called grannies or grandmother lambs. <i>(May 17, 1951,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: Feilding Freezing Works terms]

>  The November "flush" has failed to materialise at the Te Kuiti cream dumps. <i>(Nov 17, 1937,King Country Chronicle,5</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [lambing] a wave of lambs produced at one time.

>  <b>STEVENS</b> The case is recalled of a flock of two hundred ewes, caught in the first flush of lambing by an overnight storm, which lost twenty-four lambs. <i>(1958,Sheep.  Part 1, Sheep husbandry,44</i>)

>  <b>STEVENS</b> At the flush of lambing, and particularly when the the weather is unkind, they [temporary houses] are indispensable ... <i>(1958,Sheep.  Part 1, Sheep husbandry,48</i>)

### flush up
 
 <i>v.</i> to fatten (sheep)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> My next job was to attend a `ram fair' and buy some rams ...These were put in the best paddock to `flush them up' before putting them out with the ewes, ... <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,148</i>)

### flushing
 
 <i>n.</i> extra feeding before joining to help stimulate ovulation in the ewe [(farming)]

>  Flushing: Extra feeding before joining to help stimulate ovulation in the ewe and hence produce more lambs. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,50</i>)

### flushing feed
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a crop or pasture used to supplement other feed to lift the nutrition of ewes before tupping.

>  <b>DU FAUR</b> Availability of flushing feed should also be considered when determining mating time. On farm where flushing feed can be reliably provided, mating can be earlier ... <i>(1966,Sheep Farming for Profit,35</i>)

>  <b>STEVENS</b> Use of the crop in this way enables weaned lambs to be finished and provides flushing feed for the ewe flock. <i>(1958,Sheep.  Part 1, Sheep husbandry,17</i>)

### fluttering shearwater
 
 <i>n.</i> = Forster's shearwater

>  <i>Fluttering shearwater Puffinus gavia</i> (FORSTER, 1844) ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Pakaha, Forster's shearwater. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,100</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Pakaha, n. Puffinis gavia, rain-bird. = hakoakoa. </i> <i>(1917,Dictionary of the Maori Language,292</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i> hakoakoa ... Puffinis gavia gavia, fluttering shearwater. </i> <i>(1957,Dictionary of the Maori Language,32</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> FLUTTERING SHEARWATER <i>Puffinus gavia Maori name</i>: Pakaha <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,43</i>)

>  <i>63. Puffinis gavia gavia</i> (Forster, 1844) <i>Fluttering Shearwater (Pakaha)</i> Breeding on many offshore islands from Three Kings to Cook Strait ... <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,28,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> .. (the fluttering shearwater puffinus gavia is the mutton bird of the South Island), ... <i>(1979,Titi Heritage,61</i>)

>  I was ascending slowly from a deep dive near Mayor Island when young fluttering shearwaters swam down towards me at a depth of 10 m. <i>(Aug 2001,NZ Geographic,No. 52/62</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Hakoakoa ...1. Shearwater, Puffinis gavia,</i> and other spp. <i>(1917,Dictionary of the Maori Language,38</i>)

>  Fluttering shearwaters are the third seabird species being tricked by DOC and FOMI into believing Mana Island is home. <i>(Mar 7, 2006,Kapi-Mana news,18</i>)

### fly
 
 <i>n.</i> `the flap at the entrance of a tent' (O.E.D.), most commonly, as part of the tent or as a separate but attachable unit. Fly `canopy' as Satchell has it looks wrong but is nonetheless legitimate according to Auckland's senior firm of tentmakers, which kindly answered enquiries about this. Fly `shelter piece' appears at p. 43, as `a small fly of palm leaves'.

>  "The hunters are making good use of huts ... it is advisable to take a tent or a fly as a precaution." <i>(Apr 17, 2003,Dominion Post,A3</i>)

>  <b>SATCHELL</b> The fire, which must have otherwise been extinguished by the rain, was protected by a small fly of palm leaves, which the natives had given to Clifford for that purpose. <i>(1902,Land of the Lost,43,1971 ed.</i>)

>  It was only needful to sling our tent and fly over the poles ... and give it a good sweep out with a bush broom, to render it fit for occupation. <i>(Dec 1904,NZ Illustrated Magazine,165</i>)

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> ... sod walls with flies of gunny-bags sewn together. <i>(1913,Sodger Sandy's Bairn,113</i>)

>  <b>JONES</b> [?b ?c] ... on the saddle I had a fly - that is, a calico sheet with poles <i>(1933,Autobiography,38</i>)

>  <b>VON HAAST</b> Instead of a strong tent with a fly (a waterproof sheet to go horizontally over the top of the tent), Haast had been sent a tent without fly, ... soon almost torn to pieces by the wind. <i>(1848,Julius Von Haast,94</i>)

>  <b>LYON</b> ... not far away was a saw pit built of logs and with a fly - a large square of stout calico - where [they] ... on wet days were cutting the timber. <i>(1952,Faring South,109</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> However, although there was a swag skins of hanging in a galley fly and the hut was well stocked with tucker, ... <i>(1952,High Country Journey,152</i>)

>  <b>ORBELL</b> [?a ?b] The light oiled japara used for parkas and fishing coats makes up into a light tent which can be used without a fly. <i>(1952,Comfort & Commonsense in the Bush,1</i>)

>  <b>THOMPSON</b> We used the canvas canoe to bring ashore ... a skin fly [? a.] which we pitched to store dry wood for firing ... [178] The fly tent we used as sleeping quarters and the skin fly as a cooking galley. <i>(1952,Deer Hunter: The Experiences of a NZ Stalker,177</i>)

>  <b>NOLAN</b> When it can be carried, and certainly for a base camp, a fly, long enough to provide a porch, makes camping much more comfortable. <i>(1961,Bush Lore,19</i>)

>  <b>McCASKILL</b> As the tents were of canvas, flies were not used but in wet weather there was always someone careless enough to brush the roof and start a leak going. <i>(1969,Molesworth,100</i>)

>  The tent has been purchased in Spain and doesn't have a fly. Apparently, it does not rain in Spain. <i>(Apr 26, 2003,Dominion Post,F8</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to work as a flying conductor q.v. [(Wellington tramwaymen's, obs) ca1951 Go up and fly a (tram-)car down to John Street. ca1951 F.M. O'Brien, Tramwaymen's, Wellington I am flying tonight. (or vbl n) Flying's not a bad caper.]



 
 <i>n.</i> give it a fly, have a fly at to give it (have a) a go, lash; have a fly, a spin (at two-up)

>  Come on, me lucky lads, you pick 'em an' I'll pay 'em. Hop right in, diggers, and have a fly with the old man. <i>(1917,New Zealand At the Front,105</i>)

>  <b>SYDER & HODGETTS</b> `We were just raw recruits, absolutely inexperienced. .in 4;We often said we would like the experience of a big Construction site. So anyway we gave it a fly.'..<i>Ibid.</i> 407 To attempt the project, not being entirely sure of success. .in 4;`I probably won't last any longer than anyone else, but the pay's good. I'll give it a fly.' <i>(1962,Australian & NZ English [TS],359,1974 ed.</i>)

>  `Come on me lucky lads, you pick 'em. Hop in, digs, and have a fly with the old man. Come on, thick and heavy, for I'm here today and gone tomorrow.' <i>(Aug 10, 1921,Quick March,27</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> blowfly

>  <b>HURSTHOUSE</b> Sheep are never attacked by the `fly' <i>(1849,Account of Settlement of New Plymouth, An,106</i>)

>  <b>MACDONALD</b> BLOWFLY. - Cleanliness by way of proper dipping, crutching, etc., assists towards suppression of the fly. <i>(1920,NZ Sheepfarming,156</i>)

>  <b>PERRY</b> Sheep that are left uncrutched and dirty are pre-disposed to attacks of fly, as are those which have been cut about at shearing time. <i>(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,146</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a piece of canvas erected outside a tent to provide shelter for a specific purpose. [[CCR notes: a fly is to keep the tent drier: if anyone touches the inside of wet canvas water will seep through: a fly obviates this.] a. a flap at the entrance of a tent forming a door; b. an exterior sheet stretched over the ridge-pole so as to cover the ordinary tent roof with an air-space between (R. Mason); c) occas. a piece of canvas, one end fixed on two poles, the other on the ground to form a rough, sloping shelter, or wind or rain break]

>  The shaft is covered with a canvas fly. <i>(Feb 2, 1885,Wairarapa Daily,2</i>)

>  <b>PAYTON</b> We pitched our tent in their corn paddock, while the Maoris slept near us under a fly. <i>(1888,Round about NZ,264</i>)

>  We used to sleep out under the "fly" and under the stars. <i>(Sep 1992,North & South,35</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAM MARTIN</b> [=fly-tent] Simon gave me two old pieces of Callico with which I tried to make a fly to sleep in going over the Mountains <i>(Sep 26, 1863,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1863 Sep 26 William Martin Diary (Hocken MS)]

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> ... I selected a tent-fly (a twelve-foot square sheet of calico), rolled it up, threw it over my shoulder, and proceeded to a bare glade on the Kohiwai flat. This flat is heavily covered with flax and toi-toi bushes. <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,62,1935 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> ... diggers' tents by twos or threes ... most of them being surrounded by a wall built of sods or turf, and protected by a weather-stained and patched `fly'. <i>(1881,Waitaruna,129</i>)

>  <b>DOUGLAS</b> [?b] Load consists of two Batwing Tents and two flys [sic]. <i>(Jan 29, 1891,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1891 Jan 29 Douglas Letter to Roberts in 1957 Pascoe Mr Explorer Douglas 118]

>  <b>HARPER</b> .. survey parties can have their loads packed on horseback, and carry tent and fly, with a second smaller fly to pitch at the end of the tent to shelter the fire ...[51] We therefore pitch an ordinary 6 ft. by 8 ft. canvas tent, on a ridge pole, with an 8 ft. by 10 ft. fly six inches above it ... <i>(1896,Pioneer Work in the Alps of NZ,50</i>)

>  <b>SATCHELL</b> The tent, with its fly to protect the roof from the direct beams of the sun, was soon in evidence, ... <i>(1902,Land of the Lost,25,1971 ed.</i>) [NOTES: [p.214 1971 Smithyman fn 2]]

### fly a butterfly
 
 <i>phr.</i> ? to raise money on worthless cheques or bills; cf. fly a kite

### fly biscuit
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [fly cemetery](fin-fly.md#fly-cemetery))

>  Charles Lush, of Parnell, Auckland, on January 22, 1864, wrote in his diary about his activities that day, including, "... I also bought two penny worth of fly kooks for Martin." Alison Drummond, the editor of the <i>Waikato Journals of Vicesimus Lush</i> (Pegasus Press, 1982) in which this entry is printed, identifies "fly kooks" as "cookies, know to generations of children as 'squashed fly biscuits'" <i>(Mar 2, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),9</i>)

### fly boy
 
 <i>n.</i> [flaxmilling]

>  <b>CARR</b> When the flax arrives at the mill it is weighed and credited to the cutter. The `bench loader' then takes two or three bundles and puts them in a barrel (heavy end down). He then takes the longest leaves and places them on a bench convenient to the `feeder'. ...[44] The `feeder' takes each blade separately and places the butt end into the `stripper' which takes the green outer covering off. ... The fibre as it leaves the stripper is caught by the `fly boy', who collects enough to make into a hank, which he places convenient for the `shaker'. The latter, with a motion like cracking a whip, gets rid of as much rubbish as possible, after which he places the hank handy for the `washer'. This man places the fibre in a vat, through which ... clean water is running. <i>(1913,Country Work,43</i>)

### Fly Buys
 
 <i>n.</i> a loyalty card scheme

>  Many fought ;long and hard to become mebers ikf Fly Buys which, besides rewarding customers for their loyalty, gives businesses access to forward-looking customer behaviour trends and information... Fly Buys set about trying to understand groups of people in different life stages, with different needs and characteristics - and then targeting each group through mass marketing. <i>(Apr 28, 2004,Dominion Post,C4</i>)

### fly cemetery
 
 <i>n.</i> a fruit square [Joan Clouston (118 Glenmore St) writes about 21/09/1999 that this term was in use her distant youth, at primary school in the 30s.]

>  The upshot of all this was the cheese and crackers in place of what would have been finely cut cucumber sandwiches, flies' cemeteries (fruit pies) and perhaps even chocolate cake. <i>(Dec 20, 2003,Weekend Herald,A23</i>)

>  The topic that brought this unexpected attention was the origin of the name of the dried-fruit-filled-shortcake known as fly cemeteries. <i>(Dec 28, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),40</i>)

>  Fly cemeteries were known to the father of Pontius Pilate in Perthshire, Scotland. <i>(Mar 9, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),11</i>)

>  I was born and bred in south-east London... From as far back as I can remember, about 1935-36, "fly cemetery" was our invariable expression for a garibaldi biscuit. <i>(Feb 23, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),10</i>)

>  I remember my mother telling me she used to walk to her primary school in Pamerston with her best friend Elizabeth. She would send Elizabeth into the cake shop and wait with a delicious sense of anticipation and a great deal of surprise that she could persuade such a naturally good child to ask for a fly cemetery... My mother was born in 1910, so these events would date to the early 1920s... <i>(Feb 23, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),10</i>)

>  Fly cemeteries: It's not a name used in cookbooks. There it's always called fruit squares. How boring, compared to fly cemeteries, a name that is original to New Zealand. <i>(Jan 5, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),46</i>)

>  ...I, too, can remember hearing about fly cemeteries over 50 years ago. I was living with my family in Wellington when my father mentioned that he'd had a fly cemetery for afternoon tea... So maybe it was Eccles cakes in Manchester and fruit squares in Wellington that were given this affectionate nickname. . <i>(Feb 9, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),7</i>)

>  <b>VIRTUE</b> She had baked some fly cemeteries. <i>(1989,Then Upon the Evil Season,98</i>)

>  Where did Lois Daish.. get the idea that "fly cemeteries" was a phrase original to New Zealand? My family was using this term in relation to eccles cakes over 50 years ago, as I grew up in Manchester. <i>(Feb 2, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),8</i>)

>  We watched the cakes being iced or filled with cream and if we were lucky we were allowed to choose a cake - my favourite was a "fly cemetery", filled with fruit and enclosed in flaky pastry. <i>(Jun 2001,NZ Memories,No. 30/26</i>)

### fly door
 
 <i>n.</i> a screen or wire gauze door outside a main door working on a spring to keep insects out [1988 Apr 25 Haeremai to Hicks Bay: Information sheet of Hicks Bay Motel Lodge NOISE - ... Please avoid banging of fly doors, and ensure that all fly doors and window screens are shut.]

>  Door, fly-proof- Partially or wholly fitted with a perforated fabric for the admission of air and the exclusion of flying insects. <i>(1979,SANZ Glossary Building Terminology,41</i>)

### fly monte
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The languid few who congregate in the gloom have to indulge in the fascinating pastime of "fly monte" as a means of keeping boredom at bay. <i>(Jul 19, 1913,NZ Observer,6</i>)

### fly round the clock
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  At the time McColl was perfectly sober, but he told witness that he had a `fly round the clock' the night before, which witness understood to mean a bad night. <i>(Nov 11, 1911,NZ Truth,333/7/3</i>)

### fly tent
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [fly n](fin-fly.md#fly))

>  <b>THOMSON</b> We decided to pack enough tinned stores and, with a small fly-tent, go up the valley ... [179] Had we stayed in the fly tent we would have had a fairly uncomfortable night, as we had not collected any fern for bedding. <i>(1952,Deer Hunter: The Experiences of a NZ Stalker,128, 179</i>)

### fly-blight
 
 <i>n.</i>

### fly-blown
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  In some sheds the managers insist on the cuts made in shearing being tarred, to prevent them being fly-blown <i>(Sep 28, 1878,Otago Witness,3</i>)

>  <b>MALCOLM</b> A sheep became fly blown. <i>(1982,Where it all Began,121</i>)

>  All fly-blown sheep are culled... <i>(Jun 20, 2003,Dominion Post,C5</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>adj.</i> `putrid' useless, penniless, broke

>  <b>THATCHER</b> When they know you're fly-blown/ You're then left alone. <i>(1864,Songs of the War,20</i>) [NOTES: also in 1967 Bailey and Roth Shanties 52]

>  <b>135</b> (quoting Thatcher) When they're cleaned you out in the Bazaar,/ Quite negligent of you they are;/ When they know you're fly-blown./ You're then left alone. <i>(1864,Saturday Review</i>) [NOTES: c1864-7 J.G.S. Grant (Dunedin) Saturday Review 135]

>  <b>PRESHAW</b> fly blown <i>(1885,Banking Under Difficulties,116</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)]

>  <b>D'AVIGDOR</b> His position [after he sc. a digger has cut out his cheque on drink] is then described by the very graphic adjective fly-blown. <i>(1888,Antarctica: great stories,169</i>)

>  <b>FOSTON</b> [The men] usually returned to the works fly-blown [after boozing their cheques]. <i>(1921,At The Front,56</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> It appeared that from October to Christmas he had made [pounds]2000 and he was now engaged in `blowing it in'. He was reeling down the street ...and when he saw me he called out, `Hey, Tom, I'm flyblown just now but come with me in a day or two and I will make your fortune for you.' [171] ... You ask what the term `flyblown' meant. It was a word used in those days [c1860s] and meant a man had no money, but once I saw a man really flyblown. <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,170</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> [As in Australia] a person is flyblown when he is penniless. <i>(1941,NZ Slang,62</i>)

>  flyblown adj. penniless. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),8</i>)

>  fly-blown ... NZ & Austral. sl. broke. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,290</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

### fly-boy
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a person employed at a flax mill to collect fibre from the stripper, forming it into a hank. [also fly boy]

>  WANTED a man to Feed and attend to stripper, none but an experienced hand need apply. Also Washer, Catcher, and Flyboy for the Paparata Hemp Mill. <i>(Dec 6, 1889,Manawatu Herald,3</i>)

>  An experienced catcher and flyboy is wanted. <i>(Oct 16, 1890,Manawatu Herald,2</i>)

>  WANTED, Waitress 15s; Housemaid, private; Scutchers, Fly-boy; Kitchenman 15s ... <i>(Jan 30, 1900,Evening Post,1</i>)

>  WANTED, Fly-Boy for flaxmill. <i>(Sep 15, 1891,Evening Post,3</i>)

>  <b>Carr</b> The fibre as it leaves the stripper is caught by the fly boy who collects enough to make into a hank, which he places convenient for the 'shaker'. <i>(1913,Country Work,44</i>)

### fly-camp
 
 <i>n.</i> a temporary or makeshift camp or headquarters; a camp lying out and beyond a main camp; bivouac, bivvy

>  At the end of the second day, I'm bluffed on the tops and have to set up a fly-camp surrounded by an arena of windthrow-covered ridges, cliffs and waterfalls. <i>(Oct 4, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),32</i>)

>  <b>WILMOT</b> Mantle and Dan stayed in the fly camp - the rest of us came back ... the men from the fly camp came down for tucker and stayed till 6 when they returned with swags. <i>Ibid.</i> 155 We shifted our camp today, and pushed on past our flycamp. <i>(1897,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1897 Wilmot's Journal in 1950 Reed and Reed Farthest West 138-139]

>  <b>ORBELL</b> Fly Camp or Bivvy. This is the camp that needs to be of super-light equipment to go on your back to the high tops. <i>(1952,Comfort & Commonsense in the Bush,8</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> But he was a solid mate, though one felt a mite uncomfortable when, even in the most far-flung fly-camp, he would produce the Bible each evening and read a verse. <i>Ibid.</i> 45 After dividing the skins we returned to the fly-camp. <i>(1952,Deer Hunter: The Experiences of a NZ Stalker,22</i>)

>  <b>HALL-JONES</b> As the rain continued, they collected the rest of their provisions and packed them out to the various fly camps. <i>(1868,Early Fiordland,157</i>)

>  <b>TOMLINSON</b> Charlie spent a lot of time looking for Moonlight's fly camp [126] and claim, but so far as I know no one found much gold there. <i>(1968,Remembered Trails,125</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) [mustering, hunting, forestry, tramping] to make a temporary makeshift shelter.

>  <b>THOMSON</b> Both of us had been out fly-camping and were returning to our base. <i>(1952,Deer Hunter: The Experiences of a NZ Stalker,73</i>)

>  <b>CURTIS</b> Not long after that we decided to flycamp in an adjoining valley. <i>(1997,Murphy's Law and the Hunter,77</i>)

>  <b>ADDENBROOKE</b> During the little fine weather we had, we fly-camped, seeing many Wapiti, but few trophy heads. <i>(1992,Home from the Hill,158</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [mustering, hunting, forestry, tramping] a temporary makeshift shelter.

>  <b>MCCONOCHIE</b> ... I was to look up the Styx River over the Browning Pass to assess that area for a fly camp. <i>(1966,You'll Learn No Harm from the Hills,77</i>)

>  <b>WILMOT</b> Mantle and Dan stayed in the fly camp - the rest of us came back..the men from the fly camp came down for tucker and stayed till 6 when they returned with swags. <i>(1897,Journal,138</i>) [NOTES: in Reed (1950) Farthest West]

>  <b>THOMSON</b> We had very few spells in the three-hour trip and were about all-in by the time we reached the fly-camp. <i>(1952,Deer Hunter: The Experiences of a NZ Stalker,33</i>)

>  <b>PORTER</b> Glad they were of the warmth instead of freezing slowly in a fly-camp in the hills, and we were glad to have their company and their willing help. <i>(1970,Under the Nor'West Arch,35</i>)

>  <b>FARMER</b> The professional's fly camp wasn't luxurious but it was efficient. <i>(1994,Best Job Ever, The,97</i>)

### fly-camping
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>THOMSON</b> The other four shooters were out on the job for a few more days fly-camping, so the two of us had the first night in the never-nevers to ourselves, ... <i>Ibid.</i> [89] Once when out fly-camping, we [were] ... stranded several days without much in the way of food. <i>(1952,Deer Hunter: The Experiences of a NZ Stalker,32</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> ... I set off early one morning with the intention of fly camping and doing a few days' hunting along the tops. <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,32</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [mustering, hunting, forestry, tramping] camping in a temporary shelter without a tent.

>  <b>CURTIS</b> .. we decided to descend to the comfort of Kiwi Flat Hut until the weather was more conducive to fly-camping in the tops. <i>(1996,Beneath the Mountain Mist,100</i>)

>  <b>AXBEY</b> This went on for a month or so, until one day we returned from fly camping to find the owner in residence ... <i>(1991,Along the Track: a collection of NZ hunting yarns,115</i>)

>  <b>FARMER</b> The only time the pack was light was when you were off fly-camping ... <i>(1994,Best Job Ever, The,84</i>)

>  <b>CURTIS</b> That night around the campfire the head field officer selected half the intake to go fly-camping next day up the Dillon Creek. <i>(1997,Murphy's Law and the Hunter,24</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> Fly-camping was essential to be able to cover such a wide area of mountainous country that we were responsible for. <i>(1998,Sea of Gold,  A,52</i>)

### fly-crutch
 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to trim the wool from the anus of sheep to prevent fly-blowing or fly-strike.

>  <b>GASCOIGNE</b> He could help us - we got all that footrotting to get through - he could fly-crutch the ewes too. <i>(1965,From the Top of a Hill,64</i>)

>  Fly-crutch the ewes and three weeks before they join the rams flush them for six weeks on the best feed available. <i>(Jan 1965,NZ Journal of Agriculture,36</i>)

### fly-dumping
 
 <i>n.</i> fly-tipping

>  MDC have also had to stop vehicular access to the Waingawa River at the closest point to Masterton because of fly-dumping of rubbish. <i>(Apr 2000,Elements,3 : 7</i>)

### fly-flicker
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a casual, cavalier sheep-dog, reluctant to work.

>  <b>WHYTE</b> a fly-flicker - a slack, casual and unimpressive dog. <i>(1927,Sheep-Dog, The,78</i>)

>  a lazy dog: a fly-flicker. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)

>  It was time to take the maggot taxis up to the marrowbone country with the shandygaff, fly-flicker, powder puff, thistle-peeper, the wool classer and the gravel-scraper. <i>(Nov 22, 2002,Dominion,C6</i>)

### fly-happy
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  Here was a rather sad fly-happy case. <i>(Jun 28, 1943,NZEF Times,III/105 : 5</i>)

### fly-round
 
 <i>n.</i> ? a spree; ? bustling holiday [obs.]

>  <b>WRIGHT</b> He went for a fly-round over to Sydney. <i>(1897,Station Ballads,38</i>)

### flycatcher
 
 <i>n.</i> gen. English for a bird that catches flies. In NZ earlier occas. applied (perhaps generically) to <i>Rhipidura</i> sp., fantail, probably as a now obs shortening of the semi-popular fantailed-flycatcher.

>  In the morning ... I went ... to the Indian Cove, & shot there two new Kingfishers [[ed. fn] Now the new Zealand Kingfisher ...], some new yellow headed Flycatchers [[ed. fn] Now the Yellowhead <i>Mohoua ochrocephala</i> (Gmelin, 1789).], a fine large green and brown Pigeon [[ed. fn] Now the New Zealand pigeon ...] ... a small Perrokeet, green with a red forehead ... [ed. fn] Now the Red-crowned Parakeet ... <i>(Apr 2, 1773,Resolution Journal of J.R. Forster, The,2/245</i>)

>  <b>SAMUEL STEPHENS</b> The active little flycatchers ... with their pretty fan like tails. <i>(1844,Journal,251</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> Among the smaller varieties [of birds], I observed the white-headed manakin, a black and yellow fly-catcher, and an extremely diminutive wren. <i>(1847,Savage Life,2/106</i>)

>  Piwakawaka an elegant little flycatcher <i>(1847,NZ Journal,VII/200-218</i>)

>  <b>HURSTHOUSE</b> The Tirakara is an elegant little flycatcher. <i>(1849,Account of Settlement of New Plymouth, An,23</i>)

>  <b>HURSTHOUSE</b> ... and the kingfisher, the chiming bell-bird, black robin, flycatcher, ... are common round every homestead. <i>(1857,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/120</i>) [NOTES: [=FANTAIL]]

>  <b>2/63</b> RHIPIDURA FLABELLIFERA, Gml. Piwakawaka. Fan-tail. The pied Flycatcher seems to prefer proximity to water in selecting its nesting- place ... RHIPIDURA FULIGINOSA, Sparrm. Tiwaikawaka. Black Fan-tail. The Black Fan-tail Flycatcher breeds under conditions so very similar to those of the preceding species, that one description will serve for both. <i>Ibid.</i> 48 ... the Fly-catchers, pied and black, flit around ... <i>(1870,TrNZI</i>) [NOTES: (Potts Birds)]

>  RHIPIDURA <i>Flycatchers.</i> <i>(1874,TrNZI,6/145</i>) [NOTES: (Potts)]

>  <b>HAY</b> The Waka-waka (<i>Rhipidura flabellifera</i>) is the robin of our Brighter Britain. It is a fantail, or flycatcher. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/226</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> ... to say nothing of Korimako and Pitoitoi ..., we never saw or heard the notes of either the Piopio, the Tieke, or the Hihi, all of which birds were at one time more numerous even than the Huia. The Zosterops was everywhere abundant, also the Grey Warbler and Rifleman, and ... we found the Tomtit comparatively plentiful; the Parakeet chased its mate through the tree-tops with sharp cries of `twenty-eight'; ... and among the tangle of the underwood the ever-present Flycatcher displayed its pretty fan-like tail. <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand,1/14</i>)

>  <b>KELLY</b> ... the flycatcher also appeared in his familiar manner. <i>(1892,Journey Through Waitara,7</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> bird-name used elsewhere. The Australian species are <i>(1898,Austral English,148</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (figurative)

>  <b>McKAY (1897)</b> As [the gold] progresses along the different tail-race channels, it is gradually liberated from contact with the ironsands and, as free gold, is caught on tables called "fly-catchers," placed in the channel to intercept the gold. <i>(1895,Report on Geol. of SW Nelson,52</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>DRURY</b> BIRDS MET WITH IN PELORUS ... Stilts - two kinds, called Toria by natives. Flycatchers and Fantails. <i>(1855,Sailing Directions,66</i>)

>  The New Zealand and Australian robins and tomtits are more closely related to each other than to other flycatchers. <i>(1950,TrRSNZ,78(2/3) : 127</i>)

>  His [sc. Fleming's] ornithological contributions began with his classic study of the birds of the Chatham Islands.. closely followed by other major studies on the prions.. and New Zealand flycatchers. <i>(1988,Notornis,35/165</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Drosera</i> spp, <i>D. binata, D. rotundifolia</i>, fly-trap plants

>  All our species [of Drosera] catch and digest insects, and in fact are known in some districts by the name of `Fly-catchers'. <i>Ibid.</i> 325 (Petrie) Insects are so frequently caught in the glandular hairs of [Drosera spp] leaves that these herbs are known among the observant Southland settlers as `fly-catchers'. [also 1881 ibid 13/261 (Thomson)] <i>(1881,TrNZI,13/261</i>)

>  <b>FEATON</b> DROSERA. (Linn.) The Sundew ... one can scarcely examine a plant without finding the wings and legs of insects, on one or more of the leaves. ... This extremely interesting genus is represented in New Zealand by six species, all of which are said to digest meats, and are known to [142] the settlers as `Fly-catchers'. <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,141</i>)

>  <b>KIRK</b> (<i>D. binata</i>) (Andersen) [v 1940 Laing and Blackwell 189 ff the Droseraceae are widely distributed, with greatest number of species found in Australia. All are insectivorous.] <i>(1899,Students' Flora</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> <i>DROSERA ...binata </i>... A fly-catcher, the handsomest of its species and the only one on the Chathams ... Scented Sundew, Flycatcher. WAHU. <i>(1952,Chatham Islands,30</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Silene</i> spp.

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> CATCHFLY (<i>Silene [gallica]</i>). Varieties of this species have been named<i> S.anglica</i> and <i>S. quinque vulnera</i>. [1967 quinquevulnera] Common names are spotted catchfly, flycatcher, sticky weed, and five wounds. It is common in cultivated land and waste places such as gravelly river-beds throughout both islands. ... The flowers are white or often dark red, or sometimes white with a blood red blotch on each petal so that this variety is called <i>quinque vulnera</i>, `five wounds'. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,74,1967 ed.</i>)

### flycatcher, black
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The Fan-tailed Flycatcher (<i>Rhipidura flabellifera</i>) is the commonest. ... The Black Flycatcher (<i>R. melanura</i>) belongs exclusively to the South. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/10</i>) [NOTES: (Buller Ornithology)]

>  36.<i>Rhipidura fuliginosa</i>, Sparrm. Black Fantail, Black Flycatcher, Ti-waka-waka. <i>(1885,NZ Journal of Science,375</i>) [NOTES: (T.H. Potts)]

### flycatcher, pied
 
 <i>n.</i>

### flyer
 
 <i>n.</i> a very fast-shearing sheep

>  <b>BOWEN</b> FLYER. A very fast-shearing sheep; or the best in the mob. <i>(1956,Wool Away!,156</i>) [NOTES: (glossary: Shearing Terms)]

>  <b>TURNER</b> There are names for types of sheep, the cot with matted wool; the cobbler, a difficult sheep; the flyer, a fast one; the kicker; the sandyback and the gasper, which gasps apparently because grass comes into its throat. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,147</i>)

### Flyers
 
 <i>n.</i> (usu. as the Flyers) a netball franchise competing in the National Bank Cup, with a catchment area that includes Taranaki, Wanganui, Manawatu, Horowhenua, Hawke's Bay, and Poverty Bay.

>  ...the Shakers can still make it given their remaining matches against the lower North Island Flyers, the Auckland Diamonds and the Otago Rebels. <i>(May 10, 2004,Dominion Post,D2</i>)

### flying conductor
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [also flier](fin-fly.md#flier))

>  It is a remarkable fact that the [Tramway] Board has been taking on new men so liberally of late as `students' and `flying' conductors, that the service is actually over-staffed. <i>(May 13, 1911,NZ Truth,307/4/5</i>)

### flying fish
 
 <i>n.</i> maroro

>  <b>POLACK</b> The mango or <i>shark</i> (Squalus); <i>pilot-fish</i> (Scomber ductor); <i>flying-fish</i> (Exocoetus volitans); <i>hammer-headed shark</i> (Squalus zygoena); frequent the coast, expecially the River Thames, in vast shoals, and are preserved by the natives as winter food. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/323</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> Among the piscivorous tribes, are those well known in Europe under the appellations of snapper, tench, haddocks, pollock, salmon, gurnets, john dory, hakes, bream, sword-fish, elephant-fish, diodons, pipe-fish, cod, parrot-fish, flounders, skate, cat and dog-fish, sting-rays, eels. - Among the larger tribes that sport in shoals in the vicinity of the shore, are, sperm-whales, called <i>para</i>[as-acute] <i>paraua</i>[a3-acute] (physeter macrocephalus); black whale, <i>mungu nui</i> (black physeter); right whale, <i>tohora</i> (balaena mysticetus); finback, (balaena physalis); sun-fish (diodon mola); large-lipped whale (balaena mysticetus); large-lipped whale (balaena musculus); hump-back (balaena gibbosa); shark, <i>mango</i> (squalus); hammer-head (zygaena); pilots, (scomber); flying-fish (exocaetus volitans); and an infinity of others, besides a great variety of testaceous and crustaceous fishes that almost cover the rocks under water, such as clams, muscles, oysters, crays, cuttles, limpets, wilks, gigantic muscles, a foot in length, cockles, sea-ears, sea-eggs, star-fish, mutton-fish, &c.; from the latter the pearl is procured which they insert in the eyes of their carved images and boxes. <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/202fn</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> Of the Fam. <i>Esocidae</i> may be noticed the <i>Maroro</i>, or flying fish (<i>exocetus exulans</i>). This singular fish in New Zealand attains the length of eighteen inches. <i>(1855,Te Ika a Maui,624</i>)

>  <b>SHERRIN</b> <i>Exocaetus speculiga</i> C. and V. Flying-fish, or Maroro <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,305</i>)

>  <i>7. Portland Island</i>. - Tarakihi, moki, frost-fish, barracouta, king-fish, john-dory, warehou, maomao, parrot-fish, flying-fish, smooth-hound, soldier fish. <i>(1892,TrNZI,24/203</i>) [NOTES: (Thomson)]

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Maroro</i> n. <i>exocaetus speculiger; flying fish. </i> <i>(1892,Dictionary of the Maori Language,89</i>)

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> <i>Evolantia microptera</i> (Cuv. & Val.). FLYING-FISH; MARORO. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,21,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Evolantia (Exocoetus) microptera</i>: Flying-fish. <i>(1936,Handbook for New Zealand, The,71</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> Flying Fish <i>Oxyporhamphus micropterus</i> (Cuv. and Val.) ... Flying Fish <i>Cypsilurus spilonotopterus</i> (Bleeker) ... Flying Fish <i>Cypsilurus subpellucens</i> (Richardson) ... Flying Fish <i>Cypsilurus melanocerus</i> (Ogilby) ... Flying Fish<i> Cypsilurus ilma</i> (Clarke) <i>(1955,Fishes NZ,403</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 403]

### flying flock
 
 <i>n.</i> opposite of running stock (aka flying stock) [1844 -->esp. 1891 Wallace Rural Econ 255 and 259]

>  <b>PERRY</b> Many farmers on the plains do not keep a permanent flock but what is termed a `flying flock'. <i>(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,10</i>)

>  <b>MOORE</b> These [old ewes] are eagerly sought by the fat lamb farmer, who renews his `flying flock' every year or two. <i>(1969,From Forest to Farm,85</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) sheep that are bought, conditioned and sold within a short time. (See [flying stock](fin-fly.md#flying-stock))

>  <b>PERRY</b> Many farmers on the plains do not keep a permanent flock but what is termed a 'flying flock.' <i>(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,10</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> flying flock: one which is maintained up to strength by buying-in, rather than by breeding its own replacements. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,32</i>)

>  <b>STEVENS</b> The farm is all grassland, the sheep a 'flying flock' of bought-in five-year-old ewes and bought-in Southdown rams. <i>(1958,Sheep.  Part 1, Sheep husbandry,24</i>)

>  A deliberate policy along these lines is exemplified by the 'flying flock' - aged ewes, bought in, lambed early and then the ewes are sold when the lambs are weaned or even sold all-counted. <i>(1970,NZ Journal of Agriculture,69</i>)

>  Stocking is flexible at The Pyramid [station] with priority given to the sheep, and cattle treated as a 'flying flock', sold or grazed when feed is short. [bed-and-breakfast stock] <i>(Jun 8, 1994,NZ Farmer,39</i>)

### flying fox
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) [sp. use] a cable that is strung above a fenceline on which to transport fencing materials.

>  <b>MAF</b> Flying Fox: wire between two dead men used to carry fencing materials when laying out a line in steep hill country. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,28</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a wire between two dead men for transporting people, supplies etc. -->an arrangement whereby materials (or people) are carried by an overhead wire across rivers, gullies etc, often using gravity as the means of movement - [ca1950 (bush workers') A wire to which logs are fastened and slid down to their destination; ca1954, Kaingaroa State Forest, a more mechanised form of this whereby logs can be dragged along wire, or by wire, to the roadside.]

>  [Speaking in Parliament about the Roxburgh Hydro Works] ... just when the flying-fox is in position to start pouring concrete. <i>(Jul 4, 1952,NZ Parliamentary Debates</i>) [NOTES: Mr Connelly MP (Dunedin)]

>  Miner's cage, chair, or cradle. Any of these uses may be applied to the box on an endless rope used for crossing rivers. Trampers on the whole seem to prefer cage. The Australians use flying fox. <i>(Sep 1958,Tararua,31</i>) [NOTES: Ruth Mason `Track and Trail']

>  flying fox n 2 Austral : a carrier (as for mining material or produce) operating on cables over a gorge or other obstacle <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,880</i>)

>  ~ n. very large fruit-eating bat; wire conveyor for transporting articles across a river or gully, or up and down hillsides. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. (Aust.-NZ Suppl.),616</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> Apart from personal equipment there are installations belonging to the club, especially huts, sometimes a cage, a box on an endless rope for crossing a river, called in Australia a flying fox (from the name of a native bat). <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,161</i>)

>  flying-fox n. very large fruit-eating bat; wire conveyor for transporting articles across river or gully, or up and down hillsides. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),8</i>)

>  Flying Fox: Wire between two dead men used to carry fencing materials when laying out a line in steep hill country. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,28</i>)

>  flying fox NZ & Austral. carrying device operated by cables across a gorge etc. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,290</i>) [NOTES: [cf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict. 430; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 190]]

>  <b>RENNIE</b> [They] scrambled up the muddy bank to the high end of the flying fox ... <i>(1988,Super Man,118</i>)

>  The Flying Fox, a cage which transports men and materials across the river, is directed by telephone. <i>(Aug 2, 1949,Southern Cross (Wgton),9</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a cable that bridges a ravine or river, often supporting a cage or platform for the transport of materials.

>  The Flying Fox, a cage which transports men and materials across the river, is directed by telephone. <i>(Aug 2, 1949,Southern Cross (Wgton),9</i>)

>  Adam had rigged up two flying-floxes - two long wires across the valleys on which he sent the hay flying across to the ferny slopes on the other side where they fed the cattle. 2. 1982 MAF Agric. Glossary 28 Flying Fox: wire between two dead men used to carry fencing materials when laying out a line in steep hill country. <i>(1969,Landfall,XXIII 59</i>)

### flying gang
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a team of itinerant high country musterers, usually employed from October to June.

>  <b>MCRAE</b> The days of big musters and the employment of flying gangs of men has given way to permanent staff who care for the everyday whims of the stock. <i>(1998,On the Run,33</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> Just before the St Helen's straggle muster a flying-gang of eight picked men was selected to muster St James. <i>(1962,Life's Roundabout, A,26</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> During my time in the Flying Gang, Woodbank was held Mr W Hanmer Atkinson, one of the best-known of the North Canterbury run-holders. <i>(1952,High Country Journey,180</i>)

>  Of the 20-odd seasons I had 'on the hill' I think the '33 autumn was the worst I struck. I was in the flying gang in the Hanmer Springs district that year - the gang does Woodbank, the Hossack and St James. <i>(Feb 27, 1963,Weekly News,37</i>)

>  <b>COATES</b> They had come from the north and told of the flying gang at Saint Helen's, Molesworth, the Clarence Reserve and of the paddock shepherds known as shit-kickers. <i>(1995,Dining With the Cats,74</i>)

>  <b>STACEY</b> The flying gang boys who used to muster the Hossack, Woodbank and St James, had come into Hanmer for a dance. <i>(1996,Tales of a Tussock Jumper,123</i>)

### flying gurnard
 
 <i>n.</i> [? An obs or N. Hemisphere name - check;]

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Gurnet ... [Gurnard </i>cross-referenced to <i>Gurnet</i>] The species of <i>Trigla</i> found in British waters, called <i>Gurnards</i> are of the family of <i>Cottidae</i>. The word <i>Gurnet</i> is an obsolete or provincial form of <i>Gurnard</i>, revived in Australia, and applied to the fish <i>Centropogon scorpaenoides</i>, Guich., family Scorpaenidae. The original word <i>Gurnard</i> is retained in New Zealand, and applied to the new species <i>Trigla kumu</i> (kumu being the Maori name), family Cottidae. The <i>Flying Gurnet</i> is <i>Trigla polyommata</i>, Richards., found on all the Australian coasts from New South Wales to Western Australia, family <i>Cottidae</i>. It is a distinct species, not included in the British species. They have large pectoral fins, but are not known to possess the power of supporting themselves in the air like the `flying fish' which belong to other genera. Sir Fredk. McCoy says that <i>Sebastes Percoides</i>, Richards., is called Gurnet, or Garnet-perch, by the fishermen and dealers, as well as the more common <i>Neosebastes scorpaenoides</i>, Guich., and <i>Scorpaena panda</i>, Richards.] <i>Ibid.</i> 384 <i>Red Gurnet-Perch, n.</i> name given in Victoria to the fish <i>Sebastes percoides</i>, Richards., family <i>Scorpeaenidae</i>. It is also called <i>Poddy; Red Gurnard</i>, or <i>Gurnet</i>; and in New Zealand, <i>Pohuikaroa</i>. See <i>Perch</i> and <i>Gurnet</i>.] <i>(1898,Austral English</i>)

>  <b>PARKINSON</b> All the coves of this bay teem with fish of various kinds, such as cuttle-fish, large breams, ... small grey breams, small and large barracootas, flying gurnards, horse-mackarel, dog-fish, soles, dabs, mullets, drums, scorpenas or rock-fish, cole-fish, the beautiful fish called chimera, and shags. [In the Stout Collection copy, Victoria University Library, an early 19th C. or late 18th C. hand has added: `*Baracootas, a fish remarkably smooth about 7 or 8 foot long [dagger] Flying Gurnards, a flying fish, of a remarkable fine Gold Colour. [double dagger] Chimeras, a fish of Silver Colour - [triple dagger] Drum fish, so call'd from the noise they make, - ' - <i>(Jan 15, 1770,Journal,114</i>)

### Flying Nun
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Someone in [New York magazine] the <i>Village Voice</i> once remarked that what made Flying Nun bands special was that they chopped up pop music and then reconnected all the basic components in ways that nobody had ever thought of before. <i>(Mar 17, 2002,Sunday Star Times,F3</i>)

>  She also features on a number of compilations, including the 1993 landmark Shrew'd - a Flying Nun compilation of New Zealand women musicians. <i>(Jun 26, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),37</i>)

>  Flying Nun had its origins as a tiny Christchurch independent record label that introduced the country to the "Dunedin sound" of the Clean, the Verlaines, the Chills and many others, but in 2002 we're left with an institution in name only. <i>(Mar 30, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),50</i>)

>  It features a totally Flying Nun soundtrack, including the legendary Clean who were flown back specially. It couldn't be a film about Dunedin scarfies without the bands that defined the Dunedin sound, says Sarkies. <i>(Dec 5, 1998,Listener (New Zealand Listener),27</i>)

>  Yes, Flying Nun was crippingly orthodox stylistically... Flying Nun cannot shoulder all the blame - because blind adherence to orthodoxy is a defining feature of so much pakeha cultural production in this country. <i>(May 4, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),8</i>)

>  I've dined out on stories of his days at the Flying Nun office in Christcburch when I was distributing their records around the Dunedin record shops. <i>(Mar 17, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),65</i>)

>  I think it is fair to say that Flying Nun does not resemble in any way shape or form what the original idea behind it was. It is now just another record company among many. <i>(Jul 30, 2000,Sunday Star Times,C2</i>)

>  Robert Sarkies's first feature, <i>Scarfies</i>, buys right into the mythical Dunedin, the Flying Nun theme park. <i>(Aug 7, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),39</i>)

>  Flying Nun might have been, as Bannister says, "cripplingly orthodox stylistically", but those who met at 14 Esmonde Rd to talk about life and literature with Frank Sargeson were most certainly not. <i>(Jun 1, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),11</i>)

### flying saucer
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> the (British-style) Army regulation beret (NZ troops: Korea: see cit. lemon squeezer, 1953)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a largactil pill

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> Flying Saucer. (n) Lactargil pill. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,248</i>)

### flying squad
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The 'flying-squad" model that carried out those raids could be brought back in some form under a future New Zealand First (coalition) government ... <i>(Aug 2005,Tearaway,18</i>)

>  "In 2005 we struggle to know what a New Zealander really is," says Mr Peters, who recently proposed 'flying squads' to track down overstayers. <i>(Jul 2005,Tearaway,12</i>)

### flying squadron
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a group of riders who visited stations en masse. [1860s-1920s.]

>  <b>EVANS</b> The Flying Squadron. This was the name given to groups of young people who used to ride from station to station, purely for visiting and for holidays. <i>(1975,Silver Tussock, The,11</i>)

### flying stock
 
 <i>n.</i> [(farming)]

>  <b>WALLACE</b> The sheep kept [at Edendale estate, Southland] are largely a `flying' or bought-in stock for fattening and marketing. <i>(1891,Rural Economy of Aust. & NZ,259</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) stock bought in to be conditioned and sold. (See [flying flock](fin-fly.md#flying-flock))

>  <b>MCDONALD</b> When the intensive sheep farm is small and the soil good a system of 'flying stock' farming may only be pursued. <i>(1915,NZ Sheepfarming,66</i>)

>  <b>WALLACE</b> The sheep kept are largely a 'flying' or bought-in stock for fattening and marketing. <i>(1891,Rural Economy of Aust. & NZ,259</i>)

### flying survey
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the erection of a trig station.

>  <b>WESTMACOTT</b> The erection of a trig station was called a flying survey and the stations were many miles apart. <i>(1977,After-Breakfast Cigar, The,18</i>)

>  <b>WESTMACOTT</b> He had actually been on the flying survey in 1879 <i>(1977,After-Breakfast Cigar, The,28</i>)
